<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{u:thm:lower}
Under \eqref{u:eq:basic} and, in the complex case, \eqref{u:eq:properness},
\[
 \liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac1n\log\mathbb P\{\rho(X_n)>r\}\ge-I_\beta(r),
 \qquad r>1.
\]
In the real case the same lower bound holds for the event that $X_n$
has a positive real eigenvalue larger than $r$.
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{u:thm:matching}
Suppose that one of the following sets of assumptions holds:
\begin{enumerate}
\item $\mathbb F=\mathbb R$, $\xi\overset{d}=-\xi$, $\mathbb E\xi^2=1$, and
      $\mathbb E\xi^{2m}\le(2m-1)!!$ for every integer $m\ge1$;
\item $\mathbb F=\mathbb C$, $\mathbb E\xi=0$, $\mathbb E|\xi|^2=1$, condition \eqref{u:eq:properness}, and
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:sharp}
 \mathbb E e^{2\operatorname{Re}(\bar u\xi)}\le e^{|u|^2},\qquad u\in\mathbb C.
\end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
Then the strict and non-strict right tails both satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:matching}
 \lim_{n\to\infty}\frac1n\log\mathbb P\{\rho(X_n)>r\}
 =\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac1n\log\mathbb P\{\rho(X_n)\ge r\}
 =-I_\beta(r),\qquad r>1.
\end{equation}

Neither case requires a density. In the complex case the real and
imaginary components may be dependent.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{l:thm:MT}
Assume \eqref{u:eq:basic} and, when $\mathbb F=\mathbb C$, \eqref{u:eq:properness}.
Suppose in addition that $\mu$ has a bounded density with respect to
Lebesgue measure on $\mathbb F$ (planar Lebesgue measure when $\mathbb F=\mathbb C$).
For every fixed $0\le r<1$, there are constants $c_{\mu,r}>0$ and
$n_0(\mu,r)$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{l:eq:M.2}
 \mathbb P\{\rho(X_n)\le r\}\le e^{-c_{\mu,r}n^2},
 \qquad n\ge n_0(\mu,r).
\end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
\label{rem:lower-pseudovariance}
We expect the complex lower-tail bound in Theorem~\ref{l:thm:MT} to remain
valid without \eqref{u:eq:properness}, retaining centering, unit variance,
subgaussianity, and a bounded planar density. Zero pseudovariance makes
the real covariance a scalar multiple of the identity and substantially
simplifies the Gaussian comparison. Removing it would require a more
involved treatment of general real covariance in the comparison and
Gaussian estimates. We do not prove this extension here.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
\label{rem:bernoulli}
Symmetric Bernoulli entries satisfy the upper-tail hypotheses, but our
lower-tail argument encounters two obstructions in this case.
First, the comparison for arbitrary observation events in
Theorem~\ref{l:thm:AC} cannot hold for Bernoulli laws: a nonzero deterministic
linear observation has finite support under Bernoulli entries and a
continuous law under Gaussian entries. Taking the event to be that
finite support gives probabilities one and zero, respectively. This
obstruction persists for a flat observation such as
$h^{-1/2}\sum_{j=1}^h\xi_j$.
Second, the border estimate in Proposition~\ref{l:thm:MB} fixes the
$(n-1)\times(n-1)$ core $A$ and averages over the remaining $2n-1$
entries. For independent symmetric Bernoulli entries, every border
event $E$ therefore satisfies
\[
 \mathbb P(E\mid A)=0
 \quad\text{or}\quad
 \mathbb P(E\mid A)\ge 2^{-(2n-1)}.
\]
Consequently, a conditional bound of order $e^{-cn^2}$ would require
that no Bernoulli border completion satisfy the event, rather than
merely that its continuous volume be small. An extension thus needs
additional control of the arithmetic feasibility of the constraints
as the core varies.

We also explored a multipoint arithmetic approach, motivated by the
two-point quantitative invertibility estimates of
\cite[Theorems 1.5 and 1.9]{HanTwoPoint}. For real Bernoulli matrices,
this approach would require upper bounds of the form $e^{-c_m n}$ for
simultaneous small-singular-value events at $m$ nonreal shifts, with
$c_m\to\infty$ as $m\to\infty$. We did not obtain such estimates.
An additive error $e^{-cn}$ with $c$ independent of $m$ would prevent
this improvement. These limitations of the available arguments do
not constitute counterexamples to a Bernoulli lower-tail bound;
no such bound is proved here.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{thm:full-ldp}
Suppose that $\mu$ satisfies either set of hypotheses in
Theorem~\ref{u:thm:matching} and has a bounded density with respect to
Lebesgue measure on $\mathbb F$. Then $(\rho(X_n))_{n\ge1}$ satisfies a large
deviation principle on $[0,\infty)$ with speed $n$ and good rate function
\begin{equation}\label{eq:full-ldp-rate}
 J_\beta(r)=
 \begin{cases}
  +\infty, & 0\le r<1,\\[2pt]
  \dfrac\beta2\bigl(r^2-1-2\log r\bigr), & r\ge1.
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
Precisely, for every Borel set $A\subseteq[0,\infty)$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:full-ldp-borel}
\begin{split}
 -\inf_{r\in A^\circ}J_\beta(r)
 &\le \liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac1n\log\mathbb P\{\rho(X_n)\in A\}\\
 &\le \limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac1n\log\mathbb P\{\rho(X_n)\in A\}
 \le -\inf_{r\in\overline A}J_\beta(r).
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Here interior and closure are relative to $[0,\infty)$, and
$\inf\varnothing=+\infty$.
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
Write $R_n=\rho(X_n)$. The hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{u:thm:matching}
imply the common subgaussian assumptions, so Theorem~\ref{l:thm:MT}
also applies. For each fixed $a\in[0,1)$, it gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ldp-superexponential}
 \limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac1n\log\mathbb P\{R_n\le a\}=-\infty.
\end{equation}
Together with the upper-tail estimate \eqref{u:eq:matching}, this implies
$R_n\to1$ in probability. Also, $I_\beta$ is continuous and strictly
increasing on $(1,\infty)$, tends to zero at $1$, and tends to infinity
at infinity. Consequently $J_\beta$ is lower semicontinuous and has
compact sublevel sets.

For the open-set lower bound, let $O\subseteq[0,\infty)$ be open.
If $1\in O$, convergence in probability gives $\mathbb P\{R_n\in O\}\to1$.
For any $r\in O\cap(1,\infty)$, choose $1<a<r<b$ with $(a,b)\subseteq O$.
By \eqref{u:eq:matching},
\[
 \mathbb P\{a<R_n<b\}
 =\mathbb P\{R_n>a\}-\mathbb P\{R_n\ge b\}
 =\exp\{-nI_\beta(a)+o(n)\},
\]
since $I_\beta(b)>I_\beta(a)$ makes the second term exponentially
smaller than the first. Letting $a\uparrow r$ yields the lower bound
$-J_\beta(r)$; taking the supremum over such $r$, and including $r=1$
when present, gives $-\inf_O J_\beta$. If $O\cap[1,\infty)=\varnothing$,
the required lower bound is $-\infty$ and is automatic.

For the closed-set upper bound, let $F\subseteq[0,\infty)$ be closed.
The bound is automatic if $1\in F$ or $F=\varnothing$. Otherwise,
closedness separates $F$ from $1$, so choose $a\in[0,1)$ with
$F\cap[0,1)\subseteq[0,a]$. If $F\cap(1,\infty)\ne\varnothing$,
its minimum $b$ exists and is strictly larger than $1$.
Then $F\subseteq[0,a]\cup[b,\infty)$, and the union bound gives
\[
 \mathbb P\{R_n\in F\}
 \le\mathbb P\{R_n\le a\}+\mathbb P\{R_n\ge b\}.
\]
Equations~\eqref{eq:ldp-superexponential} and~\eqref{u:eq:matching}
give the upper bound $-I_\beta(b)=-\inf_F J_\beta$.
If $F\cap(1,\infty)=\varnothing$, omit the second term and obtain
$-\infty=-\inf_F J_\beta$ from \eqref{eq:ldp-superexponential}.
This treats unbounded closed sets as well. Applying these two bounds
to $A^\circ\subseteq A\subseteq\overline A$ proves
\eqref{eq:full-ldp-borel}.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{u:thm:complex-local}
Suppose $\mathbb F=\mathbb C$ and \eqref{u:eq:basic}--\eqref{u:eq:properness} hold.
For $z\in\mathbb C$ and $\varepsilon>0$, write
$B(z,\varepsilon)=\{w\in\mathbb C:|w-z|<\varepsilon\}$ and let
\[
 \mathcal E_n(z,\varepsilon)
 =\{\operatorname{spec}(X_n)\cap B(z,\varepsilon)\ne\varnothing\}.
\]
For every $|z|>1$ and $0<\varepsilon<|z|-1$,
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:complex-local-lower}
 \liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac1n\log\mathbb P(\mathcal E_n(z,\varepsilon))
 \ge-I_2(|z|-\varepsilon).
\end{equation}
If, in addition, \eqref{u:eq:sharp} holds, then
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:complex-local}
 \lim_{n\to\infty}\frac1n\log\mathbb P(\mathcal E_n(z,\varepsilon))
 =-I_2(|z|-\varepsilon).
\end{equation}
In particular, under the sharp bound,
\[
 \lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}\lim_{n\to\infty}
 \frac1n\log\mathbb P(\mathcal E_n(z,\varepsilon))=-I_2(|z|).
\]
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
\label{u:rem:ordinary-subgaussian}
The sharp assumptions in Theorem~\ref{u:thm:matching} cannot in general
be replaced by ordinary subgaussianity. A single large diagonal entry
can create an outlier at a cost smaller than the Gaussian rate $I_\beta$.
The following example makes this explicit.

Let $g$ be a standard real or proper complex Gaussian with $\mathbb E|g|^2=1$,
and set $\xi=\sqrt V\,g$, where $V$ is independent of $g$, with
$\mathbb P\{V=4/7\}=7/8$ and $\mathbb P\{V=4\}=1/8$. Since $\mathbb E V=1$, this entry law is centered and has variance one. It has
a bounded smooth density, is subgaussian, and has zero pseudovariance
in the complex case. The variance-$4$ component makes large entries
less costly than under the standard Gaussian law: for every fixed $b>2$,
\[
 \mathbb P\{|\xi_{11}/\sqrt n-b|\le n^{-1/4}\}
 \ge\exp\bigl[-\beta b^2n/8+o(n)\bigr].
\]
On this event, the normalized matrix has a diagonal entry close to $b$.
Replace this entry by an independent copy to obtain an iid matrix $Y_n$.
Then $X_n=Y_n+b e_1e_1^*+\mathcal E_n$, where $e_1$ is the first coordinate
vector and $\|\mathcal E_n\|_{\mathrm{op}}\to0$ in conditional probability.
Lemma~\ref{u:lem:outlier} gives an eigenvalue near $b$, again with
conditional probability tending to one. Since $b>2$, this gives a lower
bound for $\mathbb P\{\rho(X_n)\ge2\}$. Letting $b\downarrow2$ yields
\[
 \liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac1n\log\mathbb P\{\rho(X_n)\ge2\}
 \ge-\frac\beta2
 >-\beta\left(\frac32-\log2\right)=-I_\beta(2).
\]
Thus the upper tail can be strictly larger, on the exponential scale,
than the Gaussian prediction. The universal lower bound in
Theorem~\ref{u:thm:lower} still applies, but need not be sharp.
For Wigner matrices, Cook--Ducatez--Guionnet \cite{CookDucatezGuionnet}
established full speed-$n$ LDPs for the largest eigenvalue beyond sharp
subgaussianity, when the log-Laplace transform of the entry law has bounded
second derivative. We expect an analogous full speed-$n$ LDP for the
spectral radius in the iid non-Hermitian setting beyond the sharp class.
We do not yet have a conjectural formula for the law-dependent rate in
this generality.
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{u:lem:concentration}
Let $Z_1,\ldots,Z_{n^2}$ be iid vectors in a fixed Euclidean space with
$\mathbb E e^{c\lVertZ_1\rVert^2}<\infty$. For fixed $L,\delta>0$, every convex
$L/n$-Lipschitz function $f_n$ satisfies
\[
 \mathbb P\{|f_n(Z)-\mathbb E f_n(Z)|>\delta\}\le C e^{-c_\delta n^2}
\]
for all sufficiently large $n$, uniformly over such functions.
Here $C>0$ is universal. With $L$ and the block law fixed,
$c_\delta>0$ depends only on $\delta$, independently of $n$ and $f_n$;
dependence on these fixed data is suppressed in the notation.
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
Set $Z_j^{(R)}=Z_j\mathbf1_{\lVertZ_j\rVert\le R}$. For fixed $R$,
Talagrand's inequality \cite[Theorem 4.1.1 and Lemma 4.1.2]{Talagrand},
with whole blocks as product coordinates, gives the stated bound for
$f_n(Z^{(R)})$ at any fixed deviation after the standard mean--median
comparison. No independence within a block is needed. Moreover,
\[
 |f_n(Z)-f_n(Z^{(R)})|\le
 \Delta_n:=\frac L n\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n^2}\lVertZ_j\rVert^2
                  \mathbf1_{\lVertZ_j\rVert>R}\right)^{1/2}.
\]
For $0<\theta<c$, dominated convergence gives
$\log\mathbb E e^{\theta\lVertZ_1\rVert^2\mathbf1_{\lVertZ_1\rVert>R}}\to0$
as $R\to\infty$. Thus a fixed large $R$ ensures
$\mathbb E\Delta_n\le\delta/4$ and, by Chernoff's bound,
$\mathbb P\{\Delta_n>\delta/4\}\le e^{-c_\delta n^2}$, uniformly in $n$.
Apply the bounded-block result at deviation $\delta/2$ and use the
triangle inequality, including the centering error
$|\mathbb E f_n(Z)-\mathbb E f_n(Z^{(R)})|\le\mathbb E\Delta_n$.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{u:lem:bulk}
Under \eqref{u:eq:basic}, for each $b\in\mathbb C$ with $|b|>1$ and fixed $\tau,\delta>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:bulk-lower}
 \mathbb P\{F_{\tau,n}(b)<2\log|b|-\delta\}\le Ce^{-cn^2}.
\end{equation}
For each compact annulus $K\subset\{|z|>1\}$, a constant $M_K$
depending only on $\mu,K$ satisfies, for fixed $\tau,\delta>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:bulk-upper}
 \mathbb P\left\{\sup_{z\in K}[F_{\tau,n}(z)-2\log|z|]
                   >M_K^2\tau^2+\delta\right\}\le Ce^{-cn^2}.
\end{equation}
Here $c,C>0$ are independent of $n$, but may depend on
$\mu,b,\tau,\delta$ in \eqref{u:eq:bulk-lower} and on
$\mu,K,\tau,\delta$ in \eqref{u:eq:bulk-upper}; they need not be the same
in the two bounds.
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
Write $\log(s^2+\tau^2)=2\log\tau+h_1(s)-h_2(s)$, $s\ge0$, with
$h_1(0)=h_2(0)=0$ and
\[
 h_1'(s)=\begin{cases}2s/(s^2+\tau^2),&s\le\tau,\\1/\tau,&s>\tau,
 \end{cases}\qquad h_2'(s)=h_1'(s)-2s/(s^2+\tau^2).
\]
Both functions are convex, nondecreasing and $1/\tau$-Lipschitz.
Write $s_1(A)\ge\cdots\ge s_n(A)\ge0$ for the singular values of $A$.
To see convexity of the spectral sums, use
\[
 \sum_j(s_j(A)-a)_+
 =\max_{0\le k\le n}\left(\sum_{j=1}^k s_j(A)-ka\right),\qquad
 \sum_{j=1}^k s_j(A)=\max_{U^*U=V^*V=I_k}\operatorname{Re}\operatorname{Tr}(U^*AV).
\]
Here $(x)_+=\max(x,0)$ and $U,V$ have $k$ orthonormal columns.
Each $h_\ell$ is a nonnegative integral of the functions $(s-a)_+$,
since $h_\ell'(0)=0$ and $h_\ell''\ge0$.
The singular-value inequality
$\sum_j|s_j(A)-s_j(B)|^2\le\lVertA-B\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2$ also shows that
$n^{-1}\sum_jh_\ell(s_j(X_n-zI))$ is $1/(n\tau)$-Lipschitz in the
original entries.
Lemma~\ref{u:lem:concentration} therefore gives concentration of
$F_{\tau,n}(z)$ at fixed deviations with failure $Ce^{-cn^2}$.

By \eqref{u:eq:exterior-logdet}, the deterministic floor
$F_{\tau,n}\ge2\log\tau$ and the inequality
\[
 F_{\tau,n}(b)\ge2n^{-1}\log|\det(bI-X_n)|
\]
yield
$\liminf_n\mathbb E F_{\tau,n}(b)\ge2\log|b|$.
On the resolvent event \eqref{u:eq:resolvent},
\[
 F_{\tau,n}(z)\le2n^{-1}\log|\det(zI-X_n)|+M_K^2\tau^2.
\]
Passing to expectations is justified by the common lower floor and the
uniformly square-integrable upper envelope
\[
 F_{\tau,n}(z)\le
 \log\left(2n^{-2}\sum_{i,j=1}^n|\xi_{ij}|^2+2\sup_K|z|^2+\tau^2\right).
\]
This proves the required upper and lower mean estimates. Concentration
gives \eqref{u:eq:bulk-lower}. Finally
$|F_{\tau,n}(z)-F_{\tau,n}(w)|\le|z-w|/\tau$; a fixed finite net
of $K$ gives \eqref{u:eq:bulk-upper}.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{u:lem:soft-replacement}
Assume \eqref{u:eq:basic} and, in the complex case, \eqref{u:eq:properness}.
Let $g_j$ be independent standard real or proper complex Gaussians
with $\mathbb E|g_j|^2=1$,
and let $\xi_j$ be independent copies of the original entry. Take a finite,
possibly empty coefficient family $(v_j)\subset\mathbb F$ with $q:=\sum_j|v_j|^2\le1$,
and set $\epsilon_3:=\sum_j|v_j|^3$. For $a>0$, define
\[
 \mathcal Z_\mu(s)=\mathbb E e^{-|s-\sum_jv_j\xi_j|^2/a},\qquad
 \mathcal Z_G(s)=\mathbb E e^{-|s-\sum_jv_jg_j|^2/a},\qquad d_{\mathrm G}=a+2q/\beta.
\]
All derivatives are with respect to $s\in\mathbb F\simeq\mathbb R^\beta$.
In particular, $\nabla$ is the \emph{real gradient}, identified with an
element of $\mathbb F$; when $s=x+iy$, $\nabla f=\partial_xf+i\partial_yf$.
With $m_3=\mathbb E|\xi|^3+\mathbb E|g_1|^3$, one has
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:smooth-replacement}
 \sup_{s\in\mathbb F}\bigl(|\mathcal Z_\mu(s)-\mathcal Z_G(s)|
       +|\nabla\mathcal Z_\mu(s)-\nabla\mathcal Z_G(s)|\bigr)
 \le C_a m_3\epsilon_3.
\end{equation}
Moreover, including $q=0$,
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:gaussian-soft-formula}
 \mathcal Z_G(s)=\left(\frac{a}{d_{\mathrm G}}\right)^{\beta/2}e^{-|s|^2/d_{\mathrm G}},
 \qquad \nabla\log\mathcal Z_G(s)=-2s/d_{\mathrm G}.
\end{equation}
For each fixed $K<\infty$,
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:compact-score}
 \sup_{|s|\le K}\left|\nabla\log\mathcal Z_\mu(s)+\frac{2s}{d_{\mathrm G}}\right|
 \le C_{a,K}m_3\epsilon_3.
\end{equation}
The constants are independent of the number and choice of coefficients.
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{u:lem:coupling}
Let $v\in\mathbb F^n$ be a deterministic unit vector satisfying
\[
 \max_{1\le j\le n}|v_j|\le\frac{L}{\sqrt n}.
\]
Let $\bm t=(t_1,\ldots,t_n)^{\mathsf T}\in\mathbb F^n$ be deterministic with
$|t_i|\le T_0$, and let
$\bm x_i=(x_{ij})_{j=1}^n\sim\mu_{v,t_i}$ be independent rows as in \eqref{u:eq:row-tilt}.
There is a coupling to an iid array
$(\zeta_{ij})$ of law $\mu$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:coupling}
 n^{-1/2}(x_{ij})=Y_nT+\frac{n^{-1/2}\bm t v^*}{1+\eta^2}+E_n,
 \quad Y_n=n^{-1/2}(\zeta_{ij}),\quad\lVertE_n\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{\mathbb P}0
\end{equation}
uniformly over the specified deterministic parameters.
The error $E_n$ may depend on $Y_n$ and need not have independent entries.
Here, with $d_j=\eta^2+\sum_{k=j}^n|v_k|^2$ for $1\le j\le n+1$
(so $d_{n+1}=\eta^2$),
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:T}
 T_{jj}=1,\quad T_{kj}=-v_k\bar v_j/d_{k+1}\ (k<j),\quad
 T_{kj}=0\ (k>j),\qquad \lVertT-I\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\le(2\eta^4)^{-1}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- head-1: body_md -->
Reveal each tilted row sequentially and couple its conditional
entries to independent original entries. The conditional means give an
upper triangular regression system, whose inverse is $T$. The centered
coupling errors form a matrix martingale; truncation and matrix Freedman
control their operator norm, while the regression remainder is controlled
in Hilbert--Schmidt mean square.
The complete proof is in Subsection~\ref{u:sec:coupling-details}.


The finite-rank resolvent mechanism is classical in outlier theory
\cite{Tao,OR2014,BC2016}; see also \cite{HanBand} for band and elliptic
models. The coupling controls $T-I$ by a uniform Hilbert--Schmidt
bound. The following version treats such right deformations together
with the possibly correlated small error in \eqref{u:eq:coupling}.

\begin{lemma}[Right deformations and actual outliers]\label{u:lem:outlier}
Let $Y_n$ have the same law as $X_n$.
If $D_n$ is deterministic with $\lVertD_n\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}\le C$, then
$Y_n(I+D_n)$ has the exterior resolvent bounds and bilinear limits
\eqref{u:eq:resolvent}, with constants also depending on $C$.
For every random matrix sequence $\mathcal E_n^{\rm pert}$ satisfying
$\lVert\mathcal E_n^{\rm pert}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{\mathbb P}0$, they also
hold for $Y_n(I+D_n)+\mathcal E_n^{\rm pert}$.
For each fixed $s\in\mathbb F$ with $|s|>1$, the matrix
\[
 Y_n(I+D_n)+s v_nv_n^*+\mathcal E_n^{\rm pert}
\]
has an eigenvalue converging in probability to $s$,
for deterministic unit vectors $v_n$.
No independence from $Y_n$, or among the entries of
$\mathcal E_n^{\rm pert}$, is required.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Fix a compact set $K\subset\{|z|>1\}$. We use $m$ for a
\emph{truncation rank}, not for a matrix-power exponent; $n$ remains
the matrix dimension. For each fixed $m\ge1$ and $n>m$, truncate the
singular-value decomposition of $D_n$:
\[
 D_n=D_n^{(m)}+E_n^{(m)},\qquad
 D_n^{(m)}=\mathsf U_{n,m}\mathsf V_{n,m},
\]
where $\mathsf U_{n,m}\in\mathbb F^{n\times m}$, $\mathsf V_{n,m}\in\mathbb F^{m\times n}$,
$\lVert\mathsf U_{n,m}\rVert\le C$, $\lVert\mathsf V_{n,m}\rVert\le1$, and
$\lVertE_n^{(m)}\rVert\le C/\sqrt{m+1}$.
The last bound follows from
$(m+1)s_{m+1}(D_n)^2\le\lVertD_n\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\le C^2$.
Thus $D_n^{(m)}$ has fixed rank but need not have small norm, whereas
the remainder $E_n^{(m)}$ has small operator norm for large $m$.
Define the baseline and truncated resolvents by
\[
 R(z)=(zI-Y_n)^{-1},\qquad
 R^{(m)}(z)=(zI-Y_n(I+D_n^{(m)}))^{-1}.
\]
Since $R(z)Y_n=zR(z)-I$, the deterministic bilinear limits in
\eqref{u:eq:resolvent} imply
\[
 \mathsf V_{n,m}R(z)Y_n\mathsf U_{n,m}
 =z\mathsf V_{n,m}R(z)\mathsf U_{n,m}-\mathsf V_{n,m}\mathsf U_{n,m},
\]
and, for each fixed $m$,
\[
 \sup_{z\in K}\lVert\mathsf V_{n,m}R(z)Y_n\mathsf U_{n,m}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}
 \xrightarrow{\mathbb P}0.
\]
Indeed, all $m^2$ scalar entries converge uniformly to zero in
probability, and $m$ is fixed. With probability tending to one,
$I_m-\mathsf V_{n,m}R(z)Y_n\mathsf U_{n,m}$ is invertible and its
inverse has norm at most $2$, uniformly on $K$. Woodbury gives
\[
 R^{(m)}(z)=R(z)+R(z)Y_n\mathsf U_{n,m}
       (I_m-\mathsf V_{n,m}R(z)Y_n\mathsf U_{n,m})^{-1}\mathsf V_{n,m}R(z).
\]
Its norm is at most $C_0=M_K+2M_K^2MC$, independent of $m$, with
probability tending to one for every fixed $m$. For deterministic
unit vectors $p_n,q_n$, use $R(z)Y_n=zR(z)-I$, bound the middle
inverse in the Woodbury correction by $2$, and use
$\lVert\mathsf V_{n,m}R(z)q_n\rVert\le
\lVert\mathsf V_{n,m}\rVert\lVertR(z)\rVert\lVertq_n\rVert\le M_K$
to obtain, on the above events,
\[
 \sup_{z\in K}|p_n^*(R^{(m)}(z)-R(z))q_n|
 \le 2M_K\sup_{z\in K}\lVertp_n^*(zR(z)-I)\mathsf U_{n,m}\rVert
 \xrightarrow{\mathbb P}0.
\]
Indeed, the row vector inside the norm has $m$ coordinates, each tending
uniformly to zero by \eqref{u:eq:resolvent}; the columns of
$\mathsf U_{n,m}$ are deterministic and bounded, and $m$ is fixed.

To restore the remainder, write $R_D(z)=(zI-Y_n(I+D_n))^{-1}$ and
$\varepsilon_m=MC/\sqrt{m+1}$. Choose $m$ with $C_0\varepsilon_m<1/2$.
Since $\lVertY_nE_n^{(m)}\rVert\le\varepsilon_m$ with probability tending
to one, the Neumann series gives, uniformly on $K$,
\[
 R_D=(I-R^{(m)}Y_nE_n^{(m)})^{-1}R^{(m)},\qquad
 \lVertR_D\rVert\le\frac{C_0}{1-C_0\varepsilon_m}\le2C_0.
\]
The resolvent identity then yields
\[
 \sup_{z\in K}\lVertR_D(z)-R^{(m)}(z)\rVert
 =\sup_{z\in K}\lVertR_D(z)Y_nE_n^{(m)}R^{(m)}(z)\rVert
 \le2C_0^2\varepsilon_m.
\]
Consequently, on these events,
\begin{align*}
 \sup_{z\in K}\left|p_n^*R_D(z)q_n-\frac{p_n^*q_n}{z}\right|
 &\le \sup_{z\in K}|p_n^*(R^{(m)}(z)-R(z))q_n|\\
 &\quad+\sup_{z\in K}\left|p_n^*R(z)q_n-\frac{p_n^*q_n}{z}\right|
       +2C_0^2\varepsilon_m.
\end{align*}
For fixed $m$, the first two terms tend to zero in probability as
$n\to\infty$; then $m\to\infty$ removes the last term. This proves
both the resolvent bound and the bilinear limits. Another Neumann
series absorbs $\mathcal E_n^{\rm pert}$ using
$\lVert\mathcal E_n^{\rm pert}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{\mathbb P}0$, with no
independence requirement.

For the outlier assertion, use the perturbation from the statement and set
$W_n=Y_n(I+D_n)+\mathcal E_n^{\rm pert}$. We seek an outlier of
$W_n+s v_nv_n^*$. On a small closed disk about
$s$ contained in $\{|z|>1\}$, $W_n$ has no resolvent poles and
$1-sv_n^*(zI-W_n)^{-1}v_n\to1-s/z$ uniformly. The determinant lemma
and Rouch\'e's theorem give exactly one eigenvalue in the disk.
For real data it is real by conjugation symmetry. For fixed $K,C,s$,
these probability bounds are uniform over the permitted deterministic
matrices and unit vectors, provided the perturbation error converges
uniformly over these data. This follows by a subsequence contradiction;
the argument does not require simultaneous control over directions
selected from the data.
\end{proof}
<!-- end head-1 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{u:lem:outlier}
Let $Y_n$ have the same law as $X_n$.
If $D_n$ is deterministic with $\lVertD_n\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}\le C$, then
$Y_n(I+D_n)$ has the exterior resolvent bounds and bilinear limits
\eqref{u:eq:resolvent}, with constants also depending on $C$.
For every random matrix sequence $\mathcal E_n^{\rm pert}$ satisfying
$\lVert\mathcal E_n^{\rm pert}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{\mathbb P}0$, they also
hold for $Y_n(I+D_n)+\mathcal E_n^{\rm pert}$.
For each fixed $s\in\mathbb F$ with $|s|>1$, the matrix
\[
 Y_n(I+D_n)+s v_nv_n^*+\mathcal E_n^{\rm pert}
\]
has an eigenvalue converging in probability to $s$,
for deterministic unit vectors $v_n$.
No independence from $Y_n$, or among the entries of
$\mathcal E_n^{\rm pert}$, is required.
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
Fix a compact set $K\subset\{|z|>1\}$. We use $m$ for a
\emph{truncation rank}, not for a matrix-power exponent; $n$ remains
the matrix dimension. For each fixed $m\ge1$ and $n>m$, truncate the
singular-value decomposition of $D_n$:
\[
 D_n=D_n^{(m)}+E_n^{(m)},\qquad
 D_n^{(m)}=\mathsf U_{n,m}\mathsf V_{n,m},
\]
where $\mathsf U_{n,m}\in\mathbb F^{n\times m}$, $\mathsf V_{n,m}\in\mathbb F^{m\times n}$,
$\lVert\mathsf U_{n,m}\rVert\le C$, $\lVert\mathsf V_{n,m}\rVert\le1$, and
$\lVertE_n^{(m)}\rVert\le C/\sqrt{m+1}$.
The last bound follows from
$(m+1)s_{m+1}(D_n)^2\le\lVertD_n\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\le C^2$.
Thus $D_n^{(m)}$ has fixed rank but need not have small norm, whereas
the remainder $E_n^{(m)}$ has small operator norm for large $m$.
Define the baseline and truncated resolvents by
\[
 R(z)=(zI-Y_n)^{-1},\qquad
 R^{(m)}(z)=(zI-Y_n(I+D_n^{(m)}))^{-1}.
\]
Since $R(z)Y_n=zR(z)-I$, the deterministic bilinear limits in
\eqref{u:eq:resolvent} imply
\[
 \mathsf V_{n,m}R(z)Y_n\mathsf U_{n,m}
 =z\mathsf V_{n,m}R(z)\mathsf U_{n,m}-\mathsf V_{n,m}\mathsf U_{n,m},
\]
and, for each fixed $m$,
\[
 \sup_{z\in K}\lVert\mathsf V_{n,m}R(z)Y_n\mathsf U_{n,m}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}
 \xrightarrow{\mathbb P}0.
\]
Indeed, all $m^2$ scalar entries converge uniformly to zero in
probability, and $m$ is fixed. With probability tending to one,
$I_m-\mathsf V_{n,m}R(z)Y_n\mathsf U_{n,m}$ is invertible and its
inverse has norm at most $2$, uniformly on $K$. Woodbury gives
\[
 R^{(m)}(z)=R(z)+R(z)Y_n\mathsf U_{n,m}
       (I_m-\mathsf V_{n,m}R(z)Y_n\mathsf U_{n,m})^{-1}\mathsf V_{n,m}R(z).
\]
Its norm is at most $C_0=M_K+2M_K^2MC$, independent of $m$, with
probability tending to one for every fixed $m$. For deterministic
unit vectors $p_n,q_n$, use $R(z)Y_n=zR(z)-I$, bound the middle
inverse in the Woodbury correction by $2$, and use
$\lVert\mathsf V_{n,m}R(z)q_n\rVert\le
\lVert\mathsf V_{n,m}\rVert\lVertR(z)\rVert\lVertq_n\rVert\le M_K$
to obtain, on the above events,
\[
 \sup_{z\in K}|p_n^*(R^{(m)}(z)-R(z))q_n|
 \le 2M_K\sup_{z\in K}\lVertp_n^*(zR(z)-I)\mathsf U_{n,m}\rVert
 \xrightarrow{\mathbb P}0.
\]
Indeed, the row vector inside the norm has $m$ coordinates, each tending
uniformly to zero by \eqref{u:eq:resolvent}; the columns of
$\mathsf U_{n,m}$ are deterministic and bounded, and $m$ is fixed.

To restore the remainder, write $R_D(z)=(zI-Y_n(I+D_n))^{-1}$ and
$\varepsilon_m=MC/\sqrt{m+1}$. Choose $m$ with $C_0\varepsilon_m<1/2$.
Since $\lVertY_nE_n^{(m)}\rVert\le\varepsilon_m$ with probability tending
to one, the Neumann series gives, uniformly on $K$,
\[
 R_D=(I-R^{(m)}Y_nE_n^{(m)})^{-1}R^{(m)},\qquad
 \lVertR_D\rVert\le\frac{C_0}{1-C_0\varepsilon_m}\le2C_0.
\]
The resolvent identity then yields
\[
 \sup_{z\in K}\lVertR_D(z)-R^{(m)}(z)\rVert
 =\sup_{z\in K}\lVertR_D(z)Y_nE_n^{(m)}R^{(m)}(z)\rVert
 \le2C_0^2\varepsilon_m.
\]
Consequently, on these events,
\begin{align*}
 \sup_{z\in K}\left|p_n^*R_D(z)q_n-\frac{p_n^*q_n}{z}\right|
 &\le \sup_{z\in K}|p_n^*(R^{(m)}(z)-R(z))q_n|\\
 &\quad+\sup_{z\in K}\left|p_n^*R(z)q_n-\frac{p_n^*q_n}{z}\right|
       +2C_0^2\varepsilon_m.
\end{align*}
For fixed $m$, the first two terms tend to zero in probability as
$n\to\infty$; then $m\to\infty$ removes the last term. This proves
both the resolvent bound and the bilinear limits. Another Neumann
series absorbs $\mathcal E_n^{\rm pert}$ using
$\lVert\mathcal E_n^{\rm pert}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{\mathbb P}0$, with no
independence requirement.

For the outlier assertion, use the perturbation from the statement and set
$W_n=Y_n(I+D_n)+\mathcal E_n^{\rm pert}$. We seek an outlier of
$W_n+s v_nv_n^*$. On a small closed disk about
$s$ contained in $\{|z|>1\}$, $W_n$ has no resolvent poles and
$1-sv_n^*(zI-W_n)^{-1}v_n\to1-s/z$ uniformly. The determinant lemma
and Rouch\'e's theorem give exactly one eigenvalue in the disk.
For real data it is real by conjugation symmetry. For fixed $K,C,s$,
these probability bounds are uniform over the permitted deterministic
matrices and unit vectors, provided the perturbation error converges
uniformly over these data. This follows by a subsequence contradiction;
the argument does not require simultaneous control over directions
selected from the data.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{u:lem:spherical-polar}
Write $p=\beta n/2$. We use the gamma function
$\Gamma(s)=\int_0^\infty x^{s-1}e^{-x}\,\mathrm d x$ for $s>0$, so
$\Gamma(n)=(n-1)!$, and write $\lambda_{\min}(H)$ for the smallest
eigenvalue of $H$. For every $A=A^*>0$ in $\mathbb F^{n\times n}$,
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:spherical-negative-moment}
 \int(v^*Av)^{-p}\,\mathrm d\sigma_n(v)=(\det A)^{-\beta/2}.
\end{equation}
For $H\ge0$ and $\eta>0$, set
$\mathcal I_\eta(H)=\int e^{-pv^*Hv/\eta^2}\,\mathrm d\sigma_n(v)$.
Then
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:spherical-upper}
 \frac1n\log\mathcal I_\eta(H)
 \le\frac\beta2\left(\log\eta^2+\eta^2-1\right)
       -\frac\beta{2n}\log\det(H+\eta^4I).
\end{equation}
In the complex case, for $n\ge2$, one also has
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:witness}
 \mathcal I_\eta(H)
 \ge\frac{\Gamma(n)}{n^{n-1}}
       \frac{\eta^{2n}}{\det(H+2\eta^2I)}
       e^{-n\lambda_{\min}(H)/\eta^2}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
\emph{Identity and upper bound.}
Diagonalization and Gaussian integration give
\[
 \frac{\int_{\mathbb F^n}e^{-x^*Ax}\,\mathrm d x}
      {\int_{\mathbb F^n}e^{-\lVertx\rVert^2}\,\mathrm d x}=(\det A)^{-\beta/2}.
\]
In polar coordinates $x=rv$, the radial integral in the numerator is
$\tfrac12\Gamma(p)(v^*Av)^{-p}$. Dividing by the case $A=I$ proves
\eqref{u:eq:spherical-negative-moment}. Finally, $\log u\le u-1$ gives
$e^{-px}\le e^{p(\eta^2-1)}(x+\eta^2)^{-p}$ for $x\ge0$.
Apply this with $x=v^*Hv/\eta^2$ and use
\eqref{u:eq:spherical-negative-moment} for $A=H+\eta^4I$.

\emph{Lower bound.}
Suppose $\mathbb F=\mathbb C$ and diagonalize $H$ with eigenvalues
$0\le h_1\le\cdots\le h_n$. Normalizing a standard complex Gaussian
vector gives $w_j=|v_j|^2=Y_j/\sum_iY_i$ for independent rate-one
exponentials $Y_j$. The substitution $Y_j=sw_j$ has Jacobian $s^{n-1}$;
integrating over $s>0$ gives density $\Gamma(n)$ in $(w_2,\ldots,w_n)$ on
$\mathcal S=\{w_j\ge0:\sum_{j=2}^n w_j\le1\}$.
Set $\lambda_j=n(h_j+2\eta^2)/\eta^2$ for $j\ge2$. Then
\begin{align*}
 \mathcal I_\eta(H)
 &=\Gamma(n)e^{-nh_1/\eta^2}
     \int_{\mathcal S}e^{-n\sum_{j=2}^n(h_j-h_1)w_j/\eta^2}\,\mathrm d w\\
 &\ge\Gamma(n)e^{-nh_1/\eta^2}
     \left(\prod_{j=2}^n\lambda_j^{-1}\right)
     \mathbb P\left\{\sum_{j=2}^n E_j\le1\right\},
\end{align*}
where $E_j$ are independent exponentials of rates $\lambda_j$.
Since $\lambda_j\ge2n$, Markov's inequality bounds the last probability
below by $1-(n-1)/(2n)\ge1/2$. Finally,
\[
 \frac12\prod_{j=2}^n\lambda_j^{-1}
 =\frac{\eta^{2n}}{n^{n-1}\det(H+2\eta^2I)}
       \frac{h_1+2\eta^2}{2\eta^2}
 \ge\frac{\eta^{2n}}{n^{n-1}\det(H+2\eta^2I)},
\]
which proves \eqref{u:eq:witness}.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{u:lem:flat-annealed}
Under \eqref{u:eq:basic} and, in the complex case, \eqref{u:eq:properness},
for fixed $b,\eta,L$ as above one has
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:annealed-lower}
 \log\mathbb E_{\mathbb P_n}Z_{b,\eta,L}(X_n)=n\mathcal A_{\beta,\eta}(b)+O(\sqrt n),\qquad
 \mathcal A_{\beta,\eta}(b)=\frac\beta2
 \left(\log\frac{\eta^2}{1+\eta^2}-\frac{|b|^2}{1+\eta^2}\right).
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
For $v\in\mathcal F_{n,L}$, set $t_i=\sqrt n\,b v_i$. Then
$\sum_j|v_j|^3\le L/\sqrt n$ and $|t_i|\le |b|L$.
Lemma~\ref{u:lem:soft-replacement}, with $a=2\eta^2/\beta$ and $q=1$,
gives uniformly in $v$ and $i$,
\[
 \mathcal Z^{\mathrm{row}}_v(t_i)=\left(\frac{\eta^2}{1+\eta^2}\right)^{\beta/2}
 e^{-\beta|t_i|^2/(2(1+\eta^2))}\bigl(1+O(n^{-1/2})\bigr).
\]
Its additive error becomes relative because the displayed Gaussian
factor is bounded below by a positive constant for $|t_i|\le |b|L$.
Row independence and $\sum_i|t_i|^2=n|b|^2$ therefore yield
\[
 \mathbb E_{\mathbb P_n}Z_{b,\eta,L}(X_n)
 =\int\prod_{i=1}^n \mathcal Z^{\mathrm{row}}_v(t_i)\,\mathrm d\nu_{n,L}(v)
 =\exp\{n\mathcal A_{\beta,\eta}(b)+O(\sqrt n)\},
\]
since $\nu_{n,L}$ is a probability measure and the error is uniform.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
Fix $r>1$, $b>r$, $0<\eta^2<b/r-1$, and sufficiently large $L$.
All these parameters remain fixed as $n\to\infty$.
Define the tilted matrix law by
\[
 \frac{d\mathbb Q_n^{b,\eta,L}}{d\mathbb P_n}(X)
 =\frac{Z_{b,\eta,L}(X)}{\mathbb E_{\mathbb P_n}Z_{b,\eta,L}(X_n)}.
\]
The joint tilted law of the matrix and the auxiliary direction is
\[
 d\widehat{\mathbb Q}_n^{b,\eta,L}(X,v)
 =\frac{e^{-\beta n\lVert(X-bI)v\rVert^2/(2\eta^2)}}
 {\mathbb E_{\mathbb P_n}Z_{b,\eta,L}(X_n)}
 \,d\mathbb P_n(X)\,d\nu_{n,L}(v).
\]
Its matrix marginal is $\mathbb Q_n^{b,\eta,L}$. Under the joint
tilt the direction need not retain the distribution $\nu_{n,L}$;
we first condition on $v$ and then integrate against its tilted marginal.
Conditional on $v$, the unnormalized rows $\sqrt n\,X_{i,\cdot}$ are
independent with laws $\mu_{v,t_i}$ from \eqref{u:eq:row-tilt}, where
$t_i=\sqrt n\,b v_i$. Lemmas~\ref{u:lem:coupling}--\ref{u:lem:outlier} imply
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:tilted-event}
 \mathbb Q_n^{b,\eta,L}\{\rho(X_n)>r\}\longrightarrow1
 \quad\hbox{for }1<r<b/(1+\eta^2).
\end{equation}
In the real case, the spectral-radius event can be replaced by the
event that a positive real eigenvalue exceeds the same threshold.
The uniformity over deterministic $v$ allows integration against its
tilted marginal.

Since $0<Z_{b,\eta,L}(X)\le1$, Lemma~\ref{u:lem:flat-annealed} transfers any $Ce^{-cn^2}$
exception under $\mathbb P_n$ to an $o(1)$ exception under $\mathbb Q_n^{b,\eta,L}$.
Write $H=(X_n-bI)^*(X_n-bI)$. Restricting the spherical integral and
normalizing by $q_{n,L}$ gives
$Z_{b,\eta,L}(X_n)\le q_{n,L}^{-1}\mathcal I_\eta(H)$.
Apply \eqref{u:eq:bulk-lower} with $\tau=\eta^2$ and deviation $\eta^2$,
so no separate regularization or error parameter is needed.
With $\mathbb Q_n^{b,\eta,L}$-probability tending to one,
$F_{\eta^2,n}(b)\ge2\log b-\eta^2$.
On this event, \eqref{u:eq:flat-mass} and \eqref{u:eq:spherical-upper} yield
\begin{align*}
 \frac1n\log Z_{b,\eta,L}(X_n)
 &\le-\frac1n\log q_{n,L}
   +\frac\beta2\left[\log\eta^2+\eta^2-1-F_{\eta^2,n}(b)\right]\\
 &\le a_L+\frac\beta2\left[\log\eta^2-1-2\log b+2\eta^2\right]
   +o(1).
\end{align*}
Subtracting the normalizing constant from \eqref{u:eq:annealed-lower} now gives
\begin{align}
 \frac1n\log\frac{d\mathbb Q_n^{b,\eta,L}}{d\mathbb P_n}
 &=\frac1n\log Z_{b,\eta,L}(X_n)
   -\frac1n\log\mathbb E_{\mathbb P_n}Z_{b,\eta,L}(X_n)\notag\\
 &\le a_L+\frac\beta2\left[\log\eta^2-1-2\log b+2\eta^2\right]
   -\mathcal A_{\beta,\eta}(b)+o(1)\notag\\
 &=\frac\beta2\left[\frac{b^2}{1+\eta^2}+\log(1+\eta^2)
                   -2\log b-1+2\eta^2\right]+a_L+o(1).\label{u:eq:cost}
\end{align}
Since $b^2/(1+\eta^2)\le b^2$ and $\log(1+\eta^2)\le\eta^2$,
the last bound is at most $I_\beta(b)+a_L+3\beta\eta^2/2+o(1)$.
On the intersection of the bulk event with $\{\rho(X_n)>r\}$,
use $d\mathbb P_n=(d\mathbb Q_n^{b,\eta,L}/d\mathbb P_n)^{-1}
d\mathbb Q_n^{b,\eta,L}$. This intersection has tilted probability tending
to one by \eqref{u:eq:tilted-event}, so
\[
 \liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac1n\log\mathbb P_n\{\rho(X_n)>r\}
 \ge-I_\beta(b)-a_L-\frac{3\beta}{2}\eta^2.
\]
Let $L\to\infty$, then $\eta\downarrow0$, and finally $b\downarrow r$.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{u:lem:full-sphere}
Assume $\mathbb F=\mathbb C$, $\mathbb E\xi=0$, $\mathbb E|\xi|^2=1$, \eqref{u:eq:properness}, and
\emph{sharp subgaussianity} \eqref{u:eq:sharp}: for every $u\in\mathbb C$,
$\mathbb E e^{2\operatorname{Re}(\bar u\xi)}\le e^{|u|^2}
=\mathbb E e^{2\operatorname{Re}(\bar u g)}$, where $g$ is a standard proper
complex Gaussian with $\mathbb E|g|^2=1$.
For every fixed $\eta>0$ and $R<\infty$,
\[
 \limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{|z|\le R}
 \left[\frac1n\log\mathbb E Z_z(X_n)-\mathcal A_\eta(|z|)\right]\le0.
\]
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
Fix $\eta>0$ and $R<\infty$. All errors below are uniform over $|z|\le R$
and unit vectors $v$; the cutoffs are explicit functions of $n$,
so only the limit $n\to\infty$ is needed.

\emph{Row decomposition and sharp Laplace bound.}
For a deterministic unit vector $v\in\mathbb C^n$, define
\[
 \mathcal Z^{\mathrm{row}}_v(t)=\mathbb E e^{-|\sum_jv_j\xi_j-t|^2/\eta^2},\qquad
 t_i=\sqrt n\,zv_i.
\]
Row independence gives
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:full-sphere-rows}
 \mathbb E Z_z(X_n)=\int\prod_{i=1}^n \mathcal Z^{\mathrm{row}}_v(t_i)\,\mathrm d\sigma_n(v),
 \qquad \sum_i|t_i|^2=n|z|^2.
\end{equation}
Define
\[
 I(v)=\{j:|v_j|>n^{-1/4}\},\qquad |I(v)|\le\sqrt n,
 \qquad q=q(v)=\sum_{j\notin I(v)}|v_j|^2,
\]
and split the original row sum into two independent parts:
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 S_{\rm large}&=\sum_{j\in I(v)}v_j\xi_j,
 &\mathbb E|S_{\rm large}|^2&=1-q,\\
 S_{\rm small}&=\sum_{j\notin I(v)}v_j\xi_j,
 &\mathbb E|S_{\rm small}|^2&=q.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Independence and the sharp entry bound \eqref{u:eq:sharp} give, for $u\in\mathbb C$,
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:large-sharp-laplace}
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E e^{2\operatorname{Re}(\bar uS_{\rm large})}
 &=\prod_{j\in I(v)}\mathbb E e^{2\operatorname{Re}(\bar u v_j\xi_j)}\\
 &\overset{\eqref{u:eq:sharp}}{\le}
 e^{|u|^2\sum_{j\in I(v)}|v_j|^2}=e^{(1-q)|u|^2}.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Thus its Laplace coefficient is exactly its variance $1-q$, as for a
proper Gaussian of that variance. The same calculation gives coefficient
$1$ for the full row sum.
We use the elementary implication
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:row-chernoff}
 \mathbb E e^{2\operatorname{Re}(\bar u\zeta)}\le e^{s|u|^2}\ (u\in\mathbb C)
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \mathbb E e^{-|\zeta-t|^2/a}\le e^{-|t|^2/(a+s)},\qquad a>0,\ s\ge0.
\end{equation}
Indeed, $-|\zeta-t|^2/a\le2\operatorname{Re}(\bar u(\zeta-t))+a|u|^2$;
take expectations and choose $u=t/(a+s)$. For the full row, $s=1$, so
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:row-all-targets}
 e^{|t|^2/(1+\eta^2)}\mathcal Z^{\mathrm{row}}_v(t)\le1,
 \qquad t\in\mathbb C.
\end{equation}
This bound has no additive error and will be used for large targets.
The replacement below improves the prefactor to $h(q)$, at the cost
of an additive error that may dominate for large $|t|$.

\emph{Replace only the small part.}
Conditional on $S_{\rm large}$, replace $S_{\rm small}$ by an independent
proper Gaussian $G_q$ with $\mathbb E|G_q|^2=q$. The large part retains its
original law. Covariance matching and \eqref{u:eq:properness} give
$\mathbb E G_q^2=\mathbb E S_{\rm small}^2
=\sum_{j\notin I(v)}v_j^2\mathbb E\xi_j^2=0$ for every $v$.
Thus $G_q\overset d=\sqrt q\,g$ with a standard proper complex Gaussian
$g$ whose law does not depend on $v$; the only dependence is through $q$.
The Gaussian replacement formula \eqref{u:eq:smooth-replacement}
of Lemma~\ref{u:lem:soft-replacement}, proved in Subsection~\ref{u:sec:coupling-details},
uses only moment matching and a finite third moment. Its additive error
is at most $C_{\mu,\eta}n^{-1/4}$, since
$\sum_{j\notin I(v)}|v_j|^3\le n^{-1/4}$.
To integrate out $G_q$, for $q>0$ and $s\in\mathbb C$ complete the square:
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E e^{-|G_q-s|^2/\eta^2}
 &=\frac1{\pi q}\int_\mathbb C
 \exp\left[-\frac{\eta^2+q}{\eta^2q}
 \left|w-\frac{q s}{\eta^2+q}\right|^2
 -\frac{|s|^2}{\eta^2+q}\right]\,\mathrm d^2w\\
 &=\underbrace{\frac{\eta^2}{\eta^2+q}}_{=:h(q)}
 e^{-|s|^2/(\eta^2+q)}.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Here $\,\mathrm d^2w$ is planar Lebesgue measure and
$\int_\mathbb C e^{-c|w-w_0|^2}\,\mathrm d^2w=\pi/c$.
For $q=0$, $G_q=0$ and the same formula holds with $h(0)=1$.
This is \eqref{u:eq:gaussian-soft-formula} with $a=\eta^2$ and $\beta=2$.
Set $s=t-S_{\rm large}$ and average over $S_{\rm large}$; then apply
\eqref{u:eq:row-chernoff} with $a=\eta^2+q$ and Laplace coefficient $1-q$
from \eqref{u:eq:large-sharp-laplace}:
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:row-light-replacement}
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathcal Z^{\mathrm{row}}_v(t)
 &\le h(q)\mathbb E e^{-|S_{\rm large}-t|^2/(\eta^2+q)}+C_{\mu,\eta}n^{-1/4}\\
 &\overset{\eqref{u:eq:large-sharp-laplace}}{\le}
 h(q)e^{-|t|^2/(1+\eta^2)}+C_{\mu,\eta}n^{-1/4}.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Sharpness gives the Gaussian denominator $(\eta^2+q)+(1-q)=1+\eta^2$;
a larger Laplace coefficient would weaken the exponent.

\emph{Multiply the rows and average over the sphere.}
For $|t|^2\le\frac18\log n$, the Gaussian main term in
\eqref{u:eq:row-light-replacement} is at least $h(1)n^{-1/8}$.
Hence its additive error becomes a relative $O_{\mu,\eta}(n^{-1/8})$ error.
By \eqref{u:eq:full-sphere-rows}, at most $8n|z|^2/\log n$ targets violate
this cutoff. Use the sharp bound \eqref{u:eq:row-all-targets} there.
Since $h(q)\ge h(1)$, multiplication gives
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:row-product}
 \prod_i \mathcal Z^{\mathrm{row}}_v(t_i)\le h(q(v))^n
 \exp\left[-\frac{n|z|^2}{1+\eta^2}
       +O_{\mu,\eta,R}\left(n^{7/8}+\frac n{\log n}\right)\right].
\end{equation}
For a fixed set $I$ of size $m$, write $q_I=\sum_{j\notin I}|v_j|^2$.
Lemma~\ref{u:lem:spherical-polar}, with $A=\eta^2I_n+P_{I^c}$, gives exactly
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:beta}
 \mathbb E_{\sigma_n}h(q_I)^n
 =\frac{\eta^{2n}}{\det(\eta^2I_n+P_{I^c})}
 =h(1)^{n-m}.
\end{equation}
Here $P_{I^c}$ is the coordinate projection onto $I^c$.
Since $|I(v)|\le\sqrt n$, summing over all possible sets gives
\[
 \mathbb E_{\sigma_n}h(q(v))^n
 \le\sum_{m=0}^{\lfloor\sqrt n\rfloor}\binom nm h(1)^{n-m}
 =h(1)^n e^{O_\eta(\sqrt n\log n)}.
\]
Combining with \eqref{u:eq:full-sphere-rows} and \eqref{u:eq:row-product},
uniformly for $|z|\le R$,
\[
 \frac1n\log\mathbb E Z_z(X_n)-\mathcal A_\eta(|z|)
 \le O_{\mu,\eta,R}\left(n^{-1/8}+\frac1{\log n}
                         +\frac{\log n}{\sqrt n}\right)=o(1).
\]
This proves the lemma.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
\leavevmode\par
Under \eqref{u:eq:sharp}, independence and Chernoff give
$\mathbb P\{|u^*X_nv|>s\}\le4e^{-ns^2/2}$ for deterministic unit vectors
$u,v$ (test real and imaginary parts at $s/\sqrt2$).
Two $1/4$-nets, each of size at most $9^{2n}$, bound $\lVertX_n\rVert$ by
twice the largest tested bilinear form; a union bound gives
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:norm-tail}
 \mathbb P\{\lVertX_n\rVert>R\}\le4\exp(4n\log9-nR^2/8).
\end{equation}
Any fixed subgaussian constant would suffice for this norm cutoff.
Fix $r>1$, and choose fixed $R$ so that the coefficient of $n$
in the exponent in \eqref{u:eq:norm-tail} is below $-I_2(r)-1$. Let
$K=\{(1+r)/2\le|z|\le R+1\}$ and fix small $\eta$ with
$1+\eta^2<r^2$. Take a deterministic $1/n$-net of $\{r\le|z|\le R\}$,
of cardinality $O_R(n^2)$, contained in $\{r-1/n\le|z|\le R+1/n\}$.
An eigenvalue in the annulus supplies a grid point with
$s_{\min}(X_n-zI)\le1/n$, where $s_{\min}$ denotes the smallest
singular value. Apply \eqref{u:eq:witness} from Lemma~\ref{u:lem:spherical-polar} with
$H=(X_n-zI)^*(X_n-zI)$, whose smallest eigenvalue is
$s_{\min}(X_n-zI)^2$.
On the bulk event \eqref{u:eq:bulk-upper} with $\tau=\sqrt2\eta$,
Stirling and \eqref{u:eq:witness} give at this point
\[
 \frac1n\log Z_z(X_n)\ge
 \log\eta^2-1-2\log|z|-2M_K^2\eta^2-\delta+o(1).
\]
Markov's inequality, Lemma~\ref{u:lem:full-sphere}, and a union bound give
\[
 \limsup_n\frac1n\log\mathbb P\{\rho(X_n)\ge r,\ \lVertX_n\rVert\le R,
                   \text{bulk event of \eqref{u:eq:bulk-upper}}\}
 \le-I_{2,\eta}(r)+2M_K^2\eta^2+\delta,
\]
where $I_{2,\eta}(b)=b^2/(1+\eta^2)-1-2\log b+\log(1+\eta^2)$ is
increasing for $b>\sqrt{1+\eta^2}$. The grid size is negligible at speed
$n$, and the bulk failure probability decays at speed $n^2$.
Let $\delta,\eta\downarrow0$ after the dimension limit, with $R$ fixed.
Then $I_{2,\eta}(r)\to I_2(r)$ and the desired upper bound follows.
Theorem~\ref{u:thm:lower} supplies the matching lower bound.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
\emph{A lower bound near a prescribed point.}
We first show that, under \eqref{u:eq:basic}--\eqref{u:eq:properness},
for every $w\in\mathbb C$ with $|w|>1$ and every $\delta>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:point-local-lower}
 \liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac1n\log\mathbb P(\mathcal E_n(w,\delta))
 \ge-I_2(|w|).
\end{equation}
Fix $\eta>0$, let $b=(1+\eta^2)w$, and fix sufficiently large $L$.
Use the joint tilt $\widehat{\mathbb Q}_n^{b,\eta,L}$ and its matrix
marginal $\mathbb Q_n^{b,\eta,L}$ from the proof of
Theorem~\ref{u:thm:lower}, now with this complex $b$ and $\beta=2$.
Lemma~\ref{u:lem:flat-annealed} gives
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:local-normalization}
 \frac1n\log\mathbb E_{\mathbb P_n}Z_{b,\eta,L}(X_n)
 =\log\frac{\eta^2}{1+\eta^2}
  -\frac{|b|^2}{1+\eta^2}+o(1).
\end{equation}
Indeed, the row targets $t_i=\sqrt n\,b v_i$ satisfy
$|t_i|\le |b|L$ and $\sum_i|t_i|^2=n|b|^2$ for every
$v\in\mathcal F_{n,L}$; no rotation of the entry law is used.

Conditional on such a deterministic $v$, Lemma~\ref{u:lem:coupling}
represents the tilted matrix as
\[
 X_n=Y_nT+wvv^*+E_n,\qquad
 \lVertT-I\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\le(2\eta^4)^{-1},\qquad
 \lVertE_n\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{\mathbb P}0.
\]
Lemma~\ref{u:lem:outlier}, with the complex spike $s=w$, gives an
eigenvalue converging to $w$. Its uniformity over deterministic flat
directions allows integration against the tilted marginal of $v$.
Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:local-tilted-event}
 \mathbb Q_n^{b,\eta,L}(\mathcal E_n(w,\delta))\longrightarrow1.
\end{equation}

Let $\mathcal B_n=\{F_{\eta^2,n}(b)\ge2\log|b|-\eta^2\}$.
The complex-point version of \eqref{u:eq:bulk-lower} gives
$\mathbb P_n(\mathcal B_n^c)\le Ce^{-cn^2}$.
Since $Z_{b,\eta,L}\le1$, \eqref{u:eq:local-normalization} implies
$\mathbb Q_n^{b,\eta,L}(\mathcal B_n^c)=o(1)$.
On $\mathcal B_n$, \eqref{u:eq:flat-mass} and
\eqref{u:eq:spherical-upper} yield
\[
 \frac1n\log Z_{b,\eta,L}(X_n)
 \le a_L+\log\eta^2-1-2\log|b|+2\eta^2+o(1).
\]
Subtracting \eqref{u:eq:local-normalization}, we obtain
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:local-cost}
 \frac1n\log\frac{d\mathbb Q_n^{b,\eta,L}}{d\mathbb P_n}(X_n)
 \le a_L+C_\eta(w)+o(1),
\end{equation}
where
\begin{align*}
 C_\eta(w)&=\frac{|b|^2}{1+\eta^2}+\log(1+\eta^2)
             -2\log|b|-1+2\eta^2\\
 &=I_2(|w|)+\eta^2|w|^2-\log(1+\eta^2)+2\eta^2.
\end{align*}
By \eqref{u:eq:local-tilted-event},
$\mathbb Q_n^{b,\eta,L}(\mathcal E_n(w,\delta)\cap\mathcal B_n)\to1$.
Integrating the reciprocal likelihood ratio on this intersection gives
\[
 \liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac1n\log\mathbb P_n(\mathcal E_n(w,\delta))
 \ge-a_L-C_\eta(w).
\]
Let $L\to\infty$ and then $\eta\downarrow0$ to obtain
\eqref{u:eq:point-local-lower}.

\emph{The rate for a fixed disk.}
Fix $|z|>1$ and $0<\varepsilon<|z|-1$, and set
$a=|z|-\varepsilon>1$. For $0<t<\varepsilon$, set
$w_t=(a+t)z/|z|$. Then
$B(w_t,t/2)\subset B(z,\varepsilon)$, so
\eqref{u:eq:point-local-lower} gives
\[
 \liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac1n\log\mathbb P(\mathcal E_n(z,\varepsilon))
 \ge-I_2(a+t).
\]
Letting $t\downarrow0$ proves \eqref{u:eq:complex-local-lower}.
Under \eqref{u:eq:sharp}, the reverse bound follows from
\[
 \mathcal E_n(z,\varepsilon)\subset\{\rho(X_n)>a\}
\]
and Theorem~\ref{u:thm:matching}. This proves
\eqref{u:eq:complex-local}; its small-disk limit follows by continuity
of $I_2$.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- fact-1: body_md -->
\label{u:fact:conditional-schur}
Let $G_n$ have iid real $N(0,1/n)$ entries. Write $G_n=QUQ^\top$
in ordered real Schur form, where $Q$ is orthogonal and
\[
 U=\begin{pmatrix}
 \mathsf S_1&N_{12}&\cdots&N_{1,N_{\rm blk}}\\
 0&\mathsf S_2&\cdots&N_{2,N_{\rm blk}}\\
 \vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
 0&\cdots&0&\mathsf S_{N_{\rm blk}}
 \end{pmatrix}.
\]
Here $N_{\rm blk}$ is the number of diagonal blocks,
$\mathsf S_a\in\mathbb R^{d_a\times d_a}$ with $d_a\in\{1,2\}$, and
$N_{ab}\in\mathbb R^{d_a\times d_b}$ for $a<b$.
Fix an ordering of the eigenvalues and the corresponding block pattern.
Use the diagonal-block convention in item~1 below, and fix the remaining
sign of each block basis by requiring its first column to have positive
first nonzero coordinate. The following statements hold for almost every
spectrum, in the sense of a regular conditional distribution.
\begin{enumerate}
\item A real eigenvalue $\lambda_a$ gives the fixed scalar block
$\mathsf S_a=[\lambda_a]$. A conjugate pair $x_a\pm i y_a$, $y_a>0$, gives
\[
 \mathsf S_a=\begin{pmatrix}x_a&b_a\\-c_a&x_a\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 b_a\ge c_a>0,\qquad b_ac_a=y_a^2.
\]
Its remaining random coordinate is $\Delta_a=b_a-c_a\ge0$, with
\[
 b_a=\frac{\sqrt{\Delta_a^2+4y_a^2}+\Delta_a}{2},\qquad
 c_a=\frac{\sqrt{\Delta_a^2+4y_a^2}-\Delta_a}{2}.
\]
\item For each $a<b$, the off-diagonal block $N_{ab}$ has density
\[
 B\longmapsto
 \left(\frac n{2\pi}\right)^{d_ad_b/2}
 e^{-n\lVertB\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2/2},\qquad B\in\mathbb R^{d_a\times d_b},
\]
with respect to Lebesgue measure on its $d_ad_b$ scalar entries.
Each scalar entry thus has density
$x\mapsto\sqrt{n/(2\pi)}e^{-nx^2/2}$, $x\in\mathbb R$.
\item For each two-dimensional diagonal block $\mathsf S_a$, its
coordinate $\Delta_a=b_a-c_a$ has density
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:conditional-schur-density}
 p_{n,y_a}(t)=\frac1{Z_{n,y_a}}
 \frac{t}{\sqrt{t^2+4y_a^2}}e^{-nt^2/2}\mathbf1_{t>0},\qquad
 Z_{n,y}=\int_0^\infty\frac{t e^{-nt^2/2}}{\sqrt{t^2+4y^2}}\,\mathrm d t.
\end{equation}
The numbers $b_a,c_a$ inside $\mathsf S_a$ are functions of $\Delta_a$
as above; they are not entries of an off-diagonal block $N_{ab}$.
\item All scalar entries of all blocks $(N_{ab})_{a<b}$, together with
all coordinates $(\Delta_a)_{d_a=2}$, are mutually independent under
the conditional law given the spectrum.
\item Uniformly in the conditioned spectrum,
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:conditional-schur-second-moments}
 \mathbb E[\Delta_a^2\mid\operatorname{spec}(G_n)]\le\frac2n,
 \qquad
 \mathbb E[\lVertN_{ab}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\mid\operatorname{spec}(G_n)]
 =\frac{d_ad_b}{n}\le\frac4n.
\end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end fact-1 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{u:lem:schur}
Let $G_n$ be an $n\times n$ real random matrix with independent
$N(0,1/n)$ entries.
For each $\varepsilon>0$ there is $C_\varepsilon<\infty$ such that,
almost surely in the eigenvalues, for all integers $k\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:schur}
 \mathbb E[\lVertG_n^k\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\mid\operatorname{spec}(G_n)]
 \le C_\varepsilon n\exp(C_\varepsilon k^{2/3})
 (\max(1,\rho(G_n))+\varepsilon)^{2k}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
Use the Schur decomposition and block notation of
Fact~\ref{u:fact:conditional-schur}, so
$\lVertG_n^k\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}=\lVertU^k\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}$.
The notation $\mathsf S_a^p$ denotes the ordinary matrix power, with
$\mathsf S_a^0=I_{d_a}$.
Condition throughout on $\operatorname{spec}(G_n)$: all expectations
and independence statements below refer to this conditional law.
By that fact, the diagonal blocks are mutually independent, and all scalar entries
of the $N_{ab}$ are independent $N(0,1/n)$ variables, independent of
all diagonal blocks.

\emph{Diagonal block powers.}
Set $r_0=\max(1,\rho(G_n))$, $r_\varepsilon=r_0+\varepsilon$.
For a complex block, write
$\mathsf S=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x&b\\-c&x\end{smallmatrix}\right)$,
$b\ge c>0$, and set $y=\sqrt{bc}$ and $\Delta=b-c$.
The same fact gives $\mathbb E\Delta^2\le2/n$. The normal matrix
$\mathsf C=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x&y\\-y&x\end{smallmatrix}\right)$ has the
same eigenvalues as $\mathsf S$ and $\lVert\mathsf S-\mathsf C\rVert\le\Delta$.
Write $\lambda_\pm=x\pm iy$. Both matrices satisfy the same quadratic
equation, so for $p\ge1$, polynomial division by
$(z-\lambda_+)(z-\lambda_-)$ gives
\[
 \mathsf S^p-\mathsf C^p
 =\left(\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}\lambda_+^j\lambda_-^{p-1-j}\right)
       (\mathsf S-\mathsf C).
\]
Bounding each coefficient by $r_0^{p-1}$ yields, also for $p=0$,
\[
 \lVert\mathsf S^p\rVert\le r_0^p+p\Delta r_0^{p-1}
 \le r_\varepsilon^p(1+\Delta/\varepsilon),\qquad p\in\mathbb Z_{\ge0}.
\]
The last inequality uses
$(r_0+\varepsilon)^p\ge p\varepsilon r_0^{p-1}$ for $p\ge1$.
Moreover, $\mathbb E(1+\Delta/\varepsilon)^2\le2+4/(n\varepsilon^2)
\le C_\varepsilon$, uniformly in the power $p$.
The same bound holds for scalar blocks, with multiplier $1$.

\emph{Path expansion and cancellation.}
Each factor in $U^k$ either stays in the current diagonal block or jumps
to a block with a larger index. The contribution of a strictly increasing
block path $a_0<\cdots<a_\ell$ is therefore the sum over
nonnegative integer exponents satisfying
$p_0+\cdots+p_\ell=k-\ell$ of
\[
 \mathsf S_{a_0}^{p_0}N_{a_0a_1}\mathsf S_{a_1}^{p_1}\cdots
 N_{a_{\ell-1}a_\ell}\mathsf S_{a_\ell}^{p_\ell}.
\]
There are $\binom{k}{\ell}$ ways to allocate the $k-\ell$ diagonal steps
among the $\ell+1$ visited blocks. Distinct paths with the same
endpoints have an edge in their symmetric difference. That independent
centered off-diagonal block occurs linearly in one path only, so the
cross expectation vanishes. Different endpoints occupy different
blocks. Time allocations along a fixed path need not be orthogonal:
the $L^2$ triangle inequality gives the squared factor $\binom{k}{\ell}^2$.
Independence and $\mathbb E\lVertN_{ab}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\le4/n$ then bound the path's
expected squared Hilbert--Schmidt norm by
$2C_\varepsilon^{\ell+1}(4/n)^\ell\binom{k}{\ell}^2r_\varepsilon^{2(k-\ell)}$.

\emph{Counting paths.}
There are at most $\binom n{\ell+1}$ paths with $\ell$ jumps.
Since $\binom n{\ell+1}\le n^{\ell+1}/(\ell+1)!$ and
$\binom{k}{\ell}\le k^\ell/\ell!$, the path count contributes one factorial to the denominator,
and the squared number of time allocations contributes two. Using $r_\varepsilon\ge1$ gives
\[
 \mathbb E\lVertU^k\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\le C_\varepsilon n r_\varepsilon^{2k}
 \sum_{\ell\ge0}\frac{(C_\varepsilon k^2)^\ell}{(\ell!)^3}
 \le C_\varepsilon n r_\varepsilon^{2k}e^{C_\varepsilon k^{2/3}}.
\]
The last inequality follows by retaining only equal-index terms in
$(\sum_{\ell\ge0}a^\ell/\ell!)^3=e^{3a}$, with
$a=(C_\varepsilon k^2)^{1/3}$.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{u:cor:gaussian-power-rate}
Let $G_n$ be an $n\times n$ real random matrix with independent
$N(0,1/n)$ entries. For every sequence of positive integers $k=k_n$
with $k_n/n\to\alpha>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:power-rate}
 \lim_{n\to\infty}\frac1n\log\mathbb E\lVertG_n^{k_n}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 =F_1(\alpha):=\frac{(1+2\alpha)\log(1+2\alpha)-2\alpha}{2}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
Write $\rho_n=\rho(G_n)$ and $S_n=\max(1,\rho_n)$.
The Gaussian right-tail bound \cite[Theorem 1.2, $\beta=1$]{XuZeng},
in the same $N(0,1/n)$ normalization, gives
\[
 \limsup_n\frac1n\log\mathbb P(\rho_n>t)\le-I_1(t),\qquad t\ge1,
\]
where $t=1$ is trivial with $I_1(1)=0$.
For fixed $0<\varepsilon\le1$, Lemma~\ref{u:lem:schur} gives
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:power-tower}
 \mathbb E\lVertG_n^{k_n}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 \le C_\varepsilon n e^{C_\varepsilon k_n^{2/3}}
                \mathbb E(S_n+\varepsilon)^{2k_n}.
\end{equation}
The prefactor has logarithm $o(n)$. Tail integration gives the exact identity
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:power-tail-integral}
 \mathbb E(S_n+\varepsilon)^{2k_n}
 =(1+\varepsilon)^{2k_n}
 +\int_1^\infty 2k_n(t+\varepsilon)^{2k_n-1}\mathbb P(\rho_n>t)\,\mathrm d t.
\end{equation}

For a partition $1=t_0<\cdots<t_q=M$, the integral over $[1,M]$ is at most
$\sum_{j<q}(t_{j+1}+\varepsilon)^{2k_n}\mathbb P(\rho_n>t_j)$.
Apply the tail bound at the fixed partition points, then let the mesh
tend to zero. Including the first term in \eqref{u:eq:power-tail-integral}
gives logarithmic limsup at most
\[
 \sup_{1\le t\le M}\{2\alpha\log(t+\varepsilon)-I_1(t)\}.
\]
For $t\ge M$, the same net argument as for \eqref{u:eq:norm-tail}, now
on the real unit sphere, gives
$\mathbb P(\rho_n>t)\le2e^{n(c_{\mathrm{net}}-t^2/8)}$, $c_{\mathrm{net}}=2\log9$.
Fix $\alpha_+>\alpha$ and choose $M$ so large that
$2\alpha_+\log(t+1)\le t^2/16$ on this range.
Since $k_n\le\alpha_+n$ eventually, the remaining integral is at most
\[
 4\alpha_+n e^{nc_{\mathrm{net}}}\int_M^\infty e^{-nt^2/16}\,\mathrm d t
 \le\frac{32\alpha_+}{M}e^{n(c_{\mathrm{net}}-M^2/16)}.
\]
The last inequality uses $t/M\ge1$ inside the integral.
Its rate tends to $-\infty$ with $M$, uniformly for $0<\varepsilon\le1$.
Combining the bounds with \eqref{u:eq:power-tower}, then letting
$M\to\infty$ and $\varepsilon\downarrow0$, yields
\[
 \limsup_n\frac1n\log\mathbb E\lVertG_n^{k_n}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 \le\sup_{t\ge1}\{2\alpha\log t-I_1(t)\}=F_1(\alpha).
\]
Here the passage $\varepsilon\downarrow0$ uses
$0\le\log(t+\varepsilon)-\log t\le\log(1+\varepsilon)$ for $t\ge1$.
Differentiation places the supremum at $t=\sqrt{1+2\alpha}$.

For the reverse inequality, Theorem~\ref{u:thm:lower} applies to $G_n$.
For each fixed $t>1$, the inequality
$\rho(G_n)^{2k_n}\le\lVertG_n^{k_n}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2$ gives
\[
 \mathbb E\lVertG_n^{k_n}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\ge t^{2k_n}\mathbb P(\rho_n>t),
 \qquad
 \liminf_n\frac1n\log\mathbb E\lVertG_n^{k_n}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 \ge2\alpha\log t-I_1(t).
\]
Taking the supremum over $t>1$ proves the matching lower bound.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
Expand $\mathbb E\lVertX_n^k\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2$ as pairs of directed paths with the
same endpoints. All coefficients are positive. Independence and
symmetry make a term vanish if any entry label has odd multiplicity;
otherwise Gaussian even-moment domination bounds its expectation
by the corresponding Gaussian term. Therefore, for every $n,k$,
\[
 \mathbb E\lVertX_n^k\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\le\mathbb E\lVertG_n^k\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2.
\]
Since $\rho(X_n)^{2k}\le\lVertX_n^k\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2$, Markov and
\eqref{u:eq:power-rate} yield
\[
 \limsup_n n^{-1}\log\mathbb P\{\rho(X_n)\ge r\}
 \le\inf_{\alpha>0}\{F_1(\alpha)-2\alpha\log r\}=-I_1(r).
\]
The minimizer is $\alpha=(r^2-1)/2$. Combine with
Theorem~\ref{u:thm:lower}.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
Every derivative of order at most four of $\varphi_a(x)=e^{-|x|^2/a}$
is a polynomial times the same Gaussian, hence is bounded by a constant
depending only on $a$. Replace the summands one at a time, applying
second-order Taylor expansion to $\varphi_a$ and to each component of
$\nabla\varphi_a$. The constant, linear and quadratic terms agree:
the two entry laws have the same mean and real covariance
$\beta^{-1}\mathrm{Id}_\beta$, and multiplication by $v_j$ is a real linear map.
The remainders are bounded by $C_a m_3|v_j|^3$, uniformly in the sum
of the other entries and in $s$. Summation proves
\eqref{u:eq:smooth-replacement}. Bounded derivatives also justify
differentiation under each expectation.

The Gaussian sum has independent real coordinates of variance $q/\beta$.
Completing the square in each coordinate gives
\[
 \mathbb E e^{-(s_\ell-Z)^2/a}
 =\sqrt{\frac{a}{a+2q/\beta}}
           e^{-s_\ell^2/(a+2q/\beta)},\qquad Z\sim N(0,q/\beta).
\]
Multiplication over $\beta$ coordinates proves
\eqref{u:eq:gaussian-soft-formula}; for $q=0$ the sum is zero.
Finally Jensen gives $\mathcal Z_\mu(s),\mathcal Z_G(s)\ge
J:=e^{-(K^2+1)/a}$ on $|s|\le K$. Thus
\[
 \left|\frac{\nabla\mathcal Z_\mu}{\mathcal Z_\mu}
              -\frac{\nabla\mathcal Z_G}{\mathcal Z_G}\right|
 \le J^{-1}|\nabla\mathcal Z_\mu-\nabla\mathcal Z_G|
       +J^{-2}|\nabla\mathcal Z_G|\,|\mathcal Z_\mu-\mathcal Z_G|.
\]
The Gaussian derivative is bounded, so \eqref{u:eq:compact-score} follows.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
All estimates are uniform over the permitted parameters, with
$\eta,L,T_0$ fixed. Constants may depend on $\mu,\eta,L,T_0$;
we display the $\eta$-dependence in the remainder bounds, suppressing
dependence on the fixed $\mu,L,T_0$. Set $\Delta:=\max_j|v_j|$;
the hypothesis of Lemma~\ref{u:lem:coupling} gives $\Delta\le L/\sqrt n$.
Centeredness and \eqref{u:eq:basic} imply
$\mathbb E e^{\langle h,\xi\rangle}\le e^{C|h|^2}$: Taylor expansion gives
$1+O(|h|^2)$ for $|h|\le1$, while Young's inequality absorbs the
square-exponential moment into $e^{C|h|^2}$ for $|h|>1$.
By independence, every original sum
$U=\sum_k a_k\xi_k$ with $\sum_k|a_k|^2\le1$ satisfies
$\mathbb E e^{\langle h,U\rangle}\le e^{C|h|^2}$.
Integrating against an independent standard Gaussian $G\in\mathbb R^\beta$ gives,
for sufficiently small $c>0$,
\[
 \mathbb E e^{c|U|^2}
 =\mathbb E_G\mathbb E_U e^{\sqrt{2c}\langle G,U\rangle}
 \le\mathbb E_G e^{2Cc|G|^2}<\infty.
\]
Since the row density in \eqref{u:eq:row-tilt} is bounded uniformly,
tilted entries and prefix residuals also have uniform square-exponential
moments.

\@startsection{paragraph}{4}\z@
 {.5\linespacing\@plus.5\linespacing}{.25\linespacing}
 {\normalfont\bfseries}{Conditional means.}
Write $\varphi(s)=e^{-\beta|s|^2/(2\eta^2)}$ and, using independent
original entries, define
\[
 U_j=\sum_{k>j}v_k\xi_k,\qquad
 \mathcal Z_j(s)=\mathbb E\varphi(s-U_j),\qquad
 A_j(s)=\int\mathcal Z_j(s-v_jx)\,\mathrm d\mu(x).
\]
For a tilted row with target $t$, set $S_j=t-\sum_{k<j}v_kx_k$.
For a deterministic residual value $s\in\mathbb F$, define the kernel and mean
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:kernel}
 K_{j,s}(\,\mathrm d x)=\frac{\mathcal Z_j(s-v_jx)}{A_j(s)}\,\mathrm d\mu(x),
 \qquad m_j(s)=\int x\,\mathrm d K_{j,s}(x).
\end{equation}
The actual conditional law uses the random residual $s=S_j$:
integrating out the future entries in \eqref{u:eq:row-tilt} gives, under $\mu_{v,t}$,
\[
 \mathcal L(x_j\mid x_1,\ldots,x_{j-1})=K_{j,S_j},\qquad
 \mathbb E[x_j\mid x_1,\ldots,x_{j-1}]=m_j(S_j).
\]
For $\xi\sim\mu$ and the real gradient $\gamma=\nabla\mathcal Z_j(s)\in\mathbb F$,
the Hessian bound $\sup_s\lVert\nabla^2\mathcal Z_j(s)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}
\le C\eta^{-2}$ gives
\[
 \mathcal Z_j(s-v_j\xi)=\mathcal Z_j(s)
 -\langle \gamma,v_j\xi\rangle+O(\eta^{-2}|v_j|^2|\xi|^2).
\]
Averaging and centeredness give $A_j(s)=\mathcal Z_j(s)+O(\eta^{-2}|v_j|^2)$.
In the complex case, \eqref{u:eq:properness} and $\mathbb E|\xi|^2=1$ give
\[
 \mathbb E[\xi\langle \gamma,v_j\xi\rangle]
 =\tfrac12\bigl(\bar \gamma v_j\mathbb E\xi^2
                   +\gamma\bar v_j\mathbb E|\xi|^2\bigr)
 =\tfrac12\bar v_j \gamma.
\]
In the real case this expectation is $v_j\gamma$. Thus centeredness and
the finite third moment yield, in either field,
\begin{align*}
 A_j(s)m_j(s)&=\mathbb E[\xi\mathcal Z_j(s-v_j\xi)]\\
 &=-\mathbb E[\xi\langle \gamma,v_j\xi\rangle]
       +O(\eta^{-2}|v_j|^2\mathbb E|\xi|^3)\\
 &=-\frac{\bar v_j}{\beta}\nabla\mathcal Z_j(s)+O(\eta^{-2}|v_j|^2).
\end{align*}
Recall the tail denominators from Lemma~\ref{u:lem:coupling}:
\[
 d_j=\eta^2+\sum_{k=j}^n|v_k|^2\quad(1\le j\le n+1),
 \qquad d_{n+1}=\eta^2.
\]
Choose an arbitrary deterministic cutoff radius $K$, independent of $n$:
\[
 K\ge1,\qquad |s|\le K\ \text{(compact part)},\qquad
 |s|>K\ \text{(tail)}.
\]
On the compact part, Jensen gives the explicit lower bound
\[
 \min\{A_j(s),\mathcal Z_j(s)\}
 \ge \exp\!\left[-\frac{\beta(K^2+1)}{2\eta^2}\right],\qquad |s|\le K.
\]
The derivative bounds $\lVert\nabla^\ell\varphi\rVert_\infty\le C_\ell\eta^{-\ell}$
for $1\le\ell\le4$ and this lower bound allow the constants in the
conditional-mean estimates below, including those in $O_{\eta,K}$,
to satisfy the coarse bounds
\[
 C_{\eta,K}\le C(1+\eta^{-8})
      \exp\!\left[\frac{C(1+K^2)}{\eta^2}\right],\qquad
 C_\eta\le C(1+\eta^{-8})e^{C/\eta^2}.
\]
Here $C$ depends only on $\mu,L,T_0$; the exponential factors account
for division by the normalizers.
Dividing and applying \eqref{u:eq:compact-score} with
$a=2\eta^2/\beta$ to the future coefficients $k>j$, whose cubic sum
is at most $\Delta$ and whose variance sum is $d_{j+1}-\eta^2$, yields
$m_j(s)=\bar v_j s/d_{j+1}+O_{\eta,K}(\Delta|v_j|)$.
We use $\bar v_j s/d_j$ as the regression term. Its difference from
the leading term just obtained is controlled explicitly: since
$d_j-d_{j+1}=|v_j|^2$ and both denominators are at least $\eta^2$,
\[
 \left|\frac{\bar v_j s}{d_{j+1}}-\frac{\bar v_j s}{d_j}\right|
 =\frac{|s||v_j|^3}{d_jd_{j+1}}
 \le K\eta^{-4}|v_j|^3,\qquad |s|\le K.
\]
Since $|v_j|\le\Delta\le1$, the additional $K\eta^{-4}|v_j|^3$
error is absorbed into $C_{\eta,K}\Delta|v_j|$. Thus
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:regression}
 r_j(s):=m_j(s)-\frac{\bar v_j s}{d_j},\qquad
 \sup_{|s|\le K}|r_j(s)|\le C_{\eta,K}\Delta|v_j|.
\end{equation}
To control the complement of this compact set, take independent
$\xi,\xi'\sim\mu$, independent of the same $U_j$, and define
$F_x=\varphi(s-v_jx-U_j)$. Centeredness gives
\[
 A_j(s)m_j(s)=\tfrac12\mathbb E[(\xi-\xi')(F_\xi-F_{\xi'})].
\]
The function $\sqrt\varphi$ is $C/\eta$-Lipschitz, so Cauchy--Schwarz and
$\mathbb E(\sqrt{F_\xi}+\sqrt{F_{\xi'}})^2\le4A_j(s)$ imply
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:weighted-mean}
 A_j(s)|m_j(s)|^2\le C\eta^{-2}|v_j|^2\mathbb E|\xi-\xi'|^4
 \le C\eta^{-2}|v_j|^2.
\end{equation}
The tilted prefix density relative to the original product prefix law
is $A_j(S_j)/\mathcal Z^{\mathrm{row}}_v(t)$. Thus, using $A_j\le1$ and $d_j\ge\eta^2$,
\[
 \mathbb E_{\mu_{v,t}}[|r_j(S_j)|^2\mathbf1_{\{|S_j|>K\}}]
 \le C_\eta|v_j|^2\mathbb E_0[(1+|S_j|^2)\mathbf1_{\{|S_j|>K\}}]
 \le C_\eta|v_j|^2e^{-cK^2},
\]
where $\mathbb E_0$ evaluates the same residual function on original independent
prefix entries. Summing the tail bound over $j=1,\ldots,n$
and combining it with \eqref{u:eq:regression} yields
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:finite-row-remainder}
 \mathbb E_{\mu_{v,t}}\sum_j|r_j(S_j)|^2
 \le C_{\eta,K}\Delta^2+C_\eta e^{-cK^2}.
\end{equation}
Consequently, for $s_{ij}=t_i-\sum_{k<j}v_kx_{ik}$ and
$\mathcal R_n=n^{-1/2}(r_j(s_{ij}))$,
$\mathbb E\lVert\mathcal R_n\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\to0$, by first letting $n\to\infty$
at fixed $\eta,K$, then $K\to\infty$ with $\eta$ still fixed.

\@startsection{paragraph}{4}\z@
 {.5\linespacing\@plus.5\linespacing}{.25\linespacing}
 {\normalfont\bfseries}{Coupling errors.}
We construct the coupling one entry at a time. For a fixed residual $s$,
write
\[
 f_{j,s}(x)=\frac{dK_{j,s}}{d\mu}(x)
          =\frac{\mathcal Z_j(s-v_jx)}{A_j(s)},\qquad
 \alpha_{j,s}=\int\min\{f_{j,s},1\}\,\mathrm d\mu.
\]
Define a joint law on $\mathbb F\times\mathbb F$ by
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \pi_{j,s}(\,\mathrm d x,\,\mathrm d y)
 &=\min\{f_{j,s}(x),1\}\mu(\,\mathrm d x)\delta_x(\,\mathrm d y)\\
 &\quad+\frac{(f_{j,s}(x)-1)_+(1-f_{j,s}(y))_+}{1-\alpha_{j,s}}
                   \mu(\,\mathrm d x)\mu(\,\mathrm d y).
 \end{aligned}
\]
Here $\delta_x$ is the point mass at $x$, and the second term is omitted
when $\alpha_{j,s}=1$. The first term, of mass $\alpha_{j,s}$, makes
the two entries equal by sampling their common part. With the remaining
probability, the entries are sampled independently from the two residual
laws. Integrating either coordinate shows that the marginals of
$\pi_{j,s}$ are $K_{j,s}$ and $\mu$. All these kernels are Borel in $s$.

Now generate pairs $(x_{ij},\zeta_{ij})$ in row-major order
$\ell=(i-1)n+j$, and let $\mathcal G_\ell$ be the sigma-algebra generated
by the first $\ell$ pairs, with $\mathcal G_0$ trivial. Given the past,
the residual $s_{ij}=t_i-\sum_{k<j}v_kx_{ik}$ is known; sample the next pair
from $\pi_{j,s_{ij}}$. Thus
\[
 \mathcal L(x_{ij}\mid\mathcal G_{\ell-1})=K_{j,s_{ij}},\qquad
 \mathcal L(\zeta_{ij}\mid\mathcal G_{\ell-1})=\mu.
\]
The first identity reproduces the independent tilted rows, while the
second makes the entire comparator array iid with law $\mu$, by iterated
conditioning. The two arrays may depend on one another. Since $\mu$ is
centered, the centered coupling errors satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:coupling-error}
 e_{ij}:=x_{ij}-\zeta_{ij}-m_j(s_{ij}),\qquad
 \mathbb E(e_{ij}\mid\mathcal G_{\ell-1})=0.
\end{equation}
To bound their conditional variance, observe that the common part of
$\pi_{j,s}$ contributes zero to $|x-y|^2$. On its residual part use
$|x-y|^2\le2|x|^2+2|y|^2$; conditional centering then gives
\[
 \mathbb E(|e_{ij}|^2\mid\mathcal G_{\ell-1})
 \le\mathbb E(|x_{ij}-\zeta_{ij}|^2\mid\mathcal G_{\ell-1})
 \le2\int|x|^2|f_{j,s_{ij}}(x)-1|\,\mathrm d\mu(x).
\]

It remains to estimate this weighted difference of densities.
For integrable $W\ge0$ and $c>0$, $\operatorname{Cov}(W,e^{-cW})\le0$:
average $(W-W')(e^{-cW}-e^{-cW'})\le0$ over independent copies.
Apply this with $W=|s-U_j|^2$. Differentiation and Cauchy--Schwarz give
\[
 |\nabla\log\mathcal Z_j(s)|
 \le\frac\beta{\eta^2}
 \left(\frac{\mathbb E[|s-U_j|^2\varphi(s-U_j)]}{\mathcal Z_j(s)}\right)^{1/2}
 \le\frac\beta{\eta^2}\sqrt{\mathbb E|s-U_j|^2}
 \le C\eta^{-2}(1+|s|).
\]
Hence, with $u=|v_j|(1+|s|)$,
\[
 D(x):=\log\frac{\mathcal Z_j(s-v_jx)}{\mathcal Z_j(s)},\qquad
 |D(x)|\le C\eta^{-2}(u|x|+|v_j|^2|x|^2).
\]
For a sufficiently small $u_0=u_0(\mu,\eta)>0$, the square-exponential moment and
$|e^D-1|\le|D|e^{|D|}$ imply
$\int(1+|x|^2)|e^{D(x)}-1|\,\mathrm d\mu(x)\le Cu$ whenever $u\le u_0$.
Normalizing by $\int e^D\,\mathrm d\mu\ge1-Cu\ge1/2$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{u:eq:weighted-tv}
 \int(1+|x|^2)\left|\frac{dK_{j,s}}{d\mu}(x)-1\right|\,\mathrm d\mu(x)
 \le C|v_j|(1+|s|),\qquad u\le u_0.
\end{equation}
Combining this with the preceding coupling bound gives
\[
 \mathbb E(|e_{ij}|^2\mid\mathcal G_{\ell-1})
 \le C|v_j|(1+|s_{ij}|),\qquad |v_j|(1+|s_{ij}|)\le u_0.
\]
Row-density domination, conditional Jensen applied to
$m_j(s_{ij})=\mathbb E(x_{ij}\mid\mathcal G_{\ell-1})$, and H\"older give
$\mathbb E e^{c|e_{ij}|^2}\le C$ after decreasing $c$.

To pass from these scalar errors to their matrix operator norm, truncate
and apply matrix Freedman. Choose a sufficiently large fixed $C_0$, set
$\Lambda_n=C_0\sqrt{\log n}$, and define
\[
 h_{ij}=e_{ij}\mathbf1_{\{|e_{ij}|\le\Lambda_n\}},
 \qquad \widetilde e_{ij}=h_{ij}
                  -\mathbb E(h_{ij}\mid\mathcal G_{\ell-1}).
\]
Conditional centering decreases variance, and
$e_{ij}-\widetilde e_{ij}$ is the conditionally centered tail.
Thus $|\widetilde e_{ij}|\le2\Lambda_n$ and
\[
 \mathbb E\lVertn^{-1/2}(e_{ij}-\widetilde e_{ij})\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 \le Cn e^{-c\Lambda_n^2}=o(1).
\]
The flatness assumption in Lemma~\ref{u:lem:coupling} and the chosen
truncation level give
\[
 \Delta(1+\Lambda_n)
 \le\frac{L(1+C_0\sqrt{\log n})}{\sqrt n}\longrightarrow0.
\]
Consequently, on $\{\max_{i,j}|s_{ij}|\le\Lambda_n\}$, the condition
$|v_j|(1+|s_{ij}|)\le u_0$ holds for every entry when $n$ is large,
so the preceding conditional variance bound applies throughout.

Now fill the matrix $n^{-1/2}(\widetilde e_{ij})$ in row-major order,
keeping unrevealed entries zero. These partial matrices form a martingale
with respect to $\mathcal G_\ell$. Each increment changes just one entry,
so its operator norm is at most $b_n=2\Lambda_n/\sqrt n$.
The two predictable quadratic variation matrices are diagonal: their
diagonal entries are the row sums and column sums of the conditional
entry variances. Thus, on the same event, their operator norms are bounded by
\[
 \max\left\{
 \max_i\frac1n\sum_j\mathbb E(|\widetilde e_{ij}|^2\mid\mathcal G_{\ell-1}),
 \max_j\frac1n\sum_i\mathbb E(|\widetilde e_{ij}|^2\mid\mathcal G_{\ell-1})
 \right\}
 \le V_n:=C(1+\Lambda_n)\Delta,
\]
where $\ell=(i-1)n+j$ in each summand; we used $|v_j|\le\Delta$.
In particular $V_n\le C\sqrt{\log n/n}$.
The complementary event has probability at most $Cn^2e^{-c\Lambda_n^2}$.
Matrix Freedman in its joint-event form \cite[Corollary 1.3]{Tropp}
therefore gives, for every fixed $u>0$,
\[
 \mathbb P\{\lVertn^{-1/2}(\widetilde e_{ij})\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}>u\}
 \le Cn^2e^{-c\Lambda_n^2}
       +2n\exp\left[-\frac{u^2}{2(V_n+b_nu/3)}\right]\longrightarrow0.
\]
Removing truncation proves
$\lVert\mathcal M_n\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{\mathbb P}0$ for
$\mathcal M_n=n^{-1/2}(e_{ij})$.

\@startsection{paragraph}{4}\z@
 {.5\linespacing\@plus.5\linespacing}{.25\linespacing}
 {\normalfont\bfseries}{Triangular system.}
It remains to solve the coordinate regressions and assemble the
decomposition in Lemma~\ref{u:lem:coupling}: an iid array multiplied by $T$,
a rank-one drift, and a vanishing error. Combining the centered-error
definition \eqref{u:eq:coupling-error} with the regression decomposition
\eqref{u:eq:regression}, and substituting
$s_{ij}=t_i-\sum_{k<j}v_kx_{ik}$, gives
\begin{align*}
 x_{ij}&=\zeta_{ij}+m_j(s_{ij})+e_{ij}\\
 &=\zeta_{ij}+\frac{\bar v_j}{d_j}
       \left(t_i-\sum_{k<j}v_kx_{ik}\right)+e_{ij}+r_j(s_{ij}).
\end{align*}
Moving all terms involving $\bm x_i$ to the left gives a triangular system:
the $j$th equation involves only $x_{i1},\ldots,x_{ij}$.
Let $\mathsf L_{jj}=1$, $\mathsf L_{kj}=v_k\bar v_j/d_j$ for $k<j$,
and zero below the diagonal; define the row vector
$\bm a:=(\bar v_1/d_1,\ldots,\bar v_n/d_n)\in\mathbb F^{1\times n}$.
The bold symbols $\bm x_i,\bm\zeta_i,\bm e_i,\bm r_i\in\mathbb F^{1\times n}$
denote row vectors with components
\[
 (\bm x_i)_j=x_{ij},\quad (\bm\zeta_i)_j=\zeta_{ij},\quad
 (\bm e_i)_j=e_{ij},\quad (\bm r_i)_j=r_{ij}:=r_j(s_{ij}).
\]
Then
\[
 \bm x_i\mathsf L=\bm\zeta_i+t_i\bm a+\bm e_i+\bm r_i.
\]
The matrix $\mathsf L$ is invertible because it is upper triangular with
diagonal entries one. We identify its inverse to recover the specific
$T$ and drift in \eqref{u:eq:coupling}--\eqref{u:eq:T}.
For the candidate $T$ in \eqref{u:eq:T}, the identity
$|v_\ell|^2/(d_\ell d_{\ell+1})=d_{\ell+1}^{-1}-d_\ell^{-1}$ gives
\[
 (\mathsf LT)_{kj}
 =v_k\bar v_j\left(\frac1{d_j}-\frac1{d_{k+1}}
       -\sum_{k<\ell<j}\frac{|v_\ell|^2}{d_\ell d_{\ell+1}}\right)
 =0,\qquad k<j.
\]
The diagonal entries are one and those below the diagonal are zero,
so $\mathsf LT=I$. The same telescoping sum identifies the drift:
\[
 (\bm aT)_j=\bar v_j\left(\frac1{d_j}
             -\sum_{k<j}\frac{|v_k|^2}{d_kd_{k+1}}\right)
 =\frac{\bar v_j}{d_1}=\frac{\bar v_j}{1+\eta^2}.
\]
Thus solving the system gives
\[
 \bm x_i=\bm\zeta_iT+\frac{t_i v^*}{1+\eta^2}+(\bm e_i+\bm r_i)T.
\]
To finish, we check that multiplication by $T$ preserves the smallness
of the two errors. Its explicit entries give
\[
 \lVertT-I\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 =\sum_{k<j}\frac{|v_k|^2|v_j|^2}{d_{k+1}^2}
 \le\eta^{-4}\frac{1-\sum_j|v_j|^4}{2}\le(2\eta^4)^{-1},
\]
which is the bound in \eqref{u:eq:T}; hence
$\lVertT\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\le1+1/(\sqrt2\eta^2)$ for fixed $\eta$.
Stacking the solved rows and dividing by $\sqrt n$ gives
\eqref{u:eq:coupling} with
\[
 E_n=\frac1{\sqrt n}(e_{ij}+r_{ij})_{i,j=1}^n T
    =(\mathcal M_n+\mathcal R_n)T.
\]
Indeed,
the preceding two parts proved that $\lVert\mathcal M_n\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}$ and
$\lVert\mathcal R_n\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}$ tend to zero in probability, so
\[
 \lVertE_n\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}
 \le\bigl(\lVert\mathcal M_n\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}+\lVert\mathcal R_n\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}\bigr)
       \left(1+\frac1{\sqrt2\eta^2}\right)
 \xrightarrow{\mathbb P}0.
\]
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
We reproduce the Schur Jacobian calculation of
\cite[Theorem 5.1 and Lemma 5.2]{Edelman} for completeness.
The real Schur theorem gives diagonal blocks of size one or two.
In a two-dimensional block, choosing axes at $45^\circ$ to eigenvectors
of its symmetric part makes the diagonal entries equal to $x_a$.
The determinant then gives $b_ac_a=y_a^2>0$; a reflection and a
quarter-turn arrange $b_a\ge c_a>0$. For a fixed ordering and simple
spectrum, this fixes the block bases up to signs whenever $b_a>c_a$.
Repeated eigenvalues and the lower-dimensional sets $b_a=c_a$ have
Lebesgue measure zero and may be discarded.

\emph{The Jacobian between blocks.}
Here $d$ denotes the formal first-order differential in the real Schur
coordinates: $dQ$, $dU$, and $dG_n$ are matrices of differential
one-forms, and the usual product rule applies. The Jacobian is the
determinant of the resulting linear map between coordinate differentials.
Set $\Omega=Q^\top dQ$, so $\Omega^\top=-\Omega$. Differentiation gives
\[
 D:=Q^\top(dG_n)Q=dU+\Omega U-U\Omega.
\]
Orthogonal conjugation has Jacobian of absolute value one on matrix
space, so it suffices to compute the Jacobian of this differential.
For a lower block $a>b$, upper triangularity of $U$ gives
\[
 D_{ab}=\Omega_{ab}\mathsf S_b-\mathsf S_a\Omega_{ab}
       +\sum_{c<b}\Omega_{ac}N_{cb}
       -\sum_{c>a}N_{ac}\Omega_{cb}.
\]
The rotation blocks in both sums lie farther below the diagonal than
$(a,b)$. Ordering the pairs by decreasing $a-b$ therefore makes this
linear system block triangular. Its diagonal maps, acting on
$H\in\mathbb R^{d_a\times d_b}$, satisfy
\[
 \mathcal L_{ab}(H)=H\mathsf S_b-\mathsf S_aH,
 \qquad
 |\det\mathcal L_{ab}|=
 \prod_{\lambda\in\operatorname{spec}(\mathsf S_a)}
 \prod_{\lambda'\in\operatorname{spec}(\mathsf S_b)}|\lambda'-\lambda|.
\]
Indeed, diagonalizing the two blocks over $\mathbb C$ makes
$\mathcal L_{ab}$ diagonal on matrix units, with eigenvalues
$\lambda'-\lambda$. Thus all these factors depend only on the spectrum,
not on $N_{ab}$ or $\Delta_a$.

\emph{The Jacobian inside a block.}
After eliminating the rotation variables just treated, a scalar
diagonal block contributes $d\lambda_a$. For a two-dimensional block,
write $\Omega_{aa}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&d\theta_a\\
-d\theta_a&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)$. Its remaining differential is
\[
 d\mathsf S_a+[\Omega_{aa},\mathsf S_a]
 =\begin{pmatrix}
 dx_a+\Delta_a d\theta_a&db_a\\
 -dc_a&dx_a-\Delta_a d\theta_a
 \end{pmatrix},
\]
whose Jacobian with respect to $(x_a,\theta_a,b_a,c_a)$ has absolute
value $2\Delta_a$. Finally, each upper block has differential
$dN_{ab}$ plus already treated rotation terms, and hence contributes
Jacobian one. Relative to Haar volume for $Q$ and Lebesgue measure for
$N_{ab}$, the real $\lambda_a$, and the triples $(x_a,b_a,c_a)$, the
full Jacobian is therefore, up to a constant depending only on the
block pattern,
\[
 J=\prod_{a>b}|\det\mathcal L_{ab}|
       \prod_{a:d_a=2}2\Delta_a.
\]
The sign convention restricts $Q$ to a fixed domain independent of
the other coordinates; its volume is another constant.

\emph{Conditional density and moments.}
For each complex block, the change from $(b,c)$ to
$(y,\Delta)=(\sqrt{bc},b-c)$ satisfies
\[
 \left|\frac{\partial(b,c)}{\partial(y,\Delta)}\right|
 =\frac{2y}{\sqrt{\Delta^2+4y^2}}.
\]
This follows by differentiating $bc=y^2$ and $b-c=\Delta$, using
$b+c=\sqrt{\Delta^2+4y^2}$. Moreover,
\[
 \lVertG_n\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2=\lVertU\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 =\sum_{a:d_a=1}\lambda_a^2
  +\sum_{a:d_a=2}(2x_a^2+2y_a^2+\Delta_a^2)
  +\sum_{a<b}\lVertN_{ab}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2.
\]
Multiply the Jacobian by the Gaussian density
$\exp(-n\lVertG_n\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2/2)$ and integrate out $Q$; this is the
density factorization in \cite[Section 6.1, equation (10)]{Edelman},
with entries scaled to variance $1/n$.
After fixing the spectrum, all factors depending only on eigenvalues
enter the normalizing constant. The remaining density is proportional to
\[
 \prod_{a<b}e^{-n\lVertN_{ab}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2/2}\,\mathrm d N_{ab}
 \prod_{a:d_a=2}
 \frac{\Delta_a e^{-n\Delta_a^2/2}}
      {\sqrt{\Delta_a^2+4y_a^2}}\,\mathrm d\Delta_a,
 \qquad \Delta_a>0.
\]
The domain is a Cartesian product of Euclidean spaces and positive
half-lines. Normalizing each factor proves the stated
densities and all conditional independence claims.

For the second moments, all expectations below are conditional on the
spectrum. Integrate the derivative of
$t^2(t^2+4y_a^2)^{-1/2}e^{-nt^2/2}$ over $(0,\infty)$.
The boundary terms vanish; dividing by $Z_{n,y_a}$ gives
\[
 n\mathbb E\Delta_a^2
 =2-\mathbb E\frac{\Delta_a^2}{\Delta_a^2+4y_a^2}\le2.
\]
Finally, $\mathbb E\lVertN_{ab}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2=d_ad_b/n$ by summing the
variances of its scalar Gaussian entries. The argument applies to every
block pattern, including odd dimensions.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
