<!-- algorithms-1: body_md -->
\label{l:thm:ARN}

Given the core \(A\in\mathbb R^{d\times d}\), the independent starter
\(\mathbf s\in\mathbb R^d\), and \(k<d\), set
\(\mathbf v_1=\mathbf s/\|\mathbf s\|\) when \(\mathbf s\ne0\), and \(\mathbf v_1=e_1\)
otherwise. For \(j=1,\ldots,k\), observe \(A\mathbf v_j\) and set \[
 P_j=\sum_{i=1}^j\mathbf v_i\mathbf v_i^T,\qquad
 \mathbf r_j=(\mathrm{Id}_d-P_j)A\mathbf v_j,\qquad R_j=\|\mathbf r_j\|.
\] Set \[
 \mathbf v_{j+1}=\begin{cases}
 \displaystyle\frac{\mathbf r_j}{R_j},&R_j>0,\\[6pt]
 \displaystyle\frac{(\mathrm{Id}_d-P_j)e_{i_j}}
                    {\|(\mathrm{Id}_d-P_j)e_{i_j}\|},&R_j=0,
 \end{cases}
 \qquad
 i_j=\min\{i\in[d]:(\mathrm{Id}_d-P_j)e_i\ne0\}.
\] The \(R_j=0\) branch is the deterministic
\emph{orthogonal completion} rule. The index \(i_j\) exists since
\(j<d\). This branch adds one orthogonal unit direction while retaining
all previous directions and responses; \(R_j\) remains zero and no new
starter is sampled. Continue with the next step. Record \[
 V=[\mathbf v_1,\ldots,\mathbf v_k],\qquad V^TV=\mathrm{Id}_k,
\] the responses \(A\mathbf v_1,\ldots,A\mathbf v_k\), and the residual norms. We use
this deterministic orthogonal completion rule below as well.
<!-- end algorithms-1 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{l:thm:ML}

For each \(0<\varepsilon\le1\), there exist
\(\beta_{\mu,\varepsilon}>0\), \(c_{\mathrm{link}}(\mu,\varepsilon)>0\)
and \(d_0(\mu,\varepsilon)\) such that, for every \(d\ge d_0\), one can
choose an integer \(m\ge1\) satisfying \begin{equation}
2m<d,\qquad \beta_{\mu,\varepsilon}d\le 2m,
 \qquad 256m\le\varepsilon d,
 \label{l:eq:M.7}
\end{equation} for which \begin{equation}
\mathbb P_{A,\mathbf s}\{W_{2m}(A,\mathbf s)\le(1-\varepsilon/4)^{m^2}\}
 \le e^{-c_{\mathrm{link}}(\mu,\varepsilon)d^2}.
 \label{l:eq:M.8}
\end{equation} Here \(A\) and \(\mathbf s\) have their actual independent iid laws
\(\mu\). The main proof uses only the even query length \(2m\), for
which \(M_{2m}=m^2\). The general parameter \(k\) in \eqref{l:eq:M.6} is retained
only for the reusable link and stopping estimates below; their
application here is \(k=2m\). The estimate is unconditional in the core
and starter; the border has not been sampled in its proof.
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{l:thm:MB}

Fix \(0\le r<R\le1\), \(a>0\), and an integer \(m\ge1\) with \(2m<d\)
and \((r/R)^m\le1/2\). Fix a deterministic core
\(A\in\mathbb R^{d\times d}\) and starter \(\mathbf s\in\mathbb R^d\)
with \(W_{2m}(A,\mathbf s)\ge a^{m^2}\), where \(d=n-1\). The
probability \(\mathbb P_{\mathbf b}\) below is over precisely the
\(2d+1=2n-1\) independent scalar entries \[
 \mathbf b=(\xi_{11},x_1,\ldots,x_d,y_1,\ldots,y_d)\sim\mu^{\otimes(2d+1)}.
\] Then \begin{equation}
\mathbb P_{\mathbf b}\left\{
  \rho\begin{pmatrix}\xi_{11}&\mathbf y^T\\\mathbf x&A\end{pmatrix}\le r\sqrt n
 \right\}
 \le\exp\left\{
    C_{\mathrm{border}}(f)n+\frac{m^2}{4}\log(R/a)
   \right\}.
 \label{l:eq:M.10}
\end{equation} Explicitly, for a border event \(E\), \[
 \mathbb P_{\mathbf b}(E)=
 \int_{\mathbb R^{2d+1}}\mathbf1_E(\xi_{11},\mathbf x,\mathbf y)\,
 f(\xi_{11})\prod_{i=1}^d f(x_i)f(y_i)\,d\xi_{11}\,d\mathbf x\,d\mathbf y.
\] There is no averaging over \(A\) or \(\mathbf s\), no random change
of basis, and no Gaussian border in this statement. A Gaussian border is
introduced only as a comparison device in its proof.
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- example-1: body_md -->
\label{l:thm:NILCORE}

Consider the normalized companion matrix \[
 C_n=\begin{pmatrix}
 \xi_1&\xi_2&\cdots&\xi_n\\
 1&0&\cdots&0\\
 &\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
 0&&1&0
 \end{pmatrix},\qquad \xi_j\ \text{independent uniform on }[-1,1].
\] Its core is the nilpotent shift \(S_de_j=e_{j+1}\) for \(1\le j<d\), with \(S_de_d=0\). In
\eqref{l:eq:M.3} this corresponds to \(A=\sqrt nS_d\); with starter \(e_1\),
Arnoldi gives \(R_j=\sqrt n\) and \(W_{2m}=1\), although \(\rho(A)=0\).
Since \(\det(z\mathrm{Id}_n-C_n)=z^n-\sum_{j=1}^n\xi_jz^{n-j}\), Vieta's
formula gives \(|\xi_j|\le\binom njr^j\) whenever all roots have modulus
at most \(r\). Independence therefore yields \[
 \mathbb P\{\rho(C_n)\le r\}
 \le\prod_{j=1}^n\min\{1,\tbinom njr^j\}
 \le2^{n^2}r^{n(n+1)/2}\le2^{-n^2/2}\quad(r=1/8).
\] Thus \(n\) variables can incur an \(n^2\)-scale cost through
accumulated precision. This illustrates the mechanism; Proposition \ref{l:thm:MB}
uses a random border on both sides and a different normalization. For a
general good core, two border integrations replace this
independent-coefficient argument.
<!-- end example-1 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
Fix \(r<1\), set
\(\varepsilon=1-r\), \(a=1-\varepsilon/4\), and \(R=(r+a)/2\). Then
\(r<R<a<1\). Proposition \ref{l:thm:ML} supplies \(m\ge\beta_{\mu,\varepsilon}d/2\),
so \((r/R)^m\le1/2\) for large \(d\). On \(W_{2m}>a^{m^2}\), Proposition
\ref{l:thm:MB} gives \[
 \mathbb P_{\mathbf b}\{\rho(\mathsf X_n)\le r\sqrt n\}
 \le e^{C_{\mathrm{border}}(f)n-\delta_rm^2}
 \le e^{-c_{\mathrm{good}}d^2},\qquad
 \delta_r=\tfrac14\log(a/R)>0,
\] for example with
\(c_{\mathrm{good}}=\delta_r\beta_{\mu,\varepsilon}^2/8\) and a
sufficiently large dimension threshold. The starter does not enter the
spectral event. Averaging over \((A,\mathbf s)\), using the trivial
bound one on the bad-core event, gives \[
 \mathbb P\{\rho(\mathsf X_n)\le r\sqrt n\}
 \le e^{-c_{\mathrm{link}}d^2}+e^{-c_{\mathrm{good}}d^2}.
\] Since \(d=n-1\), this proves \eqref{l:eq:M.2} after adjusting the rate and
threshold.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- fact-2: body_md -->
\label{l:thm:HESS}

The matrix \(Q\) is orthogonal, and the transformed core \begin{equation}
B=(Q^TAQ)^T/\sqrt n
 \label{l:eq:M.4}
\end{equation} is lower Hessenberg, with \[
 B_{ij}=0\quad(j>i+1),\qquad
 B_{j,j+1}=R_j/\sqrt n\quad(1\le j<d).
\]
<!-- end fact-2 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
The construction makes \(\mathbf v_1,\ldots,\mathbf v_d\) orthonormal. For \(j<d\), \[
 A\mathbf v_j=\sum_{i=1}^j\langle \mathbf v_i,A\mathbf v_j\rangle \mathbf v_i+R_j\mathbf v_{j+1}.
\] This identity also holds when \(R_j=0\), since then \(A\mathbf v_j=P_jA\mathbf v_j\).
Thus \(Q^TAQ\) is upper Hessenberg and its \((j+1,j)\)-entry is \(R_j\).
Transposing and dividing by \(\sqrt n\) proves both claims.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{l:lem:gaussian-border}
Let \(B\in\mathbb R^{d\times d}\) be a fixed lower-Hessenberg matrix,
\(n=d+1\), and let \(T\) have the independent Gaussian border in
\eqref{l:eq:D.3}, with \(\kappa>0\). For every integer \(m\ge1\) with
\(2m<d\) and every pair \(0\le r<R\le1\) with \((r/R)^m\le1/2\), set
\[
 W_{2m}=\prod_{j=1}^{2m-1}|B_{j,j+1}|^{\min(j,2m-j)}.
\]
If \(W_{2m}>0\), then, for a constant \(C_{\mathrm G}(\kappa)\)
independent of \(B,n,m,r,R\),
\begin{equation}\label{l:eq:gaussian-border}
 \mathbb P_\kappa\{\rho(T)\le r\}
 \le e^{C_{\mathrm G}(\kappa)n}
       \left(\frac{R^{m^2}}{W_{2m}}\right)^{1/2}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
The Hessenberg core in \eqref{l:eq:M.4} has
\(B_{j,j+1}=R_j/\sqrt n\), so its \(W_{2m}\) is exactly the Arnoldi
link product in the hypothesis. Substituting \(W_{2m}\ge a^{m^2}\)
in Lemma~\ref{l:lem:gaussian-border} gives
\begin{equation}\label{l:eq:D.15}
 \mathbb P_\kappa\{\rho(T)\le r\}
 \le\exp\{C_{\mathrm G}(\kappa)n+(m^2/2)\log(R/a)\}.
\end{equation}
The transformation \eqref{l:eq:M.5} preserves the normalized spectrum.
Applying the border comparison \eqref{l:eq:D.2} therefore yields
\[
\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb P_{\mathbf b}\{\rho(\mathsf X_n)\le r\sqrt n\}
 &\le e^{J_fn}\bigl[\mathbb P_\kappa\{\rho(T)\le r\}\bigr]^{1/2}\\
 &\le\exp\left\{\left(J_f+\tfrac12C_{\mathrm G}(\kappa)\right)n
                   +\frac{m^2}{4}\log(R/a)\right\}.
\end{aligned}
\]
This proves \eqref{l:eq:M.10}, with
\(C_{\mathrm{border}}(f)=J_f+C_{\mathrm G}(\kappa)/2\).
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label{l:thm:DN}
Let \(s,p\) be nonnegative integers with \(m=s+2p\ge1\), and let
\[
 \mathbf Z=(X_1,\ldots,X_s,A_1,B_1,\ldots,A_p,B_p)\in\mathbb R^m
\]
be a real Gaussian vector with arbitrary mean and positive definite
covariance \(\Gamma\). Its coordinates may be correlated. Form the
\(m\) real or conjugate-paired values
\[
 \mathbf Y=(X_1,\ldots,X_s,
 A_1+iB_1,A_1-iB_1,\ldots,A_p+iB_p,A_p-iB_p).
\]
There is a universal \(C_*\ge\sqrt2\) such that:

\smallskip\noindent
\textnormal{(i) Product estimate (used for the row integration).}
\begin{equation}\label{l:eq:D.5}
 \mathbb E\prod_{h=1}^m|Y_h|^{-1/2}
 =\mathbb E\left[
   \prod_{j=1}^s|X_j|^{-1/2}
   \prod_{j=1}^p(A_j^2+B_j^2)^{-1/2}\right]
 \le C_*^m(\det\Gamma)^{-1/4}.
\end{equation}

\smallskip\noindent
\textnormal{(ii) Shifted determinant estimate (used for the column integration).}
For \(\mathbf Y\) above, define the real block-diagonal matrix
\[
 \mathcal D(\mathbf Z)=\operatorname{diag}
 \left(X_1,\ldots,X_s,
 \begin{pmatrix}A_1&-B_1\\B_1&A_1\end{pmatrix},\ldots,
 \begin{pmatrix}A_p&-B_p\\B_p&A_p\end{pmatrix}\right).
\]
Denote the displayed two-dimensional blocks by \(\mathcal M(A_j,B_j)\).
Each represents multiplication by \(A_j+iB_j\)
in real coordinates:
\[
 (A_j+iB_j)(u+iv)
 =(A_ju-B_jv)+i(B_ju+A_jv).
\]
Equivalently, \(\mathcal D(\mathbf Z)\) is the real-coordinate
representation of \(\operatorname{diag}(\mathbf Y)\) acting on
vectors with the same real/conjugate-pair pattern. For every fixed
\(D\in\mathbb R^{m\times m}\),
\begin{equation}\label{l:eq:D.4}
 \mathbb E|\det(\mathcal D(\mathbf Z)+D)|^{-1/2}
 \le C_*^m(\det\Gamma)^{-1/4}.
\end{equation}
The constant is uniform in the Gaussian mean and in \(D\).
Taking \(D=0\) recovers \textnormal{(i)}, since
\(\det\mathcal D(\mathbf Z)=\prod_{j=1}^sX_j
\prod_{j=1}^p(A_j^2+B_j^2)\).
<!-- end lemma-11 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
We prove \textnormal{(ii)} first. Let \(G\sim N(\mu_G,v)\), with
\(v>0\). For every fixed shift \(d_{\mathrm{sc}}\in\mathbb R\)
and every \(t>0\), the Gaussian density bound gives
\[
 \mathbb P\{|G+d_{\mathrm{sc}}|<t\}
 \le\min\left\{1,\frac{2t}{\sqrt{2\pi v}}\right\},
\]
uniformly in \(\mu_G\) and \(d_{\mathrm{sc}}\), since the event is
an interval of length \(2t\) and the density is at most
\((2\pi v)^{-1/2}\). For a single pair and a fixed real
\(2\times2\) shift \(D\), completing squares gives
\[
 \det(\mathcal M(x,y)+D)
 =(x+a_D)^2+(y+b_D)^2-c_D,\qquad c_D\ge0,
\]
where \(a_D,b_D,c_D\) depend only on \(D\). For \(t>0\), the set
\[
 \mathcal A_t=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb R^2:
                  |\det(\mathcal M(x,y)+D)|<t\}
\]
is an annulus centered at \((-a_D,-b_D)\), or a disk if the inner
radius vanishes. Its squared radii are \(c_D+t\) and
\(\max\{0,c_D-t\}\), so
\[
 \operatorname{area}(\mathcal A_t)
 =\pi\bigl(c_D+t-\max\{0,c_D-t\}\bigr)\le2\pi t.
\]
If \((G_1,G_2)\) is Gaussian with covariance \(S>0\), its density
is at most \((2\pi\sqrt{\det S})^{-1}\). Thus, uniformly in its mean
and in \(D\),
\[
 \mathbb P\{|\det(\mathcal M(G_1,G_2)+D)|<t\}
 \le\min\left\{1,\frac{t}{\sqrt{\det S}}\right\}.
\]
Integrating these bounds by \(\mathbb E X^{-1/2}=\tfrac12\int_0^\infty
 t^{-3/2}\mathbb P\{X<t\}\,dt\) gives the one-block estimates, with
factors \(v^{-1/4}\) and \((\det S)^{-1/4}\).

For more than one block, write
\(\mathbf Z=(\mathbf Z_1,\mathbf Z_{\mathrm{rem}})\), where
\(\mathbf Z_1\in\mathbb R^q\), \(q\in\{1,2\}\), is the first
scalar or pair block. Partition the fixed shift accordingly:
\[
 \mathcal D(\mathbf Z)+D
 =\begin{pmatrix}
   \mathcal D_1(\mathbf Z_1)+D_{11}&D_{12}\\
   D_{21}&\mathsf C(\mathbf Z_{\mathrm{rem}})
  \end{pmatrix},\qquad
 \mathsf C(\mathbf Z_{\mathrm{rem}})
 =\mathcal D_{\mathrm{rem}}(\mathbf Z_{\mathrm{rem}})+D_{22}.
\]
Here \(\mathcal D_1\) is the first block and
\(\mathcal D_{\mathrm{rem}}\) contains the remaining blocks.
The polynomial \(\det\mathsf C\) is not identically zero: its
highest-degree part is \(\det\mathcal D_{\mathrm{rem}}\), the product
of the remaining scalar coordinates and the pair factors
\(A_j^2+B_j^2\). Since \(\mathbf Z_{\mathrm{rem}}\) has a
nondegenerate Gaussian density, \(\mathsf C\) is invertible almost
surely.

Fix \(\mathbf Z_{\mathrm{rem}}\) where \(\mathsf C\) is invertible.
The Schur complement identity \cite[Section 0.8.5]{HJ2013} is
\[
 \det(\mathcal D(\mathbf Z)+D)
 =\det\mathsf C\,
   \det\bigl(\mathcal D_1(\mathbf Z_1)+\Delta\bigr),
 \qquad \Delta=D_{11}-D_{12}\mathsf C^{-1}D_{21}.
\]
Thus \(\Delta\) is a fixed real shift under this conditioning.
Writing the Gaussian covariance in the same partition,
\[
 \Gamma=\begin{pmatrix}
  \Gamma_{11}&\Gamma_{1,\mathrm{rem}}\\
  \Gamma_{\mathrm{rem},1}&\Gamma_{\mathrm{rem}}
 \end{pmatrix},\qquad
 S=\Gamma_{11}-\Gamma_{1,\mathrm{rem}}
       \Gamma_{\mathrm{rem}}^{-1}\Gamma_{\mathrm{rem},1}>0,
\]
the conditional law of \(\mathbf Z_1\) is Gaussian with covariance
\(S\). Its mean depends on \(\mathbf Z_{\mathrm{rem}}\), but the
one-block estimate is uniform in that mean and in \(\Delta\). Therefore
\[
 \mathbb E\bigl[|\det(\mathcal D(\mathbf Z)+D)|^{-1/2}
                  \mid\mathbf Z_{\mathrm{rem}}\bigr]
 \le C_*^q(\det S)^{-1/4}|\det\mathsf C|^{-1/2}.
\]
Now average over \(\mathbf Z_{\mathrm{rem}}\) and apply induction to
its marginal Gaussian law, whose covariance is \(\Gamma_{\mathrm{rem}}\),
and to the fixed shift \(D_{22}\). This gives
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E|\det(\mathcal D(\mathbf Z)+D)|^{-1/2}
 &\le C_*^q(\det S)^{-1/4}\,
             \mathbb E|\det\mathsf C|^{-1/2}\\
 &\le C_*^m(\det S)^{-1/4}
             (\det\Gamma_{\mathrm{rem}})^{-1/4}
  = C_*^m(\det\Gamma)^{-1/4},
 \end{aligned}
\]
where the last equality uses
\(\det\Gamma=\det S\det\Gamma_{\mathrm{rem}}\).
The one-block bounds supply the base of this induction, proving
\eqref{l:eq:D.4}.
Setting \(D=0\) then gives \eqref{l:eq:D.5} by the determinant identity
in the statement.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label{l:lem:hessenberg-cofactor}
For every \(z\in\mathbb C\) and \(1\le j\le\ell\le d\),
\begin{equation}
\operatorname{adj}(z\mathrm{Id}_d-B)_{j\ell}
 =\left(\prod_{i=j}^{\ell-1}a_i\right)
       \theta_{j-1}(z)\varphi_{\ell+1}(z),\qquad j\le\ell.
 \label{l:eq:D.6}
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
Let \(\mathsf C_{j,\ell}(z)\) be the matrix obtained from
\(z\mathrm{Id}_d-B\) by deleting row \(\ell\) and column \(j\), retaining
the original order. Partition its rows and columns respectively as
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \text{rows:}\quad &[j-1],\quad [j,\ell-1],\quad [\ell+1,d],\\
 \text{columns:}\quad &[j-1],\quad [j+1,\ell],\quad [\ell+1,d].
 \end{aligned}
\]
Because \((z\mathrm{Id}_d-B)_{ab}=0\) when \(b>a+1\), the blocks
above the diagonal vanish. Thus
\[
 \mathsf C_{j,\ell}(z)=
 \begin{pmatrix}
  \mathsf C_{\mathrm{pre}}(z)&0&0\\
  *&\mathsf C_{\mathrm{mid}}(z)&0\\
  *&*&\mathsf C_{\mathrm{suf}}(z)
 \end{pmatrix},\qquad
 \mathsf C_{\mathrm{mid}}(z)
   =(z\mathrm{Id}_d-B)_{[j,\ell-1],[j+1,\ell]}.
\]
The prefix and suffix blocks are the principal blocks on \([j-1]\)
and \([\ell+1,d]\), with determinants \(\theta_{j-1}(z)\) and
\(\varphi_{\ell+1}(z)\). The middle block is lower triangular:
its entry in relative row \(p\), column \(q\) comes from original
row \(j+p-1\), column \(j+q\), so it vanishes for \(q>p\).
Its diagonal is \(-a_j,\ldots,-a_{\ell-1}\), hence
\[
 \det\mathsf C_{\mathrm{mid}}(z)
   =(-1)^{\ell-j}\prod_{i=j}^{\ell-1}a_i.
\]
The adjugate uses the transposed cofactor, so block triangularity gives
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \operatorname{adj}(z\mathrm{Id}_d-B)_{j\ell}
  &=(-1)^{j+\ell}\det\mathsf C_{j,\ell}(z)\\
  &=(-1)^{j+\ell+\ell-j}
      \theta_{j-1}(z)\left(\prod_{i=j}^{\ell-1}a_i\right)
      \varphi_{\ell+1}(z)\\
  &=\left(\prod_{i=j}^{\ell-1}a_i\right)
      \theta_{j-1}(z)\varphi_{\ell+1}(z).
 \end{aligned}
\]
Here the sign is \((-1)^{2\ell}=1\). Empty blocks have determinant
one, and empty products equal one, so the calculation also covers
\(j=\ell\), \(j=1\) and \(\ell=d\).
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{l:thm:EVALMAT}
Let \(w>0\), and let \(z_1,\ldots,z_m\) be all the distinct roots of
\(z^m=w\), ordered with the real roots first and each nonreal root
adjacent to its complex conjugate. The two \(m\times m\) evaluation
matrices are \begin{equation}
(E_{\mathrm{pre}})_{hj}=s_j\theta_{j-1}(z_h)\quad(1\le j\le m),
 \qquad (E_{\mathrm{suf}})_{h\ell}=s_\ell^{-1}\varphi_{\ell+1}(z_h)
                    \quad(m+1\le\ell\le2m).
 \label{l:eq:D.7}
\end{equation} The columns of \(E_{\mathrm{suf}}\) retain their increasing original labels.
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- lemma-13: body_md -->
\label{l:lem:evaluation-determinant}
Fix the real lower-Hessenberg core \(B\) and an integer \(m\) with
\(2m<d\) and \(W_{2m}>0\), and write \(d_{\mathrm{tail}}=d-2m\).

\textup{(i)} There is a unique real monic polynomial \(Q_B\) of degree
\(d_{\mathrm{tail}}\), depending only on \(B\) and \(m\), such that,
for every \(w>0\) and its \(m\)-th roots \(z_1,\ldots,z_m\),
\[
 \frac{\det[\varphi_{2m-j+1}(z_h)]_{\substack{1\le h\le m\\0\le j<m}}}
      {\det[z_h^j]_{\substack{1\le h\le m\\0\le j<m}}}
   =(-1)^{d_{\mathrm{tail}}(m-1)}Q_B(w).
\]
For the matrices in Definition~\ref{l:thm:EVALMAT}, this gives
\begin{equation}
 |\det E_{\mathrm{pre}}\det E_{\mathrm{suf}}|
   =m^m w^{m-1}W_{2m}|Q_B(w)|.
 \label{l:eq:D.8}
\end{equation}

\textup{(ii)} For every \(0<R\le1\), there exists
\(w\in[R^m/2,R^m]\) such that
\begin{equation}
 |Q_B(w)|\ge\left(\frac{R^m}{4\mathrm e}\right)^{d_{\mathrm{tail}}}.
 \label{l:eq:D.9}
\end{equation}
The point \(w\) can be chosen to depend only on \(B,m,R\).
<!-- end lemma-13 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
For \textup{(i)}, first compute the prefix determinant. Write
\[
 \mathcal V=\det[z_h^{j-1}]_{h,j=1}^m.
\]
Since \(\theta_{j-1}\) is monic of degree \(j-1\), write
\[
 \theta_{j-1}(z)=z^{j-1}+\sum_{q=0}^{j-2}c_{q,j}z^q,
 \qquad 1\le j\le m,
\]
where the sum is empty for \(j=1\). In the matrix
\([\theta_{j-1}(z_h)]_{h,j=1}^m\), the first column is \(1\).
Proceeding through columns \(j=2,\ldots,m\), subtract the linear
combination \(\sum_{q=0}^{j-2}c_{q,j}(z_h^q)_{h=1}^m\) of the
already reduced columns \(1,\ldots,j-1\) from column \(j\).
This leaves column \((z_h^{j-1})_{h=1}^m\) and does not change the
determinant. Factoring out the column weights in
Definition~\ref{l:thm:EVALMAT} therefore gives
\[
 \det[\theta_{j-1}(z_h)]_{h,j=1}^m=\mathcal V,
 \qquad
 \det E_{\mathrm{pre}}
   =\left(\prod_{j=1}^m s_j\right)\mathcal V.
\]
Thus the prefix contributes precisely one Vandermonde factor.

Next compute the suffix determinant. Reverse the suffix family, writing
\(p_j=\varphi_{2m-j+1}\), monic of degree
\(d_{\mathrm{tail}}+j\) for \(0\le j<m\). The quotient
\[
 S(z_1,\ldots,z_m)
   =\frac{\det[p_j(z_h)]_{\substack{1\le h\le m\\0\le j<m}}}
          {\det[z_h^j]_{\substack{1\le h\le m\\0\le j<m}}}
\]
is a real polynomial, as follows. If \(z_h=z_k\) for \(h<k\),
the numerator has two equal rows and vanishes, so \(z_k-z_h\) divides
it. These are distinct irreducible linear factors in
\(\mathbb R[z_1,\ldots,z_m]\), hence their product divides the numerator.
That product is precisely the denominator:
\[
 \det[z_h^j]_{\substack{1\le h\le m\\0\le j<m}}
    =\prod_{1\le h<k\le m}(z_k-z_h).
\]
Thus the quotient \(S\) is a polynomial. Exchanging any two variables
changes the sign of both determinants, so \(S\) is symmetric.
Its total degree is at most \(md_{\mathrm{tail}}\), and its highest
homogeneous part is \(\prod_{h=1}^m z_h^{d_{\mathrm{tail}}}\).
To show that evaluating \(S\) at the roots of \(z^m-w\) produces
an ordinary polynomial in \(w\), we use the fundamental theorem of
symmetric polynomials. It gives a real polynomial \(P\) such that
\(S=P(e_1,\ldots,e_m)\), where
\[
 e_j(z_1,\ldots,z_m)
   =\sum_{1\le h_1<\cdots<h_j\le m}z_{h_1}\cdots z_{h_j},
 \qquad 1\le j\le m.
\]
Comparing coefficients in \(\prod_{h=1}^m(t-z_h)=t^m-w\) gives
\[
 e_j(z_1,\ldots,z_m)=0\quad(1\le j<m),\qquad
 e_m(z_1,\ldots,z_m)=\prod_{h=1}^m z_h=(-1)^{m-1}w.
\]
Consequently,
\[
 S(z_1,\ldots,z_m)=P\bigl(0,\ldots,0,(-1)^{m-1}w\bigr),
\]
which is a real polynomial in \(w\). Only powers of \(e_m\) survive;
since \(e_m\) has total degree \(m\), the degree in \(w\) is at most
\(d_{\mathrm{tail}}\). The highest homogeneous part of \(S\) gives
the leading coefficient \((-1)^{d_{\mathrm{tail}}(m-1)}\).
This defines the monic \(Q_B\); its coefficients depend only on \(B,m\),
and the displayed identity for every \(w>0\) proves uniqueness.

Factoring out the suffix column weights and using that column
reversal changes only the sign, we obtain
\[
 |\det E_{\mathrm{suf}}|
   =\left(\prod_{\ell=m+1}^{2m}|s_\ell|^{-1}\right)
       |\mathcal V|\,|Q_B(w)|.
\]
Combining this with the prefix calculation gives
\[
 |\det E_{\mathrm{pre}}\det E_{\mathrm{suf}}|
   =\frac{\prod_{j=1}^m|s_j|}{\prod_{\ell=m+1}^{2m}|s_\ell|}
       |\mathcal V|^2|Q_B(w)|.
\]
Finally,
\[
 |\mathcal V|^2=m^mw^{m-1},\qquad
 \frac{\prod_{j=1}^m|s_j|}{\prod_{\ell=m+1}^{2m}|s_\ell|}
   =\prod_{i=1}^{2m-1}|a_i|^{\min(i,2m-i)}=W_{2m},
\]
which proves \eqref{l:eq:D.8}.

For \textup{(ii)}, define the grid
\[
 \omega_j=\frac{R^m}{2}+\frac{jR^m}{2d_{\mathrm{tail}}},
 \qquad j\in\{0,\ldots,d_{\mathrm{tail}}\},
\]
and choose
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 j_*&=\min\left\{j\in\{0,\ldots,d_{\mathrm{tail}}\}:
       |Q_B(\omega_j)|=\max_{0\le\ell\le d_{\mathrm{tail}}}
                         |Q_B(\omega_\ell)|\right\},\\
 w&=\omega_{j_*}\in[R^m/2,R^m].
 \end{aligned}
\]
This choice depends only on \(B,m,R\). The grid spacing is
\(\Delta=R^m/(2d_{\mathrm{tail}})\). Taking the
\(d_{\mathrm{tail}}\)-th forward difference of the monic polynomial
\(Q_B\), with step \(\Delta\), gives
\[
 d_{\mathrm{tail}}!\Delta^{d_{\mathrm{tail}}}
 =\left|\sum_{j=0}^{d_{\mathrm{tail}}}
       (-1)^{d_{\mathrm{tail}}-j}\binom{d_{\mathrm{tail}}}{j}
       Q_B(\omega_j)\right|
 \le 2^{d_{\mathrm{tail}}}|Q_B(w)|.
\]
Using \(d_{\mathrm{tail}}!\ge(d_{\mathrm{tail}}/\mathrm e)^{d_{\mathrm{tail}}}\)
proves \eqref{l:eq:D.9}. Since \(w>0\), \(W_{2m}>0\) and
\(Q_B(w)\ne0\), \eqref{l:eq:D.8} makes both matrices invertible.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- lemma-14: body_md -->
\label{l:lem:border-negative-moment}
For the fixed core, nodes and evaluation matrices above,
\begin{equation}\label{l:eq:border-negative-moment}
 \mathbb E_\kappa\prod_{h=1}^m|P_T(z_h)|^{-1/2}
 \le C^m(n/\kappa)^{m/2}
       |\det E_{\mathrm{pre}}\det E_{\mathrm{suf}}|^{-1/2}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-14 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
Recall the scalar quantity
\[
 Y=\prod_{h=1}^m|P_T(z_h)|,\qquad
 Y^{-1/2}=\prod_{h=1}^m|P_T(z_h)|^{-1/2},
\]
whose negative half-power is the integrand in
\eqref{l:eq:border-negative-moment}.
Throughout these two integrations, replace each conjugate pair of
values by its real and imaginary parts, as in Lemma~\ref{l:thm:DN}.
For a matrix \(K\) with real rows and conjugate-paired rows, write
\(K_{\mathbb R}\) for the real matrix obtained by making the same
replacement in its rows. Each pair replacement has determinant of
absolute value \(1/2\), so, with \(b_{\mathrm{pair}}\le m/2\) pairs,
\[
 |\det K_{\mathbb R}|=2^{-b_{\mathrm{pair}}}|\det K|.
\]
In particular, if \(\mathbf v\sim N(0,(\kappa/n)\mathrm{Id}_m)\),
then \(K_{\mathbb R}\mathbf v\) has covariance determinant
\[
 2^{-2b_{\mathrm{pair}}}(\kappa/n)^m|\det K|^2.
\]
Below \(C\) is universal and may increase between lines.

\emph{Integrate the row.} Fix \(g_{\mathrm b}\), the entire border
column \(\mathbf u_{\mathrm b}\), and the row coordinates
\((\mathbf x_{\mathrm b})_j\) for \(m<j\le d\). Integrate only over
\(\mathbf x_*=(\mathbf x_{\mathrm b})_{[m]}\), the first \(m\)
coordinates of the border row. The evaluation vector is
\((P_T(z_h))_{h=1}^m=\mathbf b_{\mathrm{shift}}-H\mathbf x_*\), where
\[
 H_{hj}=\sum_{\ell=1}^d
 \operatorname{adj}(z_h\mathrm{Id}_d-B)_{j\ell}(\mathbf u_{\mathrm b})_\ell,
 \qquad 1\le h,j\le m,
\]
and the shift vector is explicitly
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 (\mathbf b_{\mathrm{shift}})_h
  &=(z_h-g_{\mathrm b})\det(z_h\mathrm{Id}_d-B)\\
  &\quad-\sum_{j=m+1}^d(\mathbf x_{\mathrm b})_j
       \sum_{\ell=1}^d
       \operatorname{adj}(z_h\mathrm{Id}_d-B)_{j\ell}
       (\mathbf u_{\mathrm b})_\ell,
       \qquad 1\le h\le m.
 \end{aligned}
\]
It collects all terms independent of \(\mathbf x_*\) and is fixed
during the row integration. Since \(\mathbf x_*\) is centered, it is
also the conditional mean of the evaluation vector.
The real coordinate vector of \((P_T(z_h))_{h=1}^m\) has covariance
determinant \(2^{-2b_{\mathrm{pair}}}(\kappa/n)^m|\det H|^2\).
Applying the product bound \eqref{l:eq:D.5} to the conjugate-paired
values \((P_T(z_h))_{h=1}^m\), whose absolute product is \(Y\), and
absorbing the factor
\(2^{b_{\mathrm{pair}}/2}\) into \(C^m\), gives, when \(H\) is invertible,
\begin{equation}\label{l:eq:D.10}
 \mathbb E_{\mathbf x_*}Y^{-1/2}
 \le C^m(n/\kappa)^{m/4}|\det H|^{-1/2}.
\end{equation}
The product bound is uniform in \(\mathbf b_{\mathrm{shift}}\), so its
dependence on \(\mathbf u_*\) does not enter the right-hand side of
\eqref{l:eq:D.10}. We next integrate the remaining factor
\(|\det H|^{-1/2}\) over those column coordinates.

\emph{Integrate the column.} Fix the coordinates
\((\mathbf u_{\mathrm b})_\ell\) of the border column vector
\(\mathbf u_{\mathrm b}\) for
\(\ell\in[d]\setminus[m+1,2m]\). Integrate only over
\(\mathbf u_*=(\mathbf u_{\mathrm b})_{[m+1,2m]}\), the \(m\) border
column coordinates with indices \(m+1,\ldots,2m\).
The cofactor factorization \eqref{l:eq:D.6} gives
\begin{equation}\label{l:eq:D.11}
 H=\operatorname{diag}(E_{\mathrm{suf}}\mathbf u_*)E_{\mathrm{pre}}+H_0,
\end{equation}
with \(H_0\) fixed. Let \(\mathbf Z_u\) consist of the real values
and the real and imaginary parts of the conjugate-paired values of
\(E_{\mathrm{suf}}\mathbf u_*\). Complex multiplication in these
coordinates gives
\[
 H_{\mathbb R}(E_{\mathrm{pre}})_{\mathbb R}^{-1}
 =\mathcal D(\mathbf Z_u)
    +(H_0)_{\mathbb R}(E_{\mathrm{pre}})_{\mathbb R}^{-1}.
\]
The determinant on the left equals \(\det H/\det E_{\mathrm{pre}}\),
and the second term on the right is a fixed real matrix. This is
exactly the shifted determinant in Lemma~\ref{l:thm:DN}\textnormal{(ii)}.
The real coordinates of \(E_{\mathrm{suf}}\mathbf u_*\) in
Lemma~\ref{l:thm:DN} have covariance determinant
\(2^{-2b_{\mathrm{pair}}}(\kappa/n)^m|\det E_{\mathrm{suf}}|^2\).
Applying \eqref{l:eq:D.4} and again absorbing the factor
\(2^{b_{\mathrm{pair}}/2}\) into \(C^m\) yields
\begin{equation}\label{l:eq:D.12}
 \mathbb E_{\mathbf u_*}|\det H|^{-1/2}
 \le C^m(n/\kappa)^{m/4}
       |\det E_{\mathrm{pre}}\det E_{\mathrm{suf}}|^{-1/2}.
\end{equation}
Its finiteness proves that \(H\) is invertible almost surely, as required
in the row integration. Combining \eqref{l:eq:D.10} and \eqref{l:eq:D.12}
proves \eqref{l:eq:border-negative-moment}. Both bounds are uniform in
all fixed outside coordinates, so Tonelli also permits averaging over
the rest of the border.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
Apply Lemma~\ref{l:lem:border-negative-moment} to the spectral-event bound
above, then insert the evaluation determinant \eqref{l:eq:D.8}:
\begin{equation}\label{l:eq:D.13}
 \mathbb P_\kappa\{\rho(T)\le r\}
 \le C^m\left[
 \frac{(n/\kappa)^m(w+r^m)^n}
      {m^mw^{m-1}W_{2m}|Q_B(w)|}\right]^{1/2}.
\end{equation}
Here \(r^m\le R^m/2\le w\le R^m\). Thus \(w+r^m\le2w\), and
\eqref{l:eq:D.9}, with \(d_{\mathrm{tail}}=n-1-2m\), gives
\begin{equation}\label{l:eq:D.14}
 \mathbb P_\kappa\{\rho(T)\le r\}
 \le C^m\left[
 2^n(4\mathrm e)^{d_{\mathrm{tail}}}
 \left(\frac{n}{\kappa m}\right)^m
 \frac{R^{m^2+2m}}{W_{2m}}\right]^{1/2}.
\end{equation}
The radial power is
\(m(n-m+1-d_{\mathrm{tail}})=m^2+2m\). Since \(R\le1\), we may
replace \(R^{m^2+2m}\) by \(R^{m^2}\). All remaining factors are
bounded by \(e^{C_{\mathrm G}(\kappa)n}\), using
\(m,d_{\mathrm{tail}}\le n\) and
\(m\log(n/(\kappa m))\le n/\kappa\).
This proves \eqref{l:eq:gaussian-border}.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
\label{l:thm:OBSLAW}

On the observation space \(\mathcal C\times\mathbb R^{d\times k}\),
define \[
 P=\mathcal L(\mathsf C,Y),\qquad
 G=\mathcal L(\mathsf C)\otimes G_0,\qquad G_0=N(0,1)^{\otimes dk}.
\] Equivalently, \(G\) is obtained by replacing \(X\) with a standard
Gaussian matrix and running the same rule. Predictability and
orthogonality make its responses fresh Gaussian columns, as proved in
Lemma \ref{l:thm:LIKE} in Section \ref{l:sec:real_comparison_analysis}. The auxiliary
input keeps its original law in both experiments.
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
\label{l:thm:AC}

There are law-dependent positive constants
\(C_{\mathrm{coarse}},C_{\mathrm{weight}},\delta_\mu\), with
\(\delta_\mu\le1/4\), and
\(\theta_{\mathrm{coarse}},\theta_\mu\in(0,1)\), such that, when
\(h\le d\), every Borel observation event \(F\) satisfies \begin{equation}
P(F)\le e^{C_{\mathrm{coarse}}dk}G(F)^{\theta_{\mathrm{coarse}}}.
 \label{l:eq:A.2}
\end{equation} For \(F\subseteq\{\max_j\ell_j(U)\le\delta_\mu\}\), one has \begin{equation}
P(F)\le\left[\mathbb E_G\mathbf1_F
                    e^{C_{\mathrm{weight}}d\Theta(U)}\right]^{\theta_\mu}.
 \label{l:eq:A.3}
\end{equation} The constants are independent of dimensions, the query rule and the
law of \(\mathsf C\).
<!-- end theorem-6 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
\label{l:thm:FIXREF}

Fix \(I_0\subseteq[d]\), set \(J_0=[d]\setminus I_0\), and assume
\(k\le |J_0|\). The variables \(\mathbf s\) and \(A_{:,I_0}\) are
supplied to the query rule at the start. Under the original law they
have their original joint law and are independent of the remaining
matrix \(A_{:,J_0}\), which has iid entries of law \(\mu\). Under the
reference law \(\mathsf Q_{I_0}\), only the entries of \(A_{:,J_0}\) are
replaced by independent standard Gaussians, independent of
\((\mathbf s,A_{:,I_0})\). The latter pair retains its original joint
law and is averaged over, not fixed to a typical value.
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- lemma-15: body_md -->
\label{l:thm:RECONPROP}

Fix \(I_0\subseteq[d]\), write \(J_0=[d]\setminus I_0\), and assume
\(k\le |J_0|\). Starting from \(\mathbf s\) and the columns
\(A_{:,I_0}\), one can successively construct orthonormal vectors
\(\widehat{\mathbf v}_1,\ldots,\widehat{\mathbf v}_k\in\mathbb R^{J_0}\) and form \[
 \mathbf y_j^{\mathrm{rem}}:=A_{:,J_0}\widehat{\mathbf v}_j\in\mathbb R^d,
 \qquad j=1,\ldots,k.
\] Here \(\mathrm{rem}\) refers to the remaining columns indexed by
\(J_0\). Each \(\widehat{\mathbf v}_j\) is a Borel function only of
\(\mathbf s,A_{:,I_0},\mathbf y_1^{\mathrm{rem}},\ldots,\mathbf y_{j-1}^{\mathrm{rem}}\).
The construction has the following properties.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\arabic{enumi}.{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setlength{\itemsep}{0pt}\setlength{\parskip}{0pt}
\item
  For every realization of \((A,\mathbf s)\), the data
  \(\mathbf s,A_{:,I_0},\mathbf y_1^{\mathrm{rem}},\ldots,\mathbf y_j^{\mathrm{rem}}\)
  determine exactly the original products \(A\mathbf v_1,\ldots,A\mathbf v_j\) and hence
  the next Arnoldi vector \(\mathbf v_{j+1}\). Thus the resulting objects are
  the orthonormal vectors \(\widehat{\mathbf v}_j\), their products with the
  remaining matrix, and the reconstructed original Arnoldi vectors and
  residual lengths. Consequently the original link and deletion events
  are measurable with respect to
  \(\sigma(\mathbf s,A_{:,I_0},\mathbf y_1^{\mathrm{rem}},\ldots,\mathbf y_k^{\mathrm{rem}})\).
\item
  If \(V_{J_0,:}\) has rank \(k\), then, with
  \(\widehat V=[\widehat{\mathbf v}_1,\ldots,\widehat{\mathbf v}_k]\), \[
   \operatorname{Ran}\widehat V=\operatorname{Ran}V_{J_0,:},\qquad
   \sum_{a=1}^k (\widehat{\mathbf v}_a)_i^2=\ell_i(J_0)\quad(i\in J_0).
  \]
\item
  Under \(\mathsf Q_{I_0}\), the vectors
  \(\mathbf y_1^{\mathrm{rem}},\ldots,\mathbf y_k^{\mathrm{rem}}\) are independent
  \(N(0,\mathrm{Id}_d)\) vectors, jointly independent of
  \((\mathbf s,A_{:,I_0})\).
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-15 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
Starting with the original \(\mathbf v_1\), suppose the previous products have
been recovered, so \(\mathbf v_j\) is known. Orthogonalize its restriction: \[
 b_{ja}=\widehat{\mathbf v}_a^T(\mathbf v_j)_{J_0}\quad(a<j),\qquad
 \widehat{\mathbf r}_j=(\mathbf v_j)_{J_0}-\sum_{a=1}^{j-1}b_{ja}\widehat{\mathbf v}_a,\qquad \widehat R_j=\|\widehat{\mathbf r}_j\|.
\] Choose \(\widehat{\mathbf v}_j=\widehat{\mathbf r}_j/\widehat R_j\) when \(\widehat R_j>0\). If \(\widehat R_j=0\), apply
the same orthogonal completion rule as in Algorithm \ref{l:thm:ARN}, now in
\(\mathbb R^{J_0}\), with \[
 \widehat P_{j-1}=\sum_{a=1}^{j-1}\widehat{\mathbf v}_a\widehat{\mathbf v}_a^T:
 \qquad
 \widehat{\mathbf v}_j=\frac{(\mathrm{Id}_{J_0}-\widehat P_{j-1})e_i}
 {\|(\mathrm{Id}_{J_0}-\widehat P_{j-1})e_i\|},
\] where \(i\) is the smallest index in \(J_0\) for which the
denominator is nonzero. This supplements the reconstructed query frame
when a restricted direction is linearly dependent, while preserving the
original Arnoldi directions and responses. Such an index exists since
\(j\le k\le|J_0|\). Form the product \(\mathbf y_j^{\mathrm{rem}}\) defined
above. Linearity gives \begin{equation}
A\mathbf v_j=A_{:,I_0}(\mathbf v_j)_{I_0}
          +\sum_{a=1}^{j-1}b_{ja}\mathbf y_a^{\mathrm{rem}}+\widehat R_j\mathbf y_j^{\mathrm{rem}}.
 \label{l:eq:S.3}
\end{equation} The identity holds also when \(\widehat R_j=0\). It advances the original
Arnoldi recursion and proves (1) by induction. Each construction step is
Borel measurable and uses only the previously formed products. At full
restricted rank every \(\widehat R_j>0\), so \(\widehat V\) is obtained by
ordinary Gram--Schmidt and is a basis of
\(\operatorname{Ran}V_{J_0,:}\); its projection diagonals give (2).
Finally, the Gaussian assertion of Lemma \ref{l:thm:LIKE} gives (3). That lemma is
proved by orthogonal changes of variables independently of the present
link estimates.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- lemma-16: body_md -->
\label{l:thm:LM}

For every fixed \(\varepsilon\in(0,1]\), there are
\(\eta_{\mu,\varepsilon}\in(0,1]\) and
\(c_{\mathrm{mix}}(\mu,\varepsilon)>0\) such that \begin{equation}
\mathsf Q_{I_0}(\mathcal B_\varepsilon)
 \le2e^{-c_{\mathrm{mix}}(\mu,\varepsilon)dk}
 \label{l:eq:L.3}
\end{equation} whenever \(k\ge2\), \(128k\le\varepsilon d\), and
\(|I_0|\le\eta_{\mu,\varepsilon}k\). The bound is uniform over the
choice of \(I_0\) and over every independent starter law.
<!-- end lemma-16 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
We first bound the all-Gaussian probability, uniformly in the starter.
Lemma \ref{l:thm:LIKE} makes the Arnoldi responses fresh standard Gaussian vectors.
The predictable residual projection at step \(j\) has rank \(d-j\), so
\(R_j^2\), \(1\le j<k\), are independent \(\chi^2_{d-j}\) variables,
where \(\chi^2_\nu\) is the chi-square law with \(\nu\) degrees of freedom.
This is the usual Gaussian Hessenberg structure; see \cite{KV2014} for
the Ginibre representation and its characteristic-polynomial recursion.
For \(Z\sim\chi^2_\nu\) and integers \(s\ge0\) with \(2s<\nu\), the
gamma integral gives \begin{equation}
\mathbb E Z^{-s}=\prod_{\ell=1}^s(\nu-2\ell)^{-1}.
 \label{l:eq:L.2}
\end{equation} Set \(p=\lfloor\varepsilon d/(64k)\rfloor\), \(D=k+1+2pk\). Since
\(128k\le\varepsilon d\), \[
 \frac{\varepsilon d}{128k}\le p\le\frac{\varepsilon d}{64k},
 \qquad D\le\varepsilon(d+1)/16.
\] Every denominator in \eqref{l:eq:L.2} with \(s=pw_j\) is at least \(d+1-D\).
Independence and \(-\log(1-u)\le2u\), \(0\le u\le1/2\), imply
\(\log\mathbb E_{\mathrm G}W_k^{-2p}\le\varepsilon pM_k/8\). Markov,
\(\log(1-\varepsilon/4)\le-\varepsilon/4\), and \(M_k\ge k^2/8\) now
yield \begin{equation}
\mathbb P_{\mathrm G}(\mathcal B_\varepsilon)
 \le e^{-3\varepsilon pM_k/8}
 \le e^{-c_{\mathrm G}(\varepsilon)dk},
 \qquad c_{\mathrm G}(\varepsilon)=\varepsilon^2/4096.
 \label{l:eq:L.1}
\end{equation}

It remains to restore the columns in \(I_0\). Choose
\(0<q\le\min\{1,2t_f\}\) and let \(\phi_1(x)=(2\pi)^{-1/2}e^{-x^2/2}\).
Boundedness of the density and the square-exponential moment give \[
 D_q:=\int_{\mathbb R}f(x)^{1+q}\phi_1(x)^{-q}\,dx
 \le(K_f\sqrt{2\pi})^q\mathbb E e^{q\xi^2/2}<\infty.
\] Also \(D_q\ge1\), by Jensen's inequality under the standard Gaussian
law. Write \(s=|I_0|\). The likelihood ratio of the mixed-column law
relative to the all-Gaussian law is \[
 L_{I_0}(A,\mathbf s)=\prod_{i=1}^d\prod_{j\in I_0}
                           \frac{f(A_{ij})}{\phi_1(A_{ij})},\qquad
 \mathbb E_{\mathrm G}L_{I_0}^{1+q}=D_q^{ds}.
\] The starter contributes no likelihood factor. H\"older and \eqref{l:eq:L.1} give
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
 \mathsf Q_{I_0}(\mathcal B_\varepsilon)
 &\le D_q^{ds/(1+q)}
              \mathbb P_{\mathrm G}(\mathcal B_\varepsilon)^{q/(1+q)}\\
 &\le\exp\!\left\{\frac{ds\log D_q-qc_{\mathrm G}(\varepsilon)dk}{1+q}\right\}.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{l:eq:L.4}
\end{equation} Take \[
 \eta_{\mu,\varepsilon}
 =\min\left\{1,\frac{qc_{\mathrm G}(\varepsilon)}{2(1+\log D_q)}\right\},
 \qquad
 c_{\mathrm{mix}}=\frac{qc_{\mathrm G}(\varepsilon)}{2(1+q)}.
\] For \(s\le\eta_{\mu,\varepsilon}k\), \eqref{l:eq:L.4} is at most
\(e^{-c_{\mathrm{mix}}dk}\), which implies \eqref{l:eq:L.3}.
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- algorithms-2: body_md -->
\label{l:thm:DEL}

Fix the frame \(V=[\mathbf v_1,\ldots,\mathbf v_k]\) obtained after the \(k\) queries of
Algorithm \ref{l:thm:ARN}, a cap \(\sigma\in(0,1/4]\), and an integer deletion
budget \(B_{\mathrm{del}}\ge0\) with \(B_{\mathrm{del}}+k\le d\),
\(B_{\mathrm{del}}<d\). Start with \(J=[d]\), and at each step form \begin{equation}
\mathsf G(J)=V_{J,:}^TV_{J,:},\qquad
 \ell_j(J)=V_{j,:}\mathsf G(J)^{-1}V_{j,:}^T\quad(j\in J),
 \label{l:eq:S.1}
\end{equation} using the coordinate weights only if \(\mathsf G(J)\) is nonsingular. For the
retained subspace \(E_J=\operatorname{Ran}V_{J,:}\subseteq\mathbb R^J\),
\[
 \ell_j(J)=(P_{E_J})_{jj}=\|P_{E_J}e_j\|^2
 =\sup_{\substack{\mathbf v\in E_J\\\|\mathbf v\|=1}}|(\mathbf v)_j|^2.
\] Thus \(\ell_j(J)\) is the largest squared \(j\)-th coordinate of a
unit vector in the retained subspace. Calling the frame flat means that
none of these coordinate weights exceeds \(\sigma\).

\Needspace{7\baselineskip}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\arabic{enumi}.{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setlength{\itemsep}{0pt}\setlength{\parskip}{0pt}
\item
  If \(\mathsf G(J)>0\) and every \(\ell_j(J)\le\sigma\), stop with
  \textbf{flat} output.
\item
  Otherwise, if \(d-|J|=B_{\mathrm{del}}\), stop with \textbf{active}
  output.
\item
  Otherwise delete the smallest \(j\in J\) with \(\ell_j(J)>\sigma\); if
  \(\mathsf G(J)\) is singular, delete the smallest \(j\in J\). Repeat from step
  1.
\end{enumerate}

Set \(I_{\mathrm{del}}=[d]\setminus J\). Only rows of the fixed frame
are deleted; no further queries are made.
<!-- end algorithms-2 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{l:thm:SS}

Given \(\sigma_{\max}>0\), one can choose a cap
\(0<\sigma\le\min\{\sigma_{\max},1/4\}\) and an integer
\(L_{\mathrm{stop}}\ge2\), depending only on \(\mu,\sigma_{\max}\). For
every \(\nu,\alpha_{\max}\in(0,1]\), there then exist
\(\beta_{\mathrm{stop}}>0\), \(c_{\mathrm{stop}}>0\) and a dimension
threshold \(d_0\), depending only on
\(\mu,\sigma_{\max},\nu,\alpha_{\max}\), such that there exists a choice
of even integers \(k=k(d)\), one for each \(d\ge d_0\), satisfying \begin{equation}
\beta_{\mathrm{stop}}d\le k\le\alpha_{\max}d,
 \qquad B_{\mathrm{del}}=L_{\mathrm{stop}}k,
 \qquad B_{\mathrm{del}}+k\le d,\quad B_{\mathrm{del}}<d,
 \label{l:eq:S.5}
\end{equation} and Algorithm \ref{l:thm:DEL}, run with cap \(\sigma\), satisfies \begin{equation}
\mathbb P_{A,\mathbf s}\{\text{active output, or flat output with }
                |I_{\mathrm{del}}|\ge\lceil\nu k\rceil\}
 \le e^{-c_{\mathrm{stop}}d^2}.
 \label{l:eq:S.6}
\end{equation} The probability is taken under \[
 \mathcal L(A,\mathbf s)=\mu^{\otimes d^2}\otimes\mu^{\otimes d}.
\] Here \(A\) is the bottom-right core of \(\mathsf X_n\), and \(\mathbf s\) is
the additional independent starter for the Arnoldi iteration in
Algorithm \ref{l:thm:ARN}. In particular, \(\sigma\) and \(L_{\mathrm{stop}}\) are
independent of \(\nu,\alpha_{\max}\) and \(d\).
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- definition-4: body_md -->
\label{l:thm:VLOSS}

For a linear subspace \(E\subseteq\mathbb R^d\) and \(I_0\subseteq[d]\)
of size \(s\), define \[
 \Delta_{I_0}(E)=-\log\det(\mathrm{Id}_s-(P_E)_{I_0,I_0}),
 \qquad-\log0=+\infty.
\] For any orthonormal frame \(Q_E\) of \(E\), Sylvester's identity
gives
\[
 \Delta_{I_0}(E)=-\log\det((Q_E)_{I_0^c,:}^T(Q_E)_{I_0^c,:}),
\]
with empty determinant one. Thus this is the loss measured in \eqref{l:eq:S.2}.
<!-- end definition-4 -->
<!-- lemma-17: body_md -->
\label{l:thm:GV}

Fix \(a>0\). There are \(\kappa_{\mathrm{vol}}(\mu,a)>0\) and
\(c_{\mathrm{vol}}(\mu,a)>0\) such that the following holds. Let
\(\mathsf M\in\mathbb R^{d\times {m_{\mathrm{col}}}}\) have independent
columns with \[
 \mathsf M_{:,j}\sim\nu_j^{\otimes d},\qquad
 \nu_j\in\{\mu,N(0,1)\},\qquad 1\le j\le {m_{\mathrm{col}}},
\] where the laws \(\nu_1,\ldots,\nu_{m_{\mathrm{col}}}\) are fixed in
advance. Uniformly over these choices, for every fixed deletion set
\(I_0\subseteq[d]\) of size \(s\), \begin{equation}
1\le s<{m_{\mathrm{col}}},\quad {m_{\mathrm{col}}}^2\le\kappa_{\mathrm{vol}}ds
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \mathbb P\{\Delta_{I_0}(\operatorname{Ran}\mathsf M)\ge as\}
 \le e^{-c_{\mathrm{vol}}ds^2/{m_{\mathrm{col}}}}.
 \label{l:eq:G.9}
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-17 -->
<!-- lemma-18: body_md -->
\label{l:thm:GE}

For each fixed \(\theta>0\) there are
\(\delta_\theta,\alpha_\theta,C_\theta>0\), depending only on
\(\mu,\theta\), such that, for the independent-column matrix
\(\mathsf M\in\mathbb R^{h\times {m_{\mathrm{col}}}}\) and the
coordinate weights of its range
\(\ell_i=(P_{\operatorname{Ran}\mathsf M})_{ii}\),
\(1\le {m_{\mathrm{col}}}\le h\), \(0<\delta\le\delta_\theta\), and
\({m_{\mathrm{col}}}/h\le\alpha_\theta\), \begin{equation}
\log\mathbb E e^{\theta h\sum_{i=1}^h\min(\ell_i,\delta)^{3/2}}
 \le C_\theta h^2({m_{\mathrm{col}}}/h)^{3/2}.
 \label{l:eq:G.6}
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-18 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
We estimate the complement of the good deletion event \(\mathcal H\)
by fixing candidate deletion sets before applying comparison.

\Needspace{6\baselineskip}
\textbf{Split the failure event.} For each fixed candidate deletion set
\(I_0\subseteq[d]\), write \[
 s:=|I_0|,\qquad J_0:=[d]\setminus I_0,\qquad h:=|J_0|=d-s.
\] Thus \(s\) is a deterministic candidate deletion count; on
\(\{I_{\mathrm{del}}=I_0\}\), it equals the actual number deleted.
Define \[
 \begin{aligned}
 F_{I_0}^{\mathrm{act}}
   &:=\{\text{active output},\ I_{\mathrm{del}}=I_0\},\\
 F_{I_0}^{\mathrm{flat}}
   &:=\{\text{flat output},\ I_{\mathrm{del}}=I_0\}.
 \end{aligned}
\] An active output always has \(|I_{\mathrm{del}}|=B_{\mathrm{del}}\).
Thus the event in \eqref{l:eq:S.6} is exactly \[
 \mathcal E:=\mathcal H^c=
 \mathop{\dot\bigcup}_{|I_0|=B_{\mathrm{del}}}F_{I_0}^{\mathrm{act}}
 \,\dot\cup\,
 \mathop{\dot\bigcup}_{\lceil\nu k\rceil\le |I_0|\le B_{\mathrm{del}}}
 F_{I_0}^{\mathrm{flat}}.
\] Lemma \ref{l:thm:RECONPROP} makes each fixed-set event observable. We estimate it
under its own reference law \(\mathsf Q_{I_0}\), then compare back to
\(\mathbb P_\mu\) before summing.

\Needspace{6\baselineskip}
\textbf{A volume bound for both branches.} Choose the cap \[
 0<\sigma\le\min\{\sigma_{\max},1/4,\delta_\mu,
                                      \delta_{4C_{\mathrm{weight}}}\}.
\] This depends only on \(\mu,\sigma_{\max}\) and permits both weighted
comparison and Lemma \ref{l:thm:GE}. The constants \(c_{\mathrm{vol}}\) and
\(\kappa_{\mathrm{vol}}\) below are those of Lemma \ref{l:thm:GV} at \(a=\sigma\).
We will choose \(L_{\mathrm{stop}}\ge2\) and a sufficiently small
\(\alpha>0\), and set \(k=2\lfloor\alpha d/2\rfloor\). For now, assume
the geometric hypotheses hold for all
\(\lceil\nu k\rceil\le s\le L_{\mathrm{stop}}k\); we verify this
simultaneously at the end. For a fixed \(I_0\) of size \(s\), write
\({m_{\mathrm{col}}}=s+k+1\). The volume certificate gives
\eqref{l:eq:P.3}:
\[
 \mathsf Q_{I_0}\{I_{\mathrm{del}}=I_0\}
 \le e^{-c_{\mathrm{vol}}ds^2/(s+k+1)}.
\]
\Needspace{6\baselineskip}
\textbf{Active outputs: choose the budget large enough.} Fix a
candidate \(I_0\) of size
\(s=|I_0|=B_{\mathrm{del}}=L_{\mathrm{stop}}k\): an active output has
used the full deletion budget. Since
\(F_{I_0}^{\mathrm{act}}\subseteq\{I_{\mathrm{del}}=I_0\}\) and
\(s+k+1\le3s\) for \(k\ge2\), coarse comparison \eqref{l:eq:A.2} gives \[
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathbb P_\mu(F_{I_0}^{\mathrm{act}})
 &\le e^{C_{\mathrm{coarse}}dk}
       \mathsf Q_{I_0}(F_{I_0}^{\mathrm{act}})^{\theta_{\mathrm{coarse}}}\\
 &\le\exp\{(C_{\mathrm{coarse}}
          -\theta_{\mathrm{coarse}}c_{\mathrm{vol}}L_{\mathrm{stop}}/3)dk\}.
 \end{aligned}
\] The comparison costs a fixed multiple of \(dk\), whereas the volume
bound supplies a multiple of \(L_{\mathrm{stop}}dk\). We can therefore
fix \begin{equation}
L_{\mathrm{stop}}:=\max\left\{2,
 \left\lceil\frac{6(C_{\mathrm{coarse}}+1)}
 {\theta_{\mathrm{coarse}}c_{\mathrm{vol}}}\right\rceil\right\}.
 \label{l:eq:P.4}
\end{equation} Then \(\theta_{\mathrm{coarse}}c_{\mathrm{vol}}L_{\mathrm{stop}}/6
\ge C_{\mathrm{coarse}}+1\), and in particular
\(\mathbb P_\mu(F_{I_0}^{\mathrm{act}})\le e^{-dk}\).

\Needspace{6\baselineskip}
\textbf{Flat but late outputs: make the weighted cost small.} Fix a
candidate \(I_0\) whose deletion count \(s=|I_0|\) satisfies \[
 \lceil\nu k\rceil\le s\le B_{\mathrm{del}}=L_{\mathrm{stop}}k.
\] Since \(F_{I_0}^{\mathrm{flat}}\subseteq\{I_{\mathrm{del}}=I_0\}\),
the common reference estimate, \(s\ge\nu k\), and
\({m_{\mathrm{col}}}=s+k+1\le(L_{\mathrm{stop}}+2)k\) give \[
 \mathsf Q_{I_0}(F_{I_0}^{\mathrm{flat}})
 \le e^{-c_{\mathrm{vol}}\nu^2dk/(L_{\mathrm{stop}}+2)}.
\] When \(\nu\) is small, this decay need not absorb the fixed
\(C_{\mathrm{coarse}}dk\) cost. We use flatness to apply
\eqref{l:eq:A.3}, keeping its random comparison cost inside the
expectation. On the retained coordinates \(J_0\), set
\[
 \Psi_{I_0}=\sum_{j\in J_0}
 \min\{(P_{\operatorname{Ran}\mathsf M^{\mathrm{ref}(I_0)}_{J_0,:}})_{jj},
       \sigma\}^{3/2}.
\] On \(F_{I_0}^{\mathrm{flat}}\), the reconstructed frame
\(\widehat V\) spans \(\operatorname{Ran}V_{J_0,:}\). Flatness and
\eqref{l:eq:P.2} therefore give
\[
 \ell_j(\widehat V)\le
 \min\{(P_{\operatorname{Ran}\mathsf M^{\mathrm{ref}(I_0)}_{J_0,:}})_{jj},
       \sigma\},\qquad j\in J_0.
\]
Thus \(\Theta(\widehat V)\le\Psi_{I_0}\) on this event.
Applying \eqref{l:eq:A.3} and then
Cauchy--Schwarz yields \begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb P_\mu(F_{I_0}^{\mathrm{flat}})
 &\le\left[\mathbb E_{\mathsf Q_{I_0}}
       \mathbf1_{F_{I_0}^{\mathrm{flat}}}
       e^{C_{\mathrm{weight}}d\Psi_{I_0}}\right]^{\theta_\mu}\\
 &\le\left[\mathsf Q_{I_0}(F_{I_0}^{\mathrm{flat}})
       \mathbb E_{\mathsf Q_{I_0}}e^{2C_{\mathrm{weight}}d\Psi_{I_0}}
       \right]^{\theta_\mu/2}.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{l:eq:P.6}
\end{equation}
The first factor is the late-output probability controlled by the
volume bound above; the second controls the cost of comparison.
For this second factor, \(h\ge d/2\) implies
\(2d/h\le4\), so Lemma \ref{l:thm:GE}
applies at parameter \(4C_{\mathrm{weight}}\). Set
\(C_{\mathrm{cap}}=C_{4C_{\mathrm{weight}}}(L_{\mathrm{stop}}+2)^{3/2}\).
Since \(h\le d\), \({m_{\mathrm{col}}}\le(L_{\mathrm{stop}}+2)k\), and
\(k/d\le\alpha\), it gives \begin{equation}
\log\mathbb E_{\mathsf Q_{I_0}}e^{2C_{\mathrm{weight}}d\Psi_{I_0}}
 \le C_{4C_{\mathrm{weight}}}\sqrt h\,{m_{\mathrm{col}}}^{3/2}
 \le C_{\mathrm{cap}}\sqrt\alpha\,dk.
 \label{l:eq:P.7}
\end{equation} Substitution in \eqref{l:eq:P.6} makes the required balance explicit: \[
 \mathbb P_\mu(F_{I_0}^{\mathrm{flat}})
 \le\exp\left\{-\frac{\theta_\mu}{2}
 \left(\frac{c_{\mathrm{vol}}\nu^2}{L_{\mathrm{stop}}+2}
               -C_{\mathrm{cap}}\sqrt\alpha\right)dk\right\}.
\] Consequently,
\(\mathbb P_\mu(F_{I_0}^{\mathrm{flat}})\le e^{-c_{\mathrm{flat}}dk}\)
provided that
\[
 C_{\mathrm{cap}}\sqrt\alpha\le
 \frac{c_{\mathrm{vol}}\nu^2}{2(L_{\mathrm{stop}}+2)}
\]
where
\[
 c_{\mathrm{flat}}:=\frac{\theta_\mu c_{\mathrm{vol}}\nu^2}
                            {4(L_{\mathrm{stop}}+2)}>0.
\]

\Needspace{6\baselineskip}
\textbf{Verify the parameter conditions.} The cap \(\sigma\) and
multiplier \(L_{\mathrm{stop}}\) are now fixed. Choose \(\alpha>0\)
small enough that \begin{equation}
\begin{gathered}
 \alpha\le\alpha_{\max},\quad (L_{\mathrm{stop}}+2)\alpha\le1/4,\quad
 (L_{\mathrm{stop}}+2)^2\alpha\le\kappa_{\mathrm{vol}}\nu,\quad
 2(L_{\mathrm{stop}}+2)\alpha\le\alpha_{4C_{\mathrm{weight}}},\\
 C_{\mathrm{cap}}\sqrt\alpha
       \le c_{\mathrm{vol}}\nu^2/[2(L_{\mathrm{stop}}+2)].
 \end{gathered}
 \label{l:eq:P.5}
\end{equation} All right-hand bounds are positive and independent of \(d\), so these
conditions are compatible. The first gives the prescribed ceiling on
\(k/d\); the last is exactly the cost bound just obtained. To check the
geometric hypotheses, take any candidate deletion count \(s=|I_0|\) in
\([\lceil\nu k\rceil,L_{\mathrm{stop}}k]\). Then \[
 \begin{gathered}
 {m_{\mathrm{col}}}=s+k+1\le(L_{\mathrm{stop}}+2)k,\qquad h=d-s\ge d/2,\\
 {m_{\mathrm{col}}}^2\le(L_{\mathrm{stop}}+2)^2k^2\le\kappa_{\mathrm{vol}}\nu dk
                            \le\kappa_{\mathrm{vol}}ds,\\
 \frac {m_{\mathrm{col}}}h\le2(L_{\mathrm{stop}}+2)\frac kd
                   \le\alpha_{4C_{\mathrm{weight}}}.
 \end{gathered}
\] These are precisely the volume and capped-moment hypotheses used
above. For sufficiently large \(d\), the rounding of \(k\) also gives
\(k\ge\max\{2,\alpha d/2\}\). With
\(B_{\mathrm{del}}=L_{\mathrm{stop}}k\), we also have
\(B_{\mathrm{del}}+k\le d\) and \(B_{\mathrm{del}}<d\). Thus the
algorithm and both estimates apply.

\Needspace{6\baselineskip}
\textbf{Sum the fixed-set bounds.} Using the event decomposition, with
at most \(2^d\) possible sets in each branch, we obtain \[
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathbb P_\mu(\mathcal E)
 &=\sum_{|I_0|=B_{\mathrm{del}}}\mathbb P_\mu(F_{I_0}^{\mathrm{act}})
   +\sum_{\lceil\nu k\rceil\le|I_0|\le B_{\mathrm{del}}}
                         \mathbb P_\mu(F_{I_0}^{\mathrm{flat}})\\
 &\le2^d(e^{-dk}+e^{-c_{\mathrm{flat}}dk})\\
 &\le2\exp\{d\log2-\tfrac\alpha2
                              \min(1,c_{\mathrm{flat}})d^2\}
 \le e^{-c_{\mathrm{stop}}d^2}
 \end{aligned}
\] for sufficiently large \(d\), with, for example,
\(c_{\mathrm{stop}}=\alpha\min(1,c_{\mathrm{flat}})/4\). This proves
\eqref{l:eq:S.6} and gives \eqref{l:eq:S.5} with \(\beta_{\mathrm{stop}}=\alpha/2\). Every
summand was estimated under the original law; no conditioning on the
random deletion set is used.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
Fix \(\varepsilon\in(0,1]\). Take \(\eta_{\mu,\varepsilon}\) and
\(c_{\mathrm{mix}}\) from Lemma \ref{l:thm:LM}. Choose
\(0<\tau\le\min\{\delta_\mu,1/4\}\) so small that \[
 C_{\mathrm{weight}}\sqrt\tau\le c_{\mathrm{mix}}/4.
\] Apply Proposition \ref{l:thm:SS} with \(\sigma_{\max}=\tau\),
\(\nu=\eta_{\mu,\varepsilon}\) and \(\alpha_{\max}=\varepsilon/128\). It
supplies a cap \(\sigma\le\tau\), an even \(k\) and a deletion budget
\(B_{\mathrm{del}}\), with \(k\ge\beta_{\mathrm{stop}}d\). Set
\(m=k/2\), so \(M_k=m^2\).
The choice of \(\nu\) allows the mixed-reference link estimate on every
good deletion output; the choice of \(\tau\) pays the comparison cost.

\Needspace{6\baselineskip}
\textbf{Discard unsuccessful deletion.} On the original probability space of
\((A,\mathbf s)\), define \[
 \mathcal B_\varepsilon
   :=\{W_k(A,\mathbf s)\le(1-\varepsilon/4)^{M_k}\}.
\] For the good deletion event \(\mathcal H\) defined above,
\eqref{l:eq:S.6} gives
\(\mathbb P_\mu(\mathcal H^c)\le e^{-c_{\mathrm{stop}}d^2}\).
Define the deterministic family of candidate sets and their events by
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathcal I&:=\{I_0\subseteq[d]:|I_0|<\lceil\nu k\rceil,
                              \ |I_0|\le B_{\mathrm{del}}\},\\
 F_{I_0}&:=\mathcal B_\varepsilon\cap\mathcal H
                                  \cap\{I_{\mathrm{del}}=I_0\}.
 \end{aligned}
\] The deletion count never exceeds its budget, so we have the disjoint
union \[
 \mathcal B_\varepsilon
 = (\mathcal B_\varepsilon\cap\mathcal H^c)
       \,\dot\cup\,\mathop{\dot\bigcup}_{I_0\in\mathcal I}F_{I_0}.
\] In particular, \[
 \mathbb P_\mu(\mathcal B_\varepsilon)
 \le e^{-c_{\mathrm{stop}}d^2}
       +\sum_{I_0\in\mathcal I}\mathbb P_\mu(F_{I_0}).
\]

\Needspace{6\baselineskip}
\textbf{Compare bad links on each good output.} Fix \(I_0\in\mathcal I\) and write
\(J_0=[d]\setminus I_0\). Since \(|I_0|\le B_{\mathrm{del}}\) and
\(B_{\mathrm{del}}+k\le d\), we have \(k\le|J_0|\). Lemma \ref{l:thm:RECONPROP}
makes \(F_{I_0}\) measurable in the reconstructed products and auxiliary
data. On this event the retained frame has full rank and
\(\max_{i\in J_0}\ell_i(\widehat V)\le\sigma\); since the weights sum to
\(k\), we have \(\Theta(\widehat V)\le k\sqrt\sigma\).
The integer inequality
\(|I_0|<\lceil\nu k\rceil\) implies
\(|I_0|<\nu k\le\eta_{\mu,\varepsilon}k\). Since
\(F_{I_0}\subseteq\mathcal B_\varepsilon\), Lemma \ref{l:thm:LM} gives \[
 \mathsf Q_{I_0}(F_{I_0})
 \le\mathsf Q_{I_0}(\mathcal B_\varepsilon)
 \le2e^{-c_{\mathrm{mix}}dk}.
\] Since \(\sigma\le\tau\), we have
\(C_{\mathrm{weight}}\sqrt\sigma\le c_{\mathrm{mix}}/4\).
The flat-coordinate comparison of Theorem~\ref{l:thm:AC} gives
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb P_\mu(F_{I_0})
 &\le e^{\theta_\mu C_{\mathrm{weight}}\sqrt\sigma\,dk}
          \mathsf Q_{I_0}(F_{I_0})^{\theta_\mu}\\
 &\le2^{\theta_\mu}
    e^{-\theta_\mu(c_{\mathrm{mix}}-C_{\mathrm{weight}}\sqrt\sigma)dk}
 \le e^{-c_{\mathrm{cand}}dk},\qquad c_{\mathrm{cand}}:=\theta_\mu c_{\mathrm{mix}}/2,
 \end{aligned}
 \label{l:eq:S.7}
\end{equation} for sufficiently large \(d\). These are probabilities of the full
events \(F_{I_0}\), not probabilities conditioned on
\(I_{\mathrm{del}}=I_0\).

\Needspace{6\baselineskip}
\textbf{Summing the candidates.} Since \(|\mathcal I|\le2^d\) and
\(k\ge\beta_{\mathrm{stop}}d\), \[
 \begin{aligned}
 \sum_{I_0\in\mathcal I}\mathbb P_\mu(F_{I_0})
 &\le 2^d e^{-c_{\mathrm{cand}}dk}
 \le e^{d\log2-c_{\mathrm{cand}}\beta_{\mathrm{stop}}d^2}\\
 &\le e^{-c_{\mathrm{few}}d^2},\qquad
 c_{\mathrm{few}}:=c_{\mathrm{cand}}\beta_{\mathrm{stop}}/2,
 \end{aligned}
\] for sufficiently large \(d\). Substituting into the event
decomposition yields \[
 \mathbb P_\mu(\mathcal B_\varepsilon)
 \le e^{-c_{\mathrm{stop}}d^2}+e^{-c_{\mathrm{few}}d^2}
 \le e^{-c_{\mathrm{link}}d^2},\qquad
 c_{\mathrm{link}}:=\tfrac12\min\{c_{\mathrm{stop}},c_{\mathrm{few}}\},
\] after increasing the dimension threshold. This is \eqref{l:eq:M.8}. Finally,
\(2m=k\ge\beta_{\mathrm{stop}}d\) and \(256m=128k\le\varepsilon d\), so
\eqref{l:eq:M.7} holds with \(\beta_{\mu,\varepsilon}=\beta_{\mathrm{stop}}\); also
\(m\ge1\) for large \(d\).
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- lemma-19: body_md -->
\label{l:thm:GP}

If the independent coordinates of \(x\in\mathbb R^h\) have everywhere
bounded densities \(f_j\le K\), then for every coisometry
\(L:\mathbb R^h\to\mathbb R^q\), \(LL^T=\mathrm{Id}_q\), the canonical
density satisfies \begin{equation}
\int_{\ker L}\prod_j f_j((L^Ty)_j+w_j)\,dw
 \le(\sqrt{\mathrm e}K)^q
 \quad\text{for every }y.
 \label{l:eq:G.1}
\end{equation} Consequently, for a fixed subspace \(F\) of codimension \(q\ge1\), \begin{equation}
\mathbb P\{\operatorname{dist}(x,F)\le u\sqrt q\}\le\min\{1,(C_Ku)^q\},
 \qquad
 \|\operatorname{dist}(x,F)^{-2}\|_{L^p}\le C'_K/q
 \quad(1\le p\le q/4).
 \label{l:eq:G.2}
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-19 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
Write \(\ell_j^{\ker}=(P_{\ker L})_{jj}\). For \(\ell_j^{\ker}>0\), its
normalized coordinate maps \(B_j:\ker L\to\mathbb R\) satisfy
\(B_jB_j^T=1\) and
\(\sum_{\substack{1\le j\le h\\\ell_j^{\ker}>0}}\ell_j^{\ker}B_j^TB_j=\mathrm{Id}_{\ker L}\).
The geometric Brascamp--Lieb inequality \cite[Proposition 2.8]{BCCT2008}
says \[
 \int_{\ker L}\prod_{\substack{1\le j\le h\\\ell_j^{\ker}>0}}
                    h_j(B_jw)^{\ell_j^{\ker}}\,dw
 \le\prod_{\substack{1\le j\le h\\\ell_j^{\ker}>0}}
                    \left(\int_{\mathbb R}h_j\right)^{\ell_j^{\ker}}.
\] For each \(1\le j\le h\) with \(\ell_j^{\ker}>0\), apply it to
\(h_j=f_j((L^Ty)_j+\sqrt{\ell_j^{\ker}}\,\cdot)^{1/\ell_j^{\ker}}\). Its
integral is at most \((\ell_j^{\ker})^{-1/2}K^{1/\ell_j^{\ker}-1}\). The
zero-coordinate maps contribute constants at most \(K\). Since
\(\sum_{j=1}^h(1-\ell_j^{\ker})=q\), the fiber bound is \[
 K^q\prod_{\ell_j^{\ker}>0}(\ell_j^{\ker})^{-\ell_j^{\ker}/2}\le K^qe^{q/2},
\] using \(-c\log c\le1-c\). This proves \eqref{l:eq:G.1} for the actual chosen
density representatives, with no exceptional fiber. Orthogonal
disintegration identifies it as a projection density. Multiplication by
the \(q\)-ball volume proves the first estimate in \eqref{l:eq:G.2}. More
explicitly, for \(Y=\operatorname{dist}(x,F)/\sqrt q\) and \(0<s<q\), \[
 \mathbb E Y^{-s}
 \le sC_K^q\int_0^{1/C_K}u^{q-s-1}\,du
    +s\int_{1/C_K}^\infty u^{-s-1}\,du
 =C_K^s\frac q{q-s}.
\] Take \(s=2p\le q/2\) and a \(p\)-th root to obtain the second bound.
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label{l:thm:IGRAM}

Assume \eqref{l:eq:M.1}. Let \(\mathsf M\in\mathbb R^{h\times m_{\mathrm{col}}}\)
have independent entries, with each column consisting of iid entries of
law \(\mu\) or \(N(0,1)\), the column types being fixed. If
\(1\le m_{\mathrm{col}}\le h/2\), then \(\mathsf G=\mathsf M^T\mathsf M>0\)
almost surely. For every \(J\subseteq[m_{\mathrm{col}}]\), write
\(J^c=[m_{\mathrm{col}}]\setminus J\). For \(t\ge0\) and
\(1\le p\le h/8\), \begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
 \left\|\frac{\det \mathsf G_{J^c,J^c}}{\det \mathsf G}\right\|_{L^p}
 &\le(C_{\mathrm{inv}}(\mu)/h)^{|J|},\\
 \|\det(\mathrm{Id}_{m_{\mathrm{col}}}+th\mathsf G^{-1})\|_{L^p}
 &\le(1+C_{\mathrm{inv}}(\mu)t)^{m_{\mathrm{col}}}.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{l:eq:G.3}
\end{equation} Here \(C_{\mathrm{inv}}(\mu)>0\) depends only on \(\mu\), uniformly
over the column types, and the empty determinant is one.
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
Each column has a density and hence almost surely avoids the span of its
predecessors; thus \(\mathsf G>0\). First expose the columns in \(J^c\), then
add those in \(J\) in a fixed order. The determinant ratio is the
product of the inverse squared distances of each new column to the
preceding span. Every such span has codimension at least
\(h-{m_{\mathrm{col}}}+1\). Apply \eqref{l:eq:G.2} to the last factor conditional
on all preceding columns, and repeat backwards. This bounds the moment
of the product without asserting independence of the distances. The
second estimate follows from the complementary-minor identity
\(\det(\mathrm{Id}_{m_{\mathrm{col}}}+th\mathsf G^{-1})=\sum_{J\subseteq[{m_{\mathrm{col}}}]}(th)^{|J|}
\det \mathsf G_{J^c,J^c}/\det \mathsf G\) and Minkowski's inequality.
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- head-2: body_md -->
\label{l:proof-of-the-coordinate-volume-bound}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Lemma \ref{l:thm:GV}]Write
\(\mathsf G_{\mathrm{del}}=\mathsf M_{I_0,:}^T\mathsf M_{I_0,:}\),
\(\mathsf G_{\mathrm{keep}}=\mathsf M_{I_0^c,:}^T\mathsf M_{I_0^c,:}\), and
\(h=d-s\). These Grams are independent because they involve disjoint
rows. For the small aspect ratios chosen below,
\(m_{\mathrm{col}}<h/2\), and Corollary \ref{l:thm:IGRAM} gives
\(\mathsf G_{\mathrm{keep}}>0\) almost surely. The matrix
\(\mathsf M(\mathsf G_{\mathrm{del}}+\mathsf G_{\mathrm{keep}})^{-1/2}\) has orthonormal
columns spanning \(\operatorname{Ran}\mathsf M\): its Gram is the
identity and the right factor is invertible. After restricting this
frame to \(I_0^c\), its Gram determinant, hence its squared volume, is
\(\det \mathsf G_{\mathrm{keep}}/\det(\mathsf G_{\mathrm{del}}+\mathsf G_{\mathrm{keep}})\);
taking the negative logarithm gives the equality below. Thus \begin{equation}
\Delta_{I_0}(\operatorname{Ran}\mathsf M)=\log\frac{\det(\mathsf G_{\mathrm{del}}+\mathsf G_{\mathrm{keep}})}
                              {\det \mathsf G_{\mathrm{keep}}}
 \le\log\det(\mathrm{Id}_{m_{\mathrm{col}}}+\tau d\mathsf G_{\mathrm{keep}}^{-1})
       +\frac{\operatorname{tr}\mathsf G_{\mathrm{del}}}{\tau d},\quad\tau>0.
 \label{l:eq:G.10}
\end{equation} To verify the inequality, add
\(\tau d\mathrm{Id}_{m_{\mathrm{col}}}\) to the retained Gram in the
numerator. Factor its determinant, apply
\(\log\det(\mathrm{Id}_{m_{\mathrm{col}}}+D)\le
\operatorname{tr}D\) for a positive semidefinite matrix \(D\), and use
\((\mathsf G_{\mathrm{keep}}+\tau d\mathrm{Id}_{m_{\mathrm{col}}})^{-1}\preceq(\tau d)^{-1}\mathrm{Id}_{m_{\mathrm{col}}}\).
No commutation of the two Grams is used.

Choose \(\lambda>0\) and \(C_{\mathrm{vol}}\ge1\), depending on \(\mu\),
such that \(C_{\mathrm{vol}}\ge4C_{\mathrm{inv}}/3\) and
\(\log\mathbb E e^{t\xi^2}\le C_{\mathrm{vol}}t\) for both scalar laws
and \(0\le t\le\lambda\). Set \[
 \tau_0=\frac a{4C_{\mathrm{vol}}},\quad
 q_0=\min\{3/32,\lambda\tau_0\},\quad
 \kappa_{\mathrm{vol}}=\min\{1/4,q_0,a\tau_0/(4C_{\mathrm{vol}})\}.
\] Set \(\tau=\tau_0s/{m_{\mathrm{col}}}\),
\(q=q_0s/{m_{\mathrm{col}}}\), \(p=qd\). The assumed aspect bound gives
\(s<{m_{\mathrm{col}}}<d/4\), \(h>3d/4\),
\(1\le {m_{\mathrm{col}}}\le p<h/8\), and \(q/\tau\le\lambda\). Thus
Corollary \ref{l:thm:IGRAM} applies at moment order \(p\). From \eqref{l:eq:G.10},
independence, and the entrywise square-exponential bound, \[
 \begin{aligned}
 \log\mathbb E e^{p\Delta_{I_0}(\operatorname{Ran}\mathsf M)}
 &\le p{m_{\mathrm{col}}}\log(1+C_{\mathrm{inv}}\tau d/h)
       +C_{\mathrm{vol}}qs{m_{\mathrm{col}}}/\tau\\
 &\le C_{\mathrm{vol}}q\tau d{m_{\mathrm{col}}}+C_{\mathrm{vol}}qs{m_{\mathrm{col}}}/\tau\\
 &=C_{\mathrm{vol}}q\tau_0ds+C_{\mathrm{vol}}q{m_{\mathrm{col}}}^2/\tau_0
 \le\tfrac12 aqds.
 \end{aligned}
\] Markov proves \eqref{l:eq:G.9} with \(c_{\mathrm{vol}}=aq_0/2\). \end{proof}
<!-- end head-2 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
Write
\(\mathsf G_{\mathrm{del}}=\mathsf M_{I_0,:}^T\mathsf M_{I_0,:}\),
\(\mathsf G_{\mathrm{keep}}=\mathsf M_{I_0^c,:}^T\mathsf M_{I_0^c,:}\), and
\(h=d-s\). These Grams are independent because they involve disjoint
rows. For the small aspect ratios chosen below,
\(m_{\mathrm{col}}<h/2\), and Corollary \ref{l:thm:IGRAM} gives
\(\mathsf G_{\mathrm{keep}}>0\) almost surely. The matrix
\(\mathsf M(\mathsf G_{\mathrm{del}}+\mathsf G_{\mathrm{keep}})^{-1/2}\) has orthonormal
columns spanning \(\operatorname{Ran}\mathsf M\): its Gram is the
identity and the right factor is invertible. After restricting this
frame to \(I_0^c\), its Gram determinant, hence its squared volume, is
\(\det \mathsf G_{\mathrm{keep}}/\det(\mathsf G_{\mathrm{del}}+\mathsf G_{\mathrm{keep}})\);
taking the negative logarithm gives the equality below. Thus \begin{equation}
\Delta_{I_0}(\operatorname{Ran}\mathsf M)=\log\frac{\det(\mathsf G_{\mathrm{del}}+\mathsf G_{\mathrm{keep}})}
                              {\det \mathsf G_{\mathrm{keep}}}
 \le\log\det(\mathrm{Id}_{m_{\mathrm{col}}}+\tau d\mathsf G_{\mathrm{keep}}^{-1})
       +\frac{\operatorname{tr}\mathsf G_{\mathrm{del}}}{\tau d},\quad\tau>0.
 \label{l:eq:G.10}
\end{equation} To verify the inequality, add
\(\tau d\mathrm{Id}_{m_{\mathrm{col}}}\) to the retained Gram in the
numerator. Factor its determinant, apply
\(\log\det(\mathrm{Id}_{m_{\mathrm{col}}}+D)\le
\operatorname{tr}D\) for a positive semidefinite matrix \(D\), and use
\((\mathsf G_{\mathrm{keep}}+\tau d\mathrm{Id}_{m_{\mathrm{col}}})^{-1}\preceq(\tau d)^{-1}\mathrm{Id}_{m_{\mathrm{col}}}\).
No commutation of the two Grams is used.

Choose \(\lambda>0\) and \(C_{\mathrm{vol}}\ge1\), depending on \(\mu\),
such that \(C_{\mathrm{vol}}\ge4C_{\mathrm{inv}}/3\) and
\(\log\mathbb E e^{t\xi^2}\le C_{\mathrm{vol}}t\) for both scalar laws
and \(0\le t\le\lambda\). Set \[
 \tau_0=\frac a{4C_{\mathrm{vol}}},\quad
 q_0=\min\{3/32,\lambda\tau_0\},\quad
 \kappa_{\mathrm{vol}}=\min\{1/4,q_0,a\tau_0/(4C_{\mathrm{vol}})\}.
\] Set \(\tau=\tau_0s/{m_{\mathrm{col}}}\),
\(q=q_0s/{m_{\mathrm{col}}}\), \(p=qd\). The assumed aspect bound gives
\(s<{m_{\mathrm{col}}}<d/4\), \(h>3d/4\),
\(1\le {m_{\mathrm{col}}}\le p<h/8\), and \(q/\tau\le\lambda\). Thus
Corollary \ref{l:thm:IGRAM} applies at moment order \(p\). From \eqref{l:eq:G.10},
independence, and the entrywise square-exponential bound, \[
 \begin{aligned}
 \log\mathbb E e^{p\Delta_{I_0}(\operatorname{Ran}\mathsf M)}
 &\le p{m_{\mathrm{col}}}\log(1+C_{\mathrm{inv}}\tau d/h)
       +C_{\mathrm{vol}}qs{m_{\mathrm{col}}}/\tau\\
 &\le C_{\mathrm{vol}}q\tau d{m_{\mathrm{col}}}+C_{\mathrm{vol}}qs{m_{\mathrm{col}}}/\tau\\
 &=C_{\mathrm{vol}}q\tau_0ds+C_{\mathrm{vol}}q{m_{\mathrm{col}}}^2/\tau_0
 \le\tfrac12 aqds.
 \end{aligned}
\] Markov proves \eqref{l:eq:G.9} with \(c_{\mathrm{vol}}=aq_0/2\).
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- lemma-20: body_md -->
\label{l:thm:SVAL}

Let \(\mathsf M\in\mathbb R^{h\times {m_{\mathrm{col}}}}\),
\(1\le {m_{\mathrm{col}}}\le h\), have independent columns with
\(\mathsf M_{:,j}\sim\nu_j^{\otimes h}\), where the laws
\(\nu_j\in\{\mu,N(0,1)\}\) are fixed in advance. Write
\(s_1(\mathsf M)\ge\cdots\ge s_{m_{\mathrm{col}}}(\mathsf M)\ge0\) for
its singular values. There are positive constants
\(t_{\mathrm{en}},C_{\mathrm{en}},a_{\mathrm{sing}},
 c_{\mathrm{sing}},\alpha_{\mathrm{sing}}\), depending only on \(\mu\),
with the following properties.

\Needspace{6\baselineskip}
\textbf{(i)} For every integer \(1\le b\le {m_{\mathrm{col}}}\), \begin{equation}
\mathbb E\exp\left(t_{\mathrm{en}}\sum_{j=1}^bs_j(\mathsf M)^2\right)
 \le e^{C_{\mathrm{en}}(h+{m_{\mathrm{col}}})b}.
 \label{l:eq:G.4}
\end{equation}
\Needspace{7\baselineskip}
\noindent\textbf{(ii)} If \({m_{\mathrm{col}}}\le\alpha_{\mathrm{sing}}h\),
then the count \[
 N_{\mathrm{small}}:=\#\{1\le j\le {m_{\mathrm{col}}}:
               s_j(\mathsf M)<a_{\mathrm{sing}}\sqrt h\}
\] satisfies \begin{equation}
\mathbb P\{N_{\mathrm{small}}\ge b\}\le3e^{-c_{\mathrm{sing}}hb}
 \quad(1\le b\le {m_{\mathrm{col}}}).
 \label{l:eq:G.5}
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-20 -->
<!-- proof-32: body_md -->
\textbf{Energy estimate.} We use a
net argument for the mixed-column model; see also
\cite[Chapters 2 and 4]{V2018}. Choose \(v_\mu\ge1\) so that both
scalar laws obey
\(\mathbb E e^{t\xi}\le e^{v_\mu t^2/2}\). Gaussian linearization gives,
for a fixed orthonormal \({m_{\mathrm{col}}}\times b\) frame \(U\), \[
 \mathbb E e^{s\|\mathsf M U\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2}
 \le(1-2v_\mu s)^{-hb/2}\quad(0<s<(2v_\mu)^{-1}).
\] The Stiefel manifold has an operator-norm \(\eta\)-net \(\mathcal N\)
of size at most \((1+2/\eta)^{{m_{\mathrm{col}}}b}\), by disjoint-ball
volume comparison in the ambient normed space. For any
\({m_{\mathrm{col}}}\times b\) matrix \(\Delta\),
\(\|\mathsf M\Delta\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\le
(\sum_{j=1}^bs_j(\mathsf M)^2)^{1/2}\|\Delta\|_{\mathrm{op}}\).
Approximating a maximizing frame by a \(1/4\)-net therefore gives \[
 \sum_{j=1}^bs_j(\mathsf M)^2\le(16/9)\max_{U\in\mathcal N}
 \|\mathsf M U\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2.
\] Summing the fixed-frame moment bound over this net proves \eqref{l:eq:G.4}.
Consequently, for every fixed \(t_{\mathrm{tail}}>0\), some
\(K_{t_{\mathrm{tail}}}(\mu)\) satisfies
\(\mathbb P\{\sum_{j=1}^bs_j(\mathsf M)^2>K_{t_{\mathrm{tail}}}hb\}
\le e^{-t_{\mathrm{tail}}hb}\).

\Needspace{6\baselineskip}
\textbf{Small-singular-value count.} For a fixed frame \(U\), \eqref{l:eq:G.1}
applied independently to the \(h\) rows bounds the density of
\(\mathsf M U\) by \(C_\mu^{hb}\). Hence \[
 \mathbb P\{\|\mathsf M U\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\le a\sqrt{hb}\}
 \le(C'_\mu a)^{hb}\le e^{-2hb}
\] for a sufficiently small \(a>0\). Set \(a_{\mathrm{sing}}=a/2\) and
\(t_{\mathrm{tail}}=1\). On the energy event
\(\sum_{j=1}^bs_j(\mathsf M)^2\le K_1hb\), any frame of \(b\) small
singular directions has a net approximant with image norm at most
\(a\sqrt{hb}\), provided \(\eta\sqrt{K_1}\le a/2\). Fix such a net and
choose \(\alpha_{\mathrm{sing}}\) so that
\({m_{\mathrm{col}}}\log(1+2/\eta)\le h\). Its size is then at most
\(e^{hb}\). The union bound over the net and the energy complement prove
\eqref{l:eq:G.5}.
<!-- end proof-32 -->
<!-- head-3: body_md -->
\label{l:proof-of-the-capped-coordinate-weight-bound}

We separate small singular directions from the rest. Their contribution
to the total coordinate mass is at most their number; the remaining
directions are controlled by independent row energies. This is why the
preceding lemma estimates a count, rather than only the least singular
value.

\begin{proof}[\proofname\ of Lemma \ref{l:thm:GE}]\textbf{Reduce to two exponential
moments.} Write
\(s_1(\mathsf M)\ge\cdots\ge s_{m_{\mathrm{col}}}(\mathsf M)\ge0\) for
the singular values, with orthonormal left and right singular vectors
\(\mathbf u_j\in\mathbb R^h\), \(\mathbf v_j\in\mathbb R^{m_{\mathrm{col}}}\),
satisfying \(\mathsf M\mathbf v_j=s_j\mathbf u_j\). Take
\(a_{\mathrm{sing}},c_{\mathrm{sing}},\alpha_{\mathrm{sing}}\) from
Lemma \ref{l:thm:SVAL}. Write \[
 N_{\mathrm{small}}=\#\{j:s_j(\mathsf M)<a_{\mathrm{sing}}\sqrt h\},
 \qquad q_i=\sum_{j=1}^{m_{\mathrm{col}}}\mathsf M_{ij}^2.
\] Define the contribution of the small positive singular directions by
\[
 b_i:=\sum_{\substack{1\le j\le {m_{\mathrm{col}}}\\0<s_j<a_{\mathrm{sing}}\sqrt h}}
                  |(\mathbf u_j)_i|^2,\qquad 1\le i\le h.
\] This is the \(i\)-th diagonal of the orthogonal projection onto their
span. Since every \(\mathbf u_j\) has norm one, \[
 \begin{aligned}
 \sum_{i=1}^h b_i
 &=\sum_{\substack{1\le j\le {m_{\mathrm{col}}}\\0<s_j<a_{\mathrm{sing}}\sqrt h}}
                         \sum_{i=1}^h|(\mathbf u_j)_i|^2\\
 &=\#\{1\le j\le {m_{\mathrm{col}}}:0<s_j<a_{\mathrm{sing}}\sqrt h\}
 \le N_{\mathrm{small}}.
 \end{aligned}
\] The inequality allows for zero singular values, which are counted by
\(N_{\mathrm{small}}\) but do not contribute to the range.

The left singular vectors with \(s_j>0\) form an orthonormal basis of
\(\operatorname{Ran}\mathsf M\). Consequently, \[
 P_{\operatorname{Ran}\mathsf M}
   =\sum_{\substack{1\le j\le {m_{\mathrm{col}}}\\s_j>0}}\mathbf u_j\mathbf u_j^T,
 \qquad
 \ell_i=b_i+\sum_{\substack{1\le j\le {m_{\mathrm{col}}}\\s_j\ge a_{\mathrm{sing}}\sqrt h}}
                               |(\mathbf u_j)_i|^2.
\] For these remaining directions, substitute
\((\mathbf u_j)_i=(\mathsf M\mathbf v_j)_i/s_j\) and use the lower bound on \(s_j\): \[
 \begin{aligned}
 \ell_i-b_i
 &=\sum_{\substack{1\le j\le {m_{\mathrm{col}}}\\s_j\ge a_{\mathrm{sing}}\sqrt h}}
                   \frac{|(\mathsf M\mathbf v_j)_i|^2}{s_j^2}\\
 &\le\frac{1}{a_{\mathrm{sing}}^2h}
       \sum_{\substack{1\le j\le {m_{\mathrm{col}}}\\s_j\ge a_{\mathrm{sing}}\sqrt h}}
                   |(\mathsf M\mathbf v_j)_i|^2\\
 &\le\frac{1}{a_{\mathrm{sing}}^2h}
                   \sum_{q=1}^{m_{\mathrm{col}}}|\mathsf M_{iq}|^2
 =\frac{q_i}{a_{\mathrm{sing}}^2h}.
 \end{aligned}
\] The last inequality is Bessel's inequality applied to the \(i\)-th
row: \((\mathsf M\mathbf v_j)_i\) is its component along \(\mathbf v_j\), and the
squared components along any subset of these orthonormal directions sum
to at most the squared row norm \(q_i\). Using
\(\min(x+y,\delta)^{3/2}\le
C\sqrt\delta\,x+C\min(y,\delta)^{3/2}\), we obtain \begin{equation}
\sum_{i=1}^h\min(\ell_i,\delta)^{3/2}
 \le C\sqrt\delta N_{\mathrm{small}}
       +C\sum_{i=1}^h\min(q_i/(a_{\mathrm{sing}}^2h),\delta)^{3/2}.
 \label{l:eq:G.7}
\end{equation} Set \(D_\theta=2C\theta\) and define \[
 g_h(y)=D_\theta h\min\left\{\frac{y}{a_{\mathrm{sing}}^2h},\delta\right\}^{3/2}.
\] Cauchy--Schwarz, followed by independence of the rows, now gives \[
 \begin{aligned}
 \log\mathbb E e^{\theta h\sum_{i=1}^h\min(\ell_i,\delta)^{3/2}}
 &\le\frac12\log\mathbb E e^{D_\theta h\sqrt\delta N_{\mathrm{small}}}
       +\frac12\sum_{i=1}^h\log\mathbb E e^{g_h(q_i)}.
 \end{aligned}
\] We control the singular-count term first, then the row terms.

\Needspace{6\baselineskip}
\textbf{The singular-count term.} Choose \(\delta\) small enough that
\(D_\theta\sqrt\delta\le c_{\mathrm{sing}}/2\). Summing \eqref{l:eq:G.5} over
integer levels gives \[
 \mathbb E e^{D_\theta h\sqrt\delta N_{\mathrm{small}}}
 \le1+3\sum_{b=1}^{m_{\mathrm{col}}} e^{-c_{\mathrm{sing}}hb/2}
 \le1+C_\mu e^{-c_\mu h}.
\] Thus the first logarithmic moment is \(O_\mu(e^{-c_\mu h})\).

\Needspace{6\baselineskip}
\textbf{The row terms and conclusion.} A common square-exponential
moment gives \(\lambda_\mu,K_{\mathrm{row}}>0\) such that \[
 \mathbb P\{q_i>y\}\le e^{-\lambda_\mu y/2}
 \qquad(y\ge K_{\mathrm{row}}{m_{\mathrm{col}}}).
\] Decreasing \(\delta\) if necessary ensures
\(g_h(y)\le\lambda_\mu y/4\); also
\(0\le g_h'(y)\le C_{\mu,\theta}\sqrt{y/h}\) almost everywhere. With
\(r_0=K_{\mathrm{row}}{m_{\mathrm{col}}}\), tail integration gives \[
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E e^{g_h(q_i)}
 &\le e^{g_h(r_0)}+
       \int_{r_0}^{\infty}g_h'(y)e^{g_h(y)}\mathbb P\{q_i>y\}\,dy\\
 &\le \exp\{C_{\mu,\theta}{m_{\mathrm{col}}}^{3/2}/\sqrt h\}
       +\frac{C_{\mu,\theta}}{\sqrt h}
          \int_0^\infty\sqrt y\,e^{-\lambda_\mu y/4}\,dy.
 \end{aligned}
\] The integral is finite. Since \({m_{\mathrm{col}}}\ge1\) and
\(\log(e^x+u)\le x+u\) for \(x,u\ge0\), this implies
\(\log\mathbb E e^{g_h(q_i)}\le C_{\mu,\theta}{m_{\mathrm{col}}}^{3/2}/\sqrt h\).
Substitution into the initial moment decomposition gives a total of
\(C_{\mu,\theta}\sqrt h\,{m_{\mathrm{col}}}^{3/2}+O_\mu(e^{-c_\mu h})\).
Since \(\sqrt h\,{m_{\mathrm{col}}}^{3/2}\ge1\), the remainder is
absorbed. Taking \(\alpha_\theta\le\alpha_{\mathrm{sing}}\) and
\(\delta_\theta\) small enough for both cap conditions proves \eqref{l:eq:G.6}.
\end{proof}
<!-- end head-3 -->
<!-- proof-33: body_md -->
\textbf{Reduce to two exponential
moments.} Write
\(s_1(\mathsf M)\ge\cdots\ge s_{m_{\mathrm{col}}}(\mathsf M)\ge0\) for
the singular values, with orthonormal left and right singular vectors
\(\mathbf u_j\in\mathbb R^h\), \(\mathbf v_j\in\mathbb R^{m_{\mathrm{col}}}\),
satisfying \(\mathsf M\mathbf v_j=s_j\mathbf u_j\). Take
\(a_{\mathrm{sing}},c_{\mathrm{sing}},\alpha_{\mathrm{sing}}\) from
Lemma \ref{l:thm:SVAL}. Write \[
 N_{\mathrm{small}}=\#\{j:s_j(\mathsf M)<a_{\mathrm{sing}}\sqrt h\},
 \qquad q_i=\sum_{j=1}^{m_{\mathrm{col}}}\mathsf M_{ij}^2.
\] Define the contribution of the small positive singular directions by
\[
 b_i:=\sum_{\substack{1\le j\le {m_{\mathrm{col}}}\\0<s_j<a_{\mathrm{sing}}\sqrt h}}
                  |(\mathbf u_j)_i|^2,\qquad 1\le i\le h.
\] This is the \(i\)-th diagonal of the orthogonal projection onto their
span. Since every \(\mathbf u_j\) has norm one, \[
 \begin{aligned}
 \sum_{i=1}^h b_i
 &=\sum_{\substack{1\le j\le {m_{\mathrm{col}}}\\0<s_j<a_{\mathrm{sing}}\sqrt h}}
                         \sum_{i=1}^h|(\mathbf u_j)_i|^2\\
 &=\#\{1\le j\le {m_{\mathrm{col}}}:0<s_j<a_{\mathrm{sing}}\sqrt h\}
 \le N_{\mathrm{small}}.
 \end{aligned}
\] The inequality allows for zero singular values, which are counted by
\(N_{\mathrm{small}}\) but do not contribute to the range.

The left singular vectors with \(s_j>0\) form an orthonormal basis of
\(\operatorname{Ran}\mathsf M\). Consequently, \[
 P_{\operatorname{Ran}\mathsf M}
   =\sum_{\substack{1\le j\le {m_{\mathrm{col}}}\\s_j>0}}\mathbf u_j\mathbf u_j^T,
 \qquad
 \ell_i=b_i+\sum_{\substack{1\le j\le {m_{\mathrm{col}}}\\s_j\ge a_{\mathrm{sing}}\sqrt h}}
                               |(\mathbf u_j)_i|^2.
\] For these remaining directions, substitute
\((\mathbf u_j)_i=(\mathsf M\mathbf v_j)_i/s_j\) and use the lower bound on \(s_j\): \[
 \begin{aligned}
 \ell_i-b_i
 &=\sum_{\substack{1\le j\le {m_{\mathrm{col}}}\\s_j\ge a_{\mathrm{sing}}\sqrt h}}
                   \frac{|(\mathsf M\mathbf v_j)_i|^2}{s_j^2}\\
 &\le\frac{1}{a_{\mathrm{sing}}^2h}
       \sum_{\substack{1\le j\le {m_{\mathrm{col}}}\\s_j\ge a_{\mathrm{sing}}\sqrt h}}
                   |(\mathsf M\mathbf v_j)_i|^2\\
 &\le\frac{1}{a_{\mathrm{sing}}^2h}
                   \sum_{q=1}^{m_{\mathrm{col}}}|\mathsf M_{iq}|^2
 =\frac{q_i}{a_{\mathrm{sing}}^2h}.
 \end{aligned}
\] The last inequality is Bessel's inequality applied to the \(i\)-th
row: \((\mathsf M\mathbf v_j)_i\) is its component along \(\mathbf v_j\), and the
squared components along any subset of these orthonormal directions sum
to at most the squared row norm \(q_i\). Using
\(\min(x+y,\delta)^{3/2}\le
C\sqrt\delta\,x+C\min(y,\delta)^{3/2}\), we obtain \begin{equation}
\sum_{i=1}^h\min(\ell_i,\delta)^{3/2}
 \le C\sqrt\delta N_{\mathrm{small}}
       +C\sum_{i=1}^h\min(q_i/(a_{\mathrm{sing}}^2h),\delta)^{3/2}.
 \label{l:eq:G.7}
\end{equation} Set \(D_\theta=2C\theta\) and define \[
 g_h(y)=D_\theta h\min\left\{\frac{y}{a_{\mathrm{sing}}^2h},\delta\right\}^{3/2}.
\] Cauchy--Schwarz, followed by independence of the rows, now gives \[
 \begin{aligned}
 \log\mathbb E e^{\theta h\sum_{i=1}^h\min(\ell_i,\delta)^{3/2}}
 &\le\frac12\log\mathbb E e^{D_\theta h\sqrt\delta N_{\mathrm{small}}}
       +\frac12\sum_{i=1}^h\log\mathbb E e^{g_h(q_i)}.
 \end{aligned}
\] We control the singular-count term first, then the row terms.

\Needspace{6\baselineskip}
\textbf{The singular-count term.} Choose \(\delta\) small enough that
\(D_\theta\sqrt\delta\le c_{\mathrm{sing}}/2\). Summing \eqref{l:eq:G.5} over
integer levels gives \[
 \mathbb E e^{D_\theta h\sqrt\delta N_{\mathrm{small}}}
 \le1+3\sum_{b=1}^{m_{\mathrm{col}}} e^{-c_{\mathrm{sing}}hb/2}
 \le1+C_\mu e^{-c_\mu h}.
\] Thus the first logarithmic moment is \(O_\mu(e^{-c_\mu h})\).

\Needspace{6\baselineskip}
\textbf{The row terms and conclusion.} A common square-exponential
moment gives \(\lambda_\mu,K_{\mathrm{row}}>0\) such that \[
 \mathbb P\{q_i>y\}\le e^{-\lambda_\mu y/2}
 \qquad(y\ge K_{\mathrm{row}}{m_{\mathrm{col}}}).
\] Decreasing \(\delta\) if necessary ensures
\(g_h(y)\le\lambda_\mu y/4\); also
\(0\le g_h'(y)\le C_{\mu,\theta}\sqrt{y/h}\) almost everywhere. With
\(r_0=K_{\mathrm{row}}{m_{\mathrm{col}}}\), tail integration gives \[
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E e^{g_h(q_i)}
 &\le e^{g_h(r_0)}+
       \int_{r_0}^{\infty}g_h'(y)e^{g_h(y)}\mathbb P\{q_i>y\}\,dy\\
 &\le \exp\{C_{\mu,\theta}{m_{\mathrm{col}}}^{3/2}/\sqrt h\}
       +\frac{C_{\mu,\theta}}{\sqrt h}
          \int_0^\infty\sqrt y\,e^{-\lambda_\mu y/4}\,dy.
 \end{aligned}
\] The integral is finite. Since \({m_{\mathrm{col}}}\ge1\) and
\(\log(e^x+u)\le x+u\) for \(x,u\ge0\), this implies
\(\log\mathbb E e^{g_h(q_i)}\le C_{\mu,\theta}{m_{\mathrm{col}}}^{3/2}/\sqrt h\).
Substitution into the initial moment decomposition gives a total of
\(C_{\mu,\theta}\sqrt h\,{m_{\mathrm{col}}}^{3/2}+O_\mu(e^{-c_\mu h})\).
Since \(\sqrt h\,{m_{\mathrm{col}}}^{3/2}\ge1\), the remainder is
absorbed. Taking \(\alpha_\theta\le\alpha_{\mathrm{sing}}\) and
\(\delta_\theta\) small enough for both cap conditions proves \eqref{l:eq:G.6}.
<!-- end proof-33 -->
<!-- lemma-21: body_md -->
\label{l:thm:LIKE}

For a response matrix \(\mathbf z\in\mathbb R^{d\times k}\), write
\(\mathbf z^{(i)}=(z_{i1},\ldots,z_{ik})^T\in\mathbb R^k\) for its
\(i\)-th row as a column vector. The response likelihood
\(L_c=dP_c/dG_0\) is \begin{equation}
L_c(\mathbf z)=\prod_{i=1}^d\frac{p_{U_c(\mathbf z)}(\mathbf z^{(i)})}{\phi_k(\mathbf z^{(i)})},
 \qquad\phi_k(y)=(2\pi)^{-k/2}e^{-\|y\|^2/2}.
 \label{l:eq:A.5}
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-21 -->
<!-- proof-34: body_md -->
At step \(a\), let
\(\mathbf r\in\mathbb R^{d\times(a-1)}\) be the preceding response
matrix and \(\mathbf V\in\mathbb R^{d\times(h-a+1)}\) the unrevealed
coordinates of the source rows. Using a fixed Borel completion, the next
query determines an orthogonal matrix
\(O_a(\mathbf r)\in\mathbb R^{(h-a+1)\times(h-a+1)}\) whose first row is
