that query's coordinate vector in the remaining subspace. The change of
variables is \[
 (\mathbf r,\mathbf V)\longmapsto
       (\mathbf r,\mathbf V O_a(\mathbf r)^T).
\] The first column of the transformed array is the next response. For
each fixed history \(\mathbf r\), the map rotates each row of
\(\mathbf V\) orthogonally. Tonelli therefore shows that it preserves
the full Lebesgue integral, even for Borel update rules. After \(k\)
queries, \(U_c(\mathbf z)\) has \(k\) orthonormal columns. Fixing the
responses \(\mathbf z\) leaves each source row \(\mathbf x_i=X_{i,:}^T\)
in the \((h-k)\)-dimensional affine fiber \[
 \mathbf x_i=U_c(\mathbf z)\mathbf z^{(i)}+\mathbf w_i,\qquad
 \mathbf w_i\in(\operatorname{Ran}U_c(\mathbf z))^\perp.
\] Integrating over the unobserved coordinates \(\mathbf w_i\), rather
than fixing them to zero, gives the marginal response density and hence
\eqref{l:eq:A.4} and \eqref{l:eq:A.5}. For Gaussian sources, \(p_U=\phi_k\) pointwise, so
the responses are independent standard Gaussians.
<!-- end proof-34 -->
<!-- lemma-22: body_md -->
\label{l:thm:ENV}

There are \(C_{\mathrm{env}},\delta_{\mathrm{env}}>0\), depending only
on \(\mu\), such that, for every \(U\in\mathbb R^{h\times k}\) with
\(U^TU=\mathrm{Id}_k\) and coordinate weights
\(\ell_j=\sum_{a=1}^k U_{ja}^2\le\delta_{\mathrm{env}}\) for \(1\le j\le h\),
every \(y\in\mathbb R^k\), and
every \(\mathbf t=(t_1,\ldots,t_h)^T\in\operatorname{Ran}U\), \begin{equation}
\log p_U(y)\le-\tfrac k2\log(2\pi)-\langle\mathbf t,Uy\rangle
 +\sum_{j=1}^h\Lambda_\mu(t_j)
 +C_{\mathrm{env}}\sum_{j=1}^h(\ell_j^{3/2}+\sqrt{\ell_j}t_j^2).
 \label{l:eq:A.7}
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-22 -->
<!-- lemma-23: body_md -->
\label{l:thm:FTILT}

There exist
\(\delta_{\mathrm{Four}}=\delta_{\mathrm{Four}}(\mu)\in(0,1/4]\) and
\(C_{\mathrm{Four}}=C_{\mathrm{Four}}(\mu)>0\) such that the
characteristic functions \(\chi_t\) defined above satisfy \begin{equation}
c\log\left[(2\pi)^{-1/2}
        \int_{\mathbb R}|\chi_t(\sqrt c\,z)|^{1/c}\,dz\right]
 \le C_{\mathrm{Four}}(c^{3/2}+\sqrt c\,t^2),
 \quad 0<c\le\delta_{\mathrm{Four}},\quad t\in\mathbb R.
 \label{l:eq:F.1}
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-23 -->
<!-- proof-35: body_md -->
Plancherel and \(f^2\le K_f f\) give \begin{equation}
\|\chi_t\|_2^2
 \le2\pi K_f e^{\Lambda_\mu(2t)-2\Lambda_\mu(t)}
 \le C_\mu e^{C_\mu t^2}.
 \label{l:eq:F.2}
\end{equation} On a sufficiently small fixed tilt interval \(|t|\le t_0\),
\(v(t)=\Lambda_\mu''(t)=1+O(|t|)\ge3/4\), and the tilted fourth moments
are uniformly bounded. These statements follow by differentiating under
the integral, dominated by the square-exponential moment on each compact
tilt interval. For independent \(Y,Y'\) of density \(f_t\), the
elementary cosine bound gives \[
 |\chi_t(u)|^2=\mathbb E\cos(u(Y-Y'))
 \le\exp\{-v(t)u^2+C_\mu u^4\}.
\] Choose a small fixed \(u_0>0\). On \(|z|\le u_0/\sqrt c\), comparison
with \(e^{-v(t)z^2/2}\) shows that the positive excess of
\(|\chi_t(\sqrt c z)|^{1/c}\) over this Gaussian is bounded by
\(C_\mu c z^4e^{-b_\mu z^2}\). To see the integrable decay, choose
\(u_0\) so the quartic remainder is at most a fixed fraction of the
quadratic term, then use \(e^v-1\le ve^v\). Its integral is \(O(c)\).

Absolute continuity implies \(|\chi_0(u)|<1\) for \(u\ne0\): equality
would force \(e^{iu\xi}\) to be constant almost surely, placing the law
on a countable set. The Riemann--Lebesgue lemma and compactness give
\(\sup_{|u|\ge u_0}|\chi_0(u)|<1\). Also
\(\|\chi_t-\chi_0\|_\infty\le\|f_t-f\|_1\to0\). Decreasing \(t_0\)
yields a common bound \(\rho_\mu<1\) on this region. By \eqref{l:eq:F.2}, the
remaining integral is at most \[
 c^{-1/2}\rho_\mu^{1/c-2}\|\chi_t\|_2^2=O(c).
\] The last assertion follows, for instance, by
\(e^{\lambda/c}\ge\lambda^2/(2c^2)\), \(\lambda=-\log\rho_\mu>0\). We
have proved \[
 (2\pi)^{-1/2}\int|\chi_t(\sqrt c z)|^{1/c}\,dz
 \le v(t)^{-1/2}+C_\mu c\le e^{C_\mu(c+|t|)}
 \qquad(|t|\le t_0).
\] Multiplying its logarithm by \(c\), and using \(c^2\le c^{3/2}\) and
\(c|t|\le(c^{3/2}+\sqrt c\,t^2)/2\), proves \eqref{l:eq:F.1} on this interval.

For \(|t|>t_0\), use \(|\chi_t|\le1\), \(1/c\ge2\) and \eqref{l:eq:F.2}: the same
normalized integral is at most \(C_\mu c^{-1/2}e^{C_\mu t^2}\). Its
logarithm times \(c\) is at most \(C'_\mu\sqrt c\,t^2\), since \(t_0>0\)
is fixed and \(c\log(1/\sqrt c)\le\sqrt c\). This completes \eqref{l:eq:F.1}.
<!-- end proof-35 -->
<!-- proof-36: body_md -->
Fix \(U\in\mathbb R^{h\times k}\) with
\(U^TU=\mathrm{Id}_k\), \(\ell_j\le\delta_{\mathrm{Four}}\) for \(1\le j\le h\), and
\(\mathbf t\in\operatorname{Ran}U\). The tilted fiber density is \[
 p_{U,\mathbf t}(y)=\int_{\ker U^T}
             \prod_{j=1}^h f_{t_j}((Uy+w)_j)\,dw.
\]

\Needspace{6\baselineskip}
\textbf{Continuity of the fiber density.} Write \(r_j=(1-\ell_j)^{-1}\) for \(1\le j\le h\).
Boundedness of \(f\) gives \(\int f_{t_j}^{r_j}\le K_f^{r_j-1}
 e^{\Lambda_\mu(r_jt_j)-r_j\Lambda_\mu(t_j)}<\infty\). The
kernel-coordinate Brascamp--Lieb calculation in the proof of Lemma \ref{l:thm:GP},
applied to the telescoping difference of two products, gives \[
 \begin{aligned}
 |p_{U,\mathbf t}(y+v)-p_{U,\mathbf t}(y)|
 &\le C_U\sum_{j=1}^h
 \|f_{t_j}(\cdot+(Uv)_j)-f_{t_j}\|_{r_j}
       \prod_{\substack{1\le i\le h\\i\ne j}}\|f_{t_i}\|_{r_i},\\
 C_U&=\prod_{j=1}^h(1-\ell_j)^{-(1-\ell_j)/2}.
 \end{aligned}
\] The same inequality bounds each fiber integral by
\(C_U\prod_{j=1}^h\|f_{t_j}\|_{r_j}<\infty\). Translation continuity in
\(L^{r_j}\), with \(1\le r_j\le4/3\), therefore proves continuity at
every \(y\). This auxiliary bound is used only for continuity.

\Needspace{6\baselineskip}
\textbf{The Fourier bound.} Omit zero rows, whose
characteristic-function factors are \(|\chi_{t_j}(0)|=1\); products in
the following Brascamp--Lieb calculation run over \(1\le j\le h\) with
\(\ell_j>0\). Normalize each remaining row by setting
\(B_j=U_{j,:}/\sqrt{\ell_j}:\mathbb R^k\to\mathbb R\). Then \[
 B_jB_j^T=1,\qquad
 \sum_{\substack{1\le j\le h\\\ell_j>0}}\ell_j B_j^TB_j=\sum_{j=1}^hU_{j,:}^TU_{j,:}
 =U^TU=\mathrm{Id}_k,\qquad \sum_{j=1}^h\ell_j=k.
\] Thus the geometric Brascamp--Lieb weights are precisely \(\ell_j\).
For each \(1\le j\le h\) with \(\ell_j>0\), define \[
 H_j(s)=|\chi_{t_j}(\sqrt{\ell_j}\,s)|^{1/\ell_j},
 \qquad I_j=\int_{\mathbb R}H_j(s)\,ds.
\] These integrals are finite by \eqref{l:eq:F.2}, since \(1/\ell_j\ge2\). The
identity \(U_{j,:}z=\sqrt{\ell_j}B_jz\) gives \[
 |\chi_{t_j}(U_{j,:}z)|=H_j(B_jz)^{\ell_j}.
\] Consequently geometric Brascamp--Lieb
\cite[Proposition 2.8]{BCCT2008} yields \[
 \int_{\mathbb R^k}\prod_{j=1}^h|\chi_{t_j}(U_{j,:}z)|\,dz
 =\int_{\mathbb R^k}\prod_{\substack{1\le j\le h\\\ell_j>0}}H_j(B_jz)^{\ell_j}\,dz
 \le\prod_{\substack{1\le j\le h\\\ell_j>0}}\left(\int_{\mathbb R}H_j(s)\,ds\right)^{\ell_j}
 =\prod_{\substack{1\le j\le h\\\ell_j>0}}I_j^{\ell_j}.
\] For each \(1\le j\le h\) with \(\ell_j>0\), apply Lemma \ref{l:thm:FTILT} with \(c=\ell_j\) and \(t=t_j\): \[
 \ell_j\log\big[(2\pi)^{-1/2}I_j\big]
 \le C_{\mathrm{Four}}(\ell_j^{3/2}+\sqrt{\ell_j}t_j^2).
\] Sum these logarithmic bounds, use \(\sum_{j=1}^h\ell_j=k\), and
exponentiate to obtain \begin{equation}
\int_{\mathbb R^k}\prod_{j=1}^h|\chi_{t_j}(U_{j,:}z)|\,dz
 \le(2\pi)^{k/2}
       \exp\left\{C_{\mathrm{Four}}\sum_{j=1}^h(\ell_j^{3/2}+\sqrt{\ell_j}t_j^2)\right\}.
 \label{l:eq:F.3}
\end{equation} Independence gives the product characteristic function below.
Its integrability in \eqref{l:eq:F.3} permits Fourier inversion
\cite[Chapter 8]{F1999}. The inverse is a continuous density; the
continuity just proved makes it agree with our specified fiber integral
at every point. Thus, for every \(y\in\mathbb R^k\),
\[
 p_{U,\mathbf t}(y)=\frac1{(2\pi)^k}
 \int_{\mathbb R^k}e^{-i\langle\boldsymbol\omega,y\rangle}
       \prod_{j=1}^h\chi_{t_j}(U_{j,:}\boldsymbol\omega)
       \,d\boldsymbol\omega.
\]

\Needspace{6\baselineskip}
\textbf{Undoing the tilt.} The definition of the scalar tilt gives
\(f(x)=e^{-t_jx+\Lambda_\mu(t_j)}f_{t_j}(x)\). Multiplying over
coordinates, \[
 \prod_{j=1}^h f((Uy+w)_j)
 =e^{-\langle\mathbf t,Uy+w\rangle+\sum_{j=1}^h\Lambda_\mu(t_j)}
       \prod_{j=1}^h f_{t_j}((Uy+w)_j).
\] Since \(w\in\ker U^T=(\operatorname{Ran}U)^\perp\),
\(\langle\mathbf t,w\rangle=0\). Thus the exponential factor is constant
along the fiber, and integrating in \(w\) yields \[
 p_U(y)=e^{-\langle\mathbf t,Uy\rangle+\sum_{j=1}^h\Lambda_\mu(t_j)}p_{U,\mathbf t}(y).
\] Taking absolute values in the inverse formula and using \eqref{l:eq:F.3} proves
\eqref{l:eq:A.7}, with \(C_{\mathrm{env}}=C_{\mathrm{Four}}\) and
\(\delta_{\mathrm{env}}=\delta_{\mathrm{Four}}\), simultaneously for all
frames and points.
<!-- end proof-36 -->
<!-- lemma-24: body_md -->
\label{l:thm:SEQCOUP}

Let \(G_0\) be the product standard Gaussian law on
\(\mathbb R^{d\times k}\), and let \(M\) be a probability law with
\(H(M\mid G_0)<\infty\). There is a coupling of a response matrix
\(\mathbf Z\sim M\) and independent vectors
\(\mathbf g_1,\ldots,\mathbf g_k\sim N(0,\mathrm{Id}_d)\) such that
\({\mathbf Z}_{:,\le j}\) is a Borel function of
\(\mathbf g_1,\ldots,\mathbf g_j\) for every \(j\), and \begin{equation}
\mathbb E\sum_{j=1}^k\|{\mathbf Z}_{:,j}-\mathbf g_j\|^2\le2H(M\mid G_0).
 \label{l:eq:C.1}
\end{equation} In particular, \(\mathbf g_j\) is independent of all preceding target
and Gaussian columns. Any query determined by \({\mathbf Z}_{:,<j}\) is
known before \(\mathbf g_j\) is revealed.
<!-- end lemma-24 -->
<!-- proof-37: body_md -->
Order the \(dk\) scalar coordinates by columns, and denote them by
\(\widehat Z_\ell,\widehat g_\ell\). Let \(\gamma=N(0,1)\), let \(\Phi\)
be its distribution function, and let \(K_\ell(z_{<\ell},\cdot)\) be
conditional laws under \(M\). Using independent
\(\widehat g_\ell\sim\gamma\), define recursively \[
 \widehat Z_\ell=
 F^{-1}_{K_\ell(\widehat Z_{<\ell},\cdot)}
       (\Phi(\widehat g_\ell)),\qquad 1\le\ell\le dk,
\] Here, for a probability law \(K\) on \(\mathbb R\),
\(F_K(x)=K((-\infty,x])\) and
\(F_K^{-1}(u)=\inf\{x:F_K(x)\ge u\}\), \(0<u<1\), are its
distribution function and generalized inverse.
The conditional quantile is jointly Borel, using rational thresholds in
its definition. The recursion has the prescribed conditional laws, hence
joint law \(M\). The one-dimensional Gaussian transport--entropy
inequality \cite{T1996} and the entropy chain rule
\cite[Section 1.4]{DE1997} give \[
 \mathbb E\sum_{\ell=1}^{dk}|\widehat Z_\ell-\widehat g_\ell|^2
 \le2\sum_{\ell=1}^{dk}\mathbb E
 H(K_\ell(\widehat Z_{<\ell},\cdot)\mid\gamma)
 =2H(M\mid G_0).
\] The conditional construction is also recorded in
\cite[Theorem 2.9 and Proposition 5.9]{BBLZ2017}. Finite total entropy
makes the conditional costs finite almost everywhere; null histories may
be assigned arbitrary probability kernels.

Regrouping coordinates into columns proves \eqref{l:eq:C.1}. The sequential
construction gives \[
 \sigma(\mathbf g_1,\ldots,\mathbf g_j,{\mathbf Z}_{:,\le j})=\sigma(\mathbf g_1,\ldots,\mathbf g_j),
\] so the next Gaussian column is independent of the entire past.
<!-- end proof-37 -->
<!-- lemma-25: body_md -->
\label{l:thm:GAUSS3}

Let \((\mathcal F_j)_{j=0}^k\) be a filtration, and let \(g_j\) be
\(\mathcal F_j\)-measurable scalar standard Gaussians
independent of \(\mathcal F_{j-1}\), and let bounded real \(a_j\) be
\(\mathcal F_{j-1}\)-measurable. For \(S_k=\sum_{j=1}^k a_jg_j\), \begin{equation}
\mathbb E|S_k|^3
 \le27\,\mathbb E\left(\sum_{j=1}^k a_j^2\right)^{3/2}.
 \label{l:eq:C.2}
\end{equation} Moreover, \[
 \mathbb E|S_k|^3+
 \mathbb E\left[\left(\sum_{j=1}^k a_j^2\right)^{1/2}|S_k|^2\right]
 \le46\,\mathbb E\left(\sum_{j=1}^k a_j^2\right)^{3/2}.
\] No independence between \(S_k\) and \(\sum_{j=1}^k a_j^2\) is
assumed.
<!-- end lemma-25 -->
<!-- proof-38: body_md -->
Write \(S_j=\sum_{a=1}^ja_ag_a\), \(V=\sum_{a=1}^k a_a^2\), and
\(S_* =\max_{0\le j\le k}|S_j|\). The elementary Taylor bound \[
 |x+y|^3\le |x|^3+3x|x|y+3|x|y^2+|y|^3
\] follows by integrating the second derivative \(6|x|\). Apply it to
each increment and take conditional expectations. The linear term
vanishes, \(\mathbb E|g_a|^3<2\), and
\(\sum_{a=1}^k|a_a|^3\le V^{3/2}\). Hence, with \(X=\mathbb E|S_k|^3\),
\(Y=\mathbb E V^{3/2}\), \[
 X\le3\mathbb E(S_*V)+2Y
 \le\frac92X^{1/3}Y^{2/3}+2Y.
\] The second inequality is H\"older together with Doob's \(L^3\) maximal
inequality \(\|S_*\|_3\le(3/2)\|S_k\|_3\). Young's inequality gives \[
 X\le\frac X3+
       \left[\frac23(9/2)^{3/2}+2\right]Y,
 \qquad X\le[(9/2)^{3/2}+3]Y\le13Y.
\] All moments are finite because the coefficients are bounded and the
sum is finite. This proves \eqref{l:eq:C.2}, with room in its stated constant.
Finally, \[
 \sqrt V|S_k|^2\le\tfrac13V^{3/2}+\tfrac23|S_k|^3
\] gives the second assertion; even the weaker constant \(27\) from
\eqref{l:eq:C.2} gives \(27+1/3+18<46\).
<!-- end proof-38 -->
<!-- proof-39: body_md -->
\leavevmode\par
The projection
bound \eqref{l:eq:G.1} and \eqref{l:eq:A.5} give
\(L_c(Y)\le e^{C_\mu dk+\|Y\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2/2}\). Under the original
source law, pointwise
\(\|XU\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\le\sum_{a=1}^k s_a(X)^2\). The energy estimate
\eqref{l:eq:G.4} of Lemma \ref{l:thm:SVAL}, applied to \(X\) when \(h\le d\), therefore gives,
for a sufficiently small fixed \(t>0\), \[
 \mathbb E_{G_0} L_c^{1+t}=\mathbb E_{P_c} L_c^t\le e^{C'_\mu dk}.
\] The equality first holds for nonnegative extended integrals; the
bound proves finiteness. H\"older yields \eqref{l:eq:A.2}. The moment estimate is
uniform in the fixed auxiliary input, so it also holds after averaging
over \(\mathsf C\).
<!-- end proof-39 -->
<!-- lemma-26: body_md -->
\label{l:thm:ENT}

There are \(C_{\mathrm{ent}}>0\) and \(\lambda\in(0,1)\), depending only
on \(\mu\), such that the following holds for every fixed auxiliary
input \(c\). Let \(M\ll G_0\) be a probability law on response matrices
\(\mathbb R^{d\times k}\), with \(H(M\mid G_0)<\infty\) and
\(\log L_c\in L^1(M)\), satisfying \[
 M\{\mathbf z:L_c(\mathbf z)>0,
       \max_{1\le j\le h}\ell_j(U_c(\mathbf z))\le\delta_\mu\}=1.
\] Then \begin{equation}
\mathbb E_M\log L_c\le C_{\mathrm{ent}}d\mathbb E_M\Theta(U_c)
                         +(1-\lambda)H(M\mid G_0).
 \label{l:eq:A.13}
\end{equation} Here
\(\mathbb E_M\Theta(U_c)=\int\Theta(U_c(\mathbf z))\,M(d\mathbf z)\).
The functions \(L_c,U_c\) are the same observation functions for every
choice of \(M\).
<!-- end lemma-26 -->
<!-- proof-40: body_md -->
Let
\(F\) satisfy the coordinate-weight cap in the theorem, and use its
section \(F_c\). First suppose \(P_c(F_c)>0\) and \(\log L_c\) is
bounded on \(F_c\). Now choose \(M=P_c(\cdot\mid F_c)\). Its density and
entropy are \[
 \frac{dM}{dG_0}=\frac{\mathbf1_{F_c}L_c}{P_c(F_c)},\qquad
 H(M\mid G_0)=\mathbb E_M\log L_c-\log P_c(F_c).
\] In particular, \(M\) meets the hypotheses of Lemma \ref{l:thm:ENT}. Combining
this identity with \eqref{l:eq:A.13} gives \begin{equation}
\log P_c(F_c)\le C_{\mathrm{ent}}d\mathbb E_M\Theta(U_c)
                                  -\lambda H(M\mid G_0).
 \label{l:eq:A.14}
\end{equation} Define the weighted Gaussian integral over \(F_c\) by \[
 I_F(c)=\int_{F_c}
       e^{C_{\mathrm{ent}}d\Theta(U_c(\mathbf z))/\lambda}\,G_0(d\mathbf z).
\] The entropy variational inequality (see \cite[Section 1.4]{DE1997})
here follows directly by applying Jensen's inequality under \(M\) to
\(e^{C_{\mathrm{ent}}d\Theta(U_c)/\lambda}/(dM/dG_0)\). Using
\(M(F_c)=1\), we obtain \[
 \frac{C_{\mathrm{ent}}d}{\lambda}\mathbb E_M\Theta(U_c)-H(M\mid G_0)
 \le\log I_F(c).
\] Thus \(P_c(F_c)\le I_F(c)^\lambda\). The integral is finite since
\(0\le\Theta\le k\).

For an arbitrary section \(F_c\), use
\(F_{c,m}=F_c\cap\{e^{-m}\le L_c\le e^m\}\). When \(P_c(F_{c,m})>0\),
its conditional law meets the preceding hypotheses; null sections
satisfy the bound automatically. Each truncated Gaussian integral is at
most \(I_F(c)\). Since \(P_c\{0<L_c<\infty\}=1\), taking increasing
limits gives \(P_c(F_c)\le I_F(c)^\lambda\) for every section satisfying
the cap.

Finally average the section probabilities over the common law of \(\mathsf C\).
Concavity gives \[
 \begin{aligned}
 P(F)&=\int_{\mathcal C}P_c(F_c)\,\mathcal L(\mathsf C)(dc)\\
 &\le\mathbb E_{\mathsf C}[I_F(\mathsf C)^\lambda]
       \le[\mathbb E_{\mathsf C} I_F(\mathsf C)]^\lambda\\
 &=\left[\mathbb E_G\mathbf1_F
       e^{C_{\mathrm{ent}}d\Theta(U)/\lambda}\right]^\lambda.
 \end{aligned}
\] This is \eqref{l:eq:A.3}, with \(\theta_\mu=\lambda\) and
\(C_{\mathrm{weight}}=C_{\mathrm{ent}}/\lambda\).
<!-- end proof-40 -->
<!-- proof-41: body_md -->
Fix \(c\) and a response law \(M\)
satisfying the hypotheses of the lemma.

\Needspace{6\baselineskip}
\textbf{Coupling and moments.} Use Lemma \ref{l:thm:SEQCOUP} to couple
\(\mathbf Z\sim M\) with independent standard Gaussian columns
\(\mathbf g_1,\ldots,\mathbf g_k\), and denote the coupling law by
\(\Pi\). Write \(\mathbb E_\Pi\) for expectation on this space. Every
query is evaluated on \(\mathbf Z\): write \(U=U_c(\mathbf Z)\),
\(\mathbf u_a=\mathbf u_a(c,{\mathbf Z}_{:,<a})\), and \(\ell_j=\ell_j(U)\). The
sequential coupling makes \(\mathbf u_a\) known before \(\mathbf g_a\) is
revealed. The matrix \(\mathbf Z\) is a response record with law \(M\);
the construction requires no source matrix. Define the \(d\times h\)
matrices \begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
 N&=\sum_{a=1}^k \mathbf g_a\mathbf u_a^T,&
 E_{\mathrm{err}}&=\sum_{a=1}^k({\mathbf Z}_{:,a}-\mathbf g_a)\mathbf u_a^T,\\
 \mathbf ZU^T&=N+E_{\mathrm{err}},&
 \mathbb E_\Pi\|E_{\mathrm{err}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
 &=\mathbb E_\Pi\sum_{a=1}^k\|{\mathbf Z}_{:,a}-\mathbf g_a\|^2\le2H(M\mid G_0).
 \end{aligned}
 \label{l:eq:A.11}
\end{equation} Thus \(N\) uses Gaussian responses in the same query frame, and
\(E_{\mathrm{err}}\) is the coupling error. The norm equality follows
pathwise from orthonormality. Each row of \(N\) and
\(E_{\mathrm{err}}\), transposed, lies in \(\operatorname{Ran}U\). Write
\(g_{ia}=(\mathbf g_a)_i\) for the scalar coordinates. For
\(1\le i\le d\) and \(1\le j\le h\), the sum
\(N_{ij}=\sum_{a=1}^k (\mathbf u_a)_jg_{ia}\) has predictable coefficients and
variance sum \(\sum_{a=1}^k (\mathbf u_a)_j^2=\ell_j\). Lemma \ref{l:thm:GAUSS3} gives \begin{equation}
\mathbb E_\Pi|N_{ij}|^3+\mathbb E_\Pi[\sqrt{\ell_j}|N_{ij}|^2]
 \le46\mathbb E_\Pi\ell_j^{3/2}.
 \label{l:eq:A.12}
\end{equation}

\Needspace{6\baselineskip}
\textbf{Choice of tilt and entropy bound.} Write \(C_\mu\) for positive
constants depending only on \(\mu\), which may increase below and are
independent of \(0<\lambda<1\). The local expansion of \(\Lambda_\mu\)
and \eqref{l:eq:A.6} give \begin{equation}
\mathcal R_\mu(t):=\Lambda_\mu(t)-t^2/2
 \le C_\mu\min\{|t|^3,t^2\}\qquad(t\in\mathbb R).
 \label{l:eq:A.8}
\end{equation} Using the cubic bound when \(|u|\le|x|\) and the quadratic bound
otherwise yields \begin{equation}
\mathcal R_\mu(x+u)\le C_\mu(|x|^3+u^2).
 \label{l:eq:A.9}
\end{equation}
We keep the Gaussian part of the tilt and include only a small fraction
of the coupling error. The decrease in the quadratic likelihood term is
linear to first order in \(\lambda\), while the added remainder cost is
quadratic.
For each \(1\le i\le d\), write \(y={\mathbf Z}_{i,:}^T\) and take \[
 \mathbf t_i=N_{i,:}^T+\lambda(E_{\mathrm{err}})_{i,:}^T
           \in\operatorname{Ran}U,
\] leaving \(\lambda\) to be chosen. The pointwise bound \eqref{l:eq:A.7} permits
this tilt on the coupling space. Subtracting
\(\log\phi_k(y)=-\tfrac k2\log(2\pi)-\|y\|^2/2\) and expanding
\(\Lambda_\mu(s)=s^2/2+\mathcal R_\mu(s)\) gives \[
 \begin{aligned}
 \log\frac{p_U(y)}{\phi_k(y)}
 \le{}&\frac{\|y\|^2}{2}-\langle\mathbf t_i,Uy\rangle
          +\frac{\|\mathbf t_i\|^2}{2}
          +\sum_{j=1}^h\mathcal R_\mu((\mathbf t_i)_j)\\
 &+C_{\mathrm{env}}\Theta(U)
          +C_{\mathrm{env}}\sum_{j=1}^h\sqrt{\ell_j}(\mathbf t_i)_j^2.
 \end{aligned}
\] The Gaussian normalizing constants cancel. Since
\(Uy=(N+E_{\mathrm{err}})_{i,:}^T\) and \(\|Uy\|=\|y\|\), the three
quadratic terms equal \[
 \frac12\|Uy-\mathbf t_i\|^2
 =\frac{(1-\lambda)^2}{2}\|(E_{\mathrm{err}})_{i,:}\|^2.
\] Apply \eqref{l:eq:A.9} with \(x=N_{ij}\), \(u=\lambda(E_{\mathrm{err}})_{ij}\).
Also use \((a+b)^2\le2a^2+2b^2\) and \(\sqrt{\ell_j}\le1\). The two
remaining sums satisfy \[
 \begin{aligned}
 \sum_{j=1}^h\mathcal R_\mu((\mathbf t_i)_j)
 &\le C_\mu\sum_{j=1}^h|N_{ij}|^3
       +C_\mu\lambda^2\|(E_{\mathrm{err}})_{i,:}\|^2,\\
 \sum_{j=1}^h\sqrt{\ell_j}(\mathbf t_i)_j^2
 &\le2\sum_{j=1}^h\sqrt{\ell_j}N_{ij}^2
       +2\lambda^2\|(E_{\mathrm{err}})_{i,:}\|^2.
 \end{aligned}
\] Substitution gives, for a fixed sufficiently large \(C_\mu\ge1\), \[
 \log\frac{p_U(y)}{\phi_k(y)}
 \le C_\mu\!\left[\Theta(U)+\sum_{j=1}^h
         (|N_{ij}|^3+\sqrt{\ell_j}N_{ij}^2)\right]
       +a_\lambda\|(E_{\mathrm{err}})_{i,:}\|^2,
\] where the error coefficient is bounded by \begin{equation}
a_\lambda:=\frac12-\lambda+C_\mu\lambda^2
       \le\frac{1-\lambda}{2}<\frac12
       \quad\text{for sufficiently small fixed }\lambda\in(0,1).
 \label{l:eq:A.10}
\end{equation} Fix such a \(\lambda\), depending only on \(\mu\).

Take logarithms of the product \eqref{l:eq:A.5} and sum the row bounds. Since the frame is common to all rows,
\(\Theta(U)\) appears exactly \(d\) times, while
\(\sum_{i=1}^d\|(E_{\mathrm{err}})_{i,:}\|^2
=\|E_{\mathrm{err}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\). Thus \[
 \begin{aligned}
 \log L_c(\mathbf Z)
 &=\sum_{i=1}^d\log\frac{p_U({\mathbf Z}_{i,:}^T)}
                              {\phi_k({\mathbf Z}_{i,:}^T)}\\
 &\le C_\mu d\Theta(U)
       +C_\mu\sum_{i=1}^d\sum_{j=1}^h
           (|N_{ij}|^3+\sqrt{\ell_j}N_{ij}^2)
       +\frac{1-\lambda}{2}\|E_{\mathrm{err}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2.
 \end{aligned}
\] Take \(\mathbb E_\Pi\) and apply \eqref{l:eq:A.11}--\eqref{l:eq:A.12}. Since
\(\mathbf Z\sim M\), expectations of functions of \(\mathbf Z\) alone
are \(\mathbb E_M\). The coupling cost \(2H(M\mid G_0)\) turns the error
coefficient \((1-\lambda)/2\) into \(1-\lambda\), yielding \eqref{l:eq:A.13} with a
law-dependent \(C_{\mathrm{ent}}\). All choices are uniform in the
auxiliary input \(c\) and the law \(M\).
<!-- end proof-41 -->
<!-- lemma-27: body_md -->
\label{l:thm:CGEO}

If \(x\in\mathbb C^h\) has independent entries with planar densities
\(f_j\le K\), then, for every complex coisometry
\(L:\mathbb C^h\to\mathbb C^q\), meaning \(LL^*=\mathrm{Id}_q\),
the canonical projection density satisfies \begin{equation}
\int_{\ker L}\prod_j f_j((L^*y)_j+w_j)\,dw\le(eK)^q
 \quad\text{for every }y\in\mathbb C^q.
 \label{l:eq:Z.3}
\end{equation} For independent-entry matrices whose column laws are \(\mu\) or
\(\mathcal N_{\mathbb C}(0,1)\), the conclusions of Corollary \ref{l:thm:IGRAM} and
Lemmas \ref{l:thm:GV} and \ref{l:thm:GE} hold with \(\mathsf G=\mathsf M^*\mathsf M\), complex ranges,
and law-dependent constants. The energy bound \eqref{l:eq:G.4} holds as well.
<!-- end lemma-27 -->
<!-- proof-42: body_md -->
Write \(c_j=(P_{\ker L})_{jj}\). The normalized coordinate maps on
\(\ker L\), with weights \(c_j>0\), are geometric Brascamp--Lieb data
with two-dimensional real targets. The calculation in
Lemma~\ref{l:thm:GP}, using \cite[Proposition 2.8]{BCCT2008}, becomes
\[
 \int_{\ker L}\prod_{j=1}^h f_j((L^*y)_j+w_j)\,dw
 \le K^q\prod_{\substack{1\le j\le h\\c_j>0}}c_j^{-c_j}
 \le(eK)^q,
 \qquad \sum_{j=1}^h(1-c_j)=q.
\]
The volume of a complex \(q\)-dimensional ball and tail integration give
\begin{equation}
\mathbb P\{\operatorname{dist}(x,F)\le u\sqrt q\}
 \le\min\{1,(C_Ku)^{2q}\},\qquad
 \|\operatorname{dist}(x,F)^{-2}\|_{L^p}\le C'_K/q
 \quad(1\le p\le q/4),
 \label{l:eq:Z.4}
\end{equation}
for every fixed complex subspace \(F\) of codimension \(q\).
Conditioning on successive columns gives Corollary~\ref{l:thm:IGRAM}.
For Lemma~\ref{l:thm:GV}, the required complementary-minor and
positive-Gram identities also hold for Hermitian Grams.

For Lemma~\ref{l:thm:SVAL}, let \(U\) be a complex \(b\)-frame and
\(v\) a common subgaussian constant for the two column laws. The
fixed-frame estimates and an \(\eta\)-net \(\mathcal N_\eta\) satisfy
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E e^{s\|\mathsf M U\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2}
 &\le(1-2vs)^{-hb},\qquad 0<s<(2v)^{-1},\\
 |\mathcal N_\eta|&\le(1+2/\eta)^{2m_{\mathrm{col}}b},\qquad
 \mathbb P\{\|\mathsf M U\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\le a\sqrt{hb}\}
 \le(Ca)^{2hb}.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Both the net exponent and the small-ball exponent double. The
maximizing-frame argument proves \eqref{l:eq:G.4} for
\(m_{\mathrm{col}}\le h\); the small-ball net argument proves
\eqref{l:eq:G.5} after reducing the aspect ratio.
Finally the singular-value decomposition and Bessel bound
\eqref{l:eq:G.7} are Hermitian identities. With
\(q_i=\sum_{j=1}^{m_{\mathrm{col}}}|\mathsf M_{ij}|^2\), the
small-singular-value count and row-energy tail integration in
Lemma~\ref{l:thm:GE} give the coordinate-weight bound.
<!-- end proof-42 -->
<!-- lemma-28: body_md -->
\label{l:thm:CENV}

There are \(C>0\) and \(\delta\in(0,1/4]\) depending only on \(\mu\),
such that \begin{equation}
c\log\left[(2\pi)^{-1}\int_{\mathbb R^2}
       |\chi_t(\sqrt c\,u)|^{1/c}\,du\right]
 \le C(c^{3/2}+\sqrt c\,|t|^2),
 \qquad 0<c\le\delta,\quad t\in\mathbb R^2.
 \label{l:eq:Z.5}
\end{equation} For \(U\in\mathbb C^{h\times k}\), \(U^*U=\mathrm{Id}_k\), write
\(\ell_j=\sum_{a=1}^k|U_{ja}|^2\), and let
\(q_U(y)=\int_{\ker U^*}\prod_j f_{\mathrm{iso}}((Uy)_j+w_j)\,dw\). If
\(\max_j\ell_j\le\delta\), then for every \(y\in\mathbb C^k\) and
\(\mathbf t=(t_1,\ldots,t_h)^T\in\operatorname{Ran}U\), \begin{equation}
\log q_U(y)\le-k\log(2\pi)-\langle\mathbf t,Uy\rangle_{\mathbb R}
       +\sum_{j=1}^h\Lambda(t_j)
       +C\sum_{j=1}^h(\ell_j^{3/2}+\sqrt{\ell_j}|t_j|^2).
 \label{l:eq:Z.6}
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-28 -->
<!-- proof-43: body_md -->
In Lemma~\ref{l:thm:FTILT}, replace the scalar tilted variance by
\(\Sigma(t)=\nabla^2\Lambda(t)=\mathrm{Id}_2+O(|t|)\).
The local Gaussian integral is \((\det\Sigma(t))^{-1/2}\), and the
Fourier-tail change of variables contributes \(c^{-1}\).
The same cosine expansion, spectral-gap argument and Plancherel bound
therefore give
\[
 (2\pi)^{-1}\int_{\mathbb R^2}|\chi_t(\sqrt c\,u)|^{1/c}\,du
 \le(\det\Sigma(t))^{-1/2}+Cc\le e^{C(c+|t|)}
\]
for small \(t\), and \(Cc^{-1}e^{C|t|^2}\) for large \(t\).
Here absolute continuity excludes \(|\chi_0(u)|=1\) at \(u\ne0\),
which would confine the law to countably many parallel lines.
The final estimates in the proof of Lemma~\ref{l:thm:FTILT} give
\eqref{l:eq:Z.5}.

For the projection bound, the maps
\(B_jy=(Uy)_j/\sqrt{\ell_j}\), for \(\ell_j>0\), have real
two-dimensional targets and satisfy
\(\sum_{j:\ell_j>0}\ell_j B_j^*B_j=\mathrm{Id}_k\).
Geometric Brascamp--Lieb and \eqref{l:eq:Z.5} give
\[
 \int_{\mathbb C^k}\prod_{j=1}^h|\chi_{t_j}((Uz)_j)|\,dz
 \le(2\pi)^k
 e^{C\sum_{j=1}^h(\ell_j^{3/2}+\sqrt{\ell_j}|t_j|^2)}.
\]
The fiber-continuity argument in Lemma~\ref{l:thm:ENV} applies to these
blocks with the same exponents \((1-\ell_j)^{-1}\).
Fourier inversion in real dimension \(2k\) contributes
\((2\pi)^{-2k}\), leaving \((2\pi)^{-k}\).
We can undo the tilt just as in that lemma: its factor is constant on the fiber
because \(\mathbf t\in\operatorname{Ran}U\) and \(w\in\ker U^*\).
This proves \eqref{l:eq:Z.6} at every point.
<!-- end proof-43 -->
<!-- theorem-7: body_md -->
\label{l:thm:CAC}

Let \(X\in\mathbb C^{d\times h}\) have iid law \(\mu\), and let \(\mathsf C\) be
an independent auxiliary input taking values in a standard Borel space
\(\mathcal C\). A Borel rule makes
predictable queries \(\mathbf u_a\in\mathbb C^h\), observes \(\mathbf y_a=X\mathbf u_a\), and
satisfies \(U^*U=\mathrm{Id}_k\) on every history, where
\(U=[\mathbf u_1,\ldots,\mathbf u_k]\in\mathbb C^{h\times k}\) and
\(Y=[\mathbf y_1,\ldots,\mathbf y_k]\in\mathbb C^{d\times k}\). Define
\(P=\mathcal L(\mathsf C,Y)\),
\(G=\mathcal L(\mathsf C)\otimes\mathcal N_{\mathbb C}(0,1)^{\otimes dk}\),
\(\ell_j=\sum_{a=1}^k|U_{ja}|^2\), and
\(\Theta=\sum_{j=1}^h\ell_j^{3/2}\). There are positive law-dependent
constants \(C_{\mathrm{coarse}},C_{\mathrm{weight}},\delta_\mu\) and
\(\theta_{\mathrm{coarse}},\theta_\mu\in(0,1)\) such that, for
\(h\le d\) and every Borel observation event
\(F\subseteq\mathcal C\times\mathbb C^{d\times k}\), \begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
 P(F)&\le e^{C_{\mathrm{coarse}}dk}G(F)^{\theta_{\mathrm{coarse}}},\\
 P(F)&\le\left[\mathbb E_G\mathbf1_F
              e^{C_{\mathrm{weight}}d\Theta}\right]^{\theta_\mu}
       &&\text{if }F\subseteq\{\max_j\ell_j\le\delta_\mu\}.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{l:eq:Z.7}
\end{equation} The reference is realized by a standard circular complex Gaussian
source, and all constants are uniform over the query rule and auxiliary
law.
<!-- end theorem-7 -->
<!-- proof-44: body_md -->
Fix the auxiliary input \(c\). Set \(\mathbf Z=\sqrt2\,\overline Y\),
and evaluate the queries on the recovered history:
\(\widetilde U_c(\mathbf z)=U(c,\overline{\mathbf z}/\sqrt2)\).
For source rows written as columns,
\[
 \mathbf z^{(i)}=(\mathbf Z_{i,:})^T
 =U^*(\sqrt2\,\overline{X_{i,:}})^T.
\]
Here \(U=\widetilde U_c(\mathbf Z)\). Thus the responses are Hermitian projections of independent isotropic
blocks. This invertible transformation preserves likelihood ratios and
relative entropy; write \(\widetilde P_c,\widetilde G_0\) for the
transformed laws, and set \(L_c=d\widetilde P_c/d\widetilde G_0\).
The Gaussian reference \(\widetilde G_0\) has
\(2dk\) independent standard real Gaussian coordinates.
The unitary changes of variables in Lemma~\ref{l:thm:LIKE} preserve
Lebesgue measure and are determined by previous responses, so
\begin{equation}
L_c(\mathbf Z)=\prod_{i=1}^d
 \frac{q_{\widetilde U_c(\mathbf Z)}(\mathbf z^{(i)})}
 {\phi_{2k}(\mathbf z^{(i)})},
 \qquad\phi_{2k}(z)=(2\pi)^{-k}e^{-\|z\|^2/2}.
 \label{l:eq:Z.8}
\end{equation}
For Gaussian sources, \(q_U=\phi_{2k}\), giving the stated reference.

Choose \(\delta_\mu\le\min\{1/4,\delta\}\) from Lemma~\ref{l:thm:CENV}.
For the weighted bound, take a law \(M\) satisfying the entropy and
integrability hypotheses of Lemma~\ref{l:thm:ENT} on the flat-frame event.
Apply Lemma~\ref{l:thm:SEQCOUP} to the real observation coordinates,
ordered by complex columns, and write \(\Pi\) for the coupling law.
Its Gaussian innovations \(g_{ia}\) have
independent \(N(0,1)\) real and imaginary parts, and
\[
 \mathbf n_i=\sum_{a=1}^k\mathbf u_a g_{ia},\qquad
 \mathbf e_i=\sum_{a=1}^k\mathbf u_a(Z_{ia}-g_{ia}),\qquad
 U\mathbf z^{(i)}=\mathbf n_i+\mathbf e_i,
 \quad\mathbb E_\Pi\sum_{i=1}^d\|\mathbf e_i\|^2
 \le2H(M\mid\widetilde G_0).
\]
The query is known before either component of the current Gaussian
column is revealed. Multiplication by \(b+ic\) has real matrix
\(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}b&-c\\c&b\end{smallmatrix}\right)\), so
each component of \((\mathbf n_i)_j\) has predictable variance sum
\(\ell_j\). Lemma~\ref{l:thm:GAUSS3} therefore gives
\begin{equation}
\mathbb E_\Pi|(\mathbf n_i)_j|^3+
 \mathbb E_\Pi[\sqrt{\ell_j}|(\mathbf n_i)_j|^2]
 \le132\,\mathbb E_M\ell_j^{3/2}.
 \label{l:eq:Z.9}
\end{equation}
No independence of the original real and imaginary entry components is
used. Since \(\Lambda(t)-|t|^2/2\le C\min\{|t|^3,|t|^2\}\), the
tilt \(\mathbf t_i=\mathbf n_i+\lambda\mathbf e_i\) from the proof of
Lemma~\ref{l:thm:ENT} reduces the quadratic part to a term in the
coupling error, with all inner products taken over the reals. The error
coefficient is at most
\(1/2-\lambda+C\lambda^2\), as in \eqref{l:eq:A.10}.
Choose \(\lambda>0\) small; \eqref{l:eq:Z.6}, \eqref{l:eq:Z.9} and the
coupling cost give
\begin{equation}
\mathbb E_M\log L_c\le C_{\mathrm{ent}}d\mathbb E_M\Theta
                      +(1-\lambda)H(M\mid\widetilde G_0).
 \label{l:eq:Z.10}
\end{equation}
The entropy identity, Jensen inequality and likelihood truncation in the
weighted proof of Theorem~\ref{l:thm:AC} now give the second bound in
\eqref{l:eq:Z.7}.
For the coarse bound, \eqref{l:eq:Z.3} gives
\(L_c\le e^{Cdk+\|\mathbf Z\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2/2}\), and
\(\|\mathbf Z\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\le2\sum_{a=1}^k s_a(X)^2\).
The energy bound of Lemma~\ref{l:thm:CGEO} lets us finish as in the
coarse proof of Theorem~\ref{l:thm:AC}. We then average over \(c\) and
return to the original responses.
<!-- end proof-44 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{l:thm:CML}

For every \(0<\varepsilon\le1\), there are
\(\beta_{\mu,\varepsilon},\allowbreak c_{\mathrm{link}}(\mu,\varepsilon)>0\) such
that, for all sufficiently large \(d\), a deterministic integer
\(m\ge1\) satisfies \begin{equation}
2m<d,\qquad \beta_{\mu,\varepsilon}d\le2m,
 \qquad256m\le\varepsilon d,
 \qquad
 \mathbb P_{A,\mathbf s}\{W_{2m}\le(1-\varepsilon/4)^{m^2}\}
       \le e^{-c_{\mathrm{link}}d^2}.
 \label{l:eq:Z.11}
\end{equation} Here the complex core \(A\) and starter \(\mathbf s\) have
independent iid entries with law \(\mu\).
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- proof-45: body_md -->
Under a core with iid standard complex Gaussian entries,
the residual squares are independent and
\(R_j^2\sim\operatorname{Gamma}(d-j,1)\), with shape \(d-j\) and
rate one. For integers \(0\le s<d-j\),
\begin{equation}
\mathbb E(R_j^2)^{-s}=\prod_{\ell=1}^s(d-j-\ell)^{-1}.
 \label{l:eq:Z.12}
\end{equation}
The denominators are no smaller than in \eqref{l:eq:L.2}, so the same
moment calculation gives \eqref{l:eq:L.1}.
For mixed reference columns, with \(\phi(z)=\pi^{-1}e^{-|z|^2}\),
choose \(0<q\le\min\{1,t_0\}\). Write
\(L_{I_0}=\prod_{i=1}^d\prod_{j\in I_0}f(A_{ij})/\phi(A_{ij})\);
\(\mathbb E_{\mathrm G}\) denotes expectation over this standard complex
Gaussian core and the unchanged independent starter. Then
\[
 D_q=\int_{\mathbb C}f^{1+q}\phi^{-q}
 \le(\pi K)^q\mathbb E e^{q|\zeta|^2}<\infty,
 \qquad \mathbb E_{\mathrm G}L_{I_0}^{1+q}=D_q^{d|I_0|}.
\]
This is the likelihood moment used in Lemma~\ref{l:thm:LM}.

To use the stopping argument, we also need reconstruction and the
determinant-loss identities.
Reconstruction in Lemma~\ref{l:thm:RECONPROP} uses Hermitian
Gram--Schmidt; the determinant loss \eqref{l:eq:S.2} and range
monotonicity \eqref{l:eq:G.8} hold for Hermitian Grams.
The Krylov minors have the same Vandermonde witness, and a nonzero
complex polynomial has zero set of planar product measure zero.
Thus the reference-range inclusion \eqref{l:eq:P.2} still holds.
We can now follow the proofs of Propositions~\ref{l:thm:SS} and
\ref{l:thm:ML}, using Lemma~\ref{l:thm:CGEO} for the geometric
estimates and Theorem~\ref{l:thm:CAC} for comparison. The constants
may change, but the parameter choices and union bounds do not.
Setting \(k=2m\) gives \eqref{l:eq:Z.11}.
<!-- end proof-45 -->
<!-- lemma-29: body_md -->
\label{l:thm:CDN}

Let \(Z\in\mathbb C^m\) be a complex Gaussian vector with arbitrary
mean, Hermitian covariance \(\Gamma>0\), and zero centered
pseudocovariance: with \(Z_c=Z-\mathbb E Z\), these conditions mean
\(\Gamma=\mathbb E[Z_cZ_c^*]\) and \(\mathbb E[Z_cZ_c^T]=0\).
For every deterministic \(D\in\mathbb C^{m\times m}\),
\begin{equation}
\mathbb E|\det(\operatorname{diag}Z+D)|^{-1/2}
 \le C^m(\det\Gamma)^{-1/4}.
 \label{l:eq:Z.13}
\end{equation} In particular the same bound holds for
\(\mathbb E\prod_j|Z_j|^{-1/2}\). The constant is universal.
<!-- end lemma-29 -->
<!-- proof-46: body_md -->
A circular Gaussian scalar of variance \(v\), with any mean, has
density at most \((\pi v)^{-1}\). Hence
\(\mathbb P\{|Z+a|<t\}\le\min\{1,t^2/v\}\), and tail integration
gives \(\mathbb E|Z+a|^{-1/2}\le Cv^{-1/4}\).
Apply the Schur-complement induction of Lemma~\ref{l:thm:DN}(ii),
integrating one complex coordinate at a time, conditional on all the
others. Its conditional
law remains circular, with variance \(v\) equal to the covariance
Schur complement, and \(\det\Gamma=v\det\Gamma_{\mathrm{rem}}\).
The complementary determinant is nonzero almost surely, since its
highest-degree term is the product of its diagonal variables.
The scalar bound proves \eqref{l:eq:Z.13}; set \(D=0\) for the product
assertion.
<!-- end proof-46 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\label{l:thm:CBORDER}

Fix \(0\le r<R\le1\), \(a>0\), and an integer \(m\ge1\) with
\(2m<d=n-1\) and \((r/R)^m\le1/2\). For
every fixed complex core \(A\) and starter \(\mathbf s\) with
\(W_{2m}(A,\mathbf s)\ge a^{m^2}\), an independent border
\(\mathbf b=(\xi_{11},\mathbf x,\mathbf y)\sim\mu^{\otimes(2n-1)}\)
satisfies \begin{equation}
\mathbb P_{\mathbf b}\left\{\rho
    \begin{pmatrix}\xi_{11}&\mathbf y^T\\\mathbf x&A\end{pmatrix}\le r\sqrt n\right\}
 \le\exp\{C_{\mathrm{border}}(\mu)n+(m^2/4)\log(R/a)\}.
 \label{l:eq:Z.14}
\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- proof-47: body_md -->
We begin by replacing the border with a circular Gaussian one, as in
Subsection~\ref{l:sec:real_border}. For \(\kappa\ge t_0^{-1}\) and
\(\phi_\kappa(z)=(\pi\kappa)^{-1}e^{-|z|^2/\kappa}\), write
\(\mathbb P_\kappa=\mathcal N_{\mathbb C}(0,\kappa)^{\otimes(2n-1)}\)
for the law of the unnormalized Gaussian border, with the core fixed. Then
\[
 J_f=\int_{\mathbb C}f^2/\phi_\kappa
 \le\pi\kappa K\,\mathbb E e^{|\zeta|^2/\kappa}<\infty,
 \qquad \mathbb P_{\mathbf b}(E)
 \le J_f^{(2n-1)/2}\mathbb P_\kappa(E)^{1/2}.
\]
The core stays fixed in this comparison. Complete Arnoldi to a unitary
\(Q\); the normalized transformed matrix is
\[
 T=\begin{pmatrix}g_{\mathrm b}&\mathbf x_{\mathrm b}^T\\
                  \mathbf u_{\mathrm b}&B\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad B=(Q^*AQ)^T/\sqrt n.
\]
Its core is lower Hessenberg, with \(B_{j,j+1}=R_j/\sqrt n\), and
its Gaussian border entries remain independent
\(\mathcal N_{\mathbb C}(0,\kappa/n)\) by unitary invariance.
The ordinary transpose in this characteristic-polynomial identity is
retained; projections and covariances use adjoints.

The cofactor and evaluation identities in
Lemmas~\ref{l:lem:hessenberg-cofactor} and
\ref{l:lem:evaluation-determinant}(i) are algebraic over \(\mathbb C\).
The polynomial \(Q_B\) is now complex monic of degree
\(d_{\mathrm{tail}}=d-2m\). Its highest forward difference on the
same real grid is still \(d_{\mathrm{tail}}!\) times the grid spacing
to that power. The triangle inequality therefore gives the choice of
\(w\) in Lemma~\ref{l:lem:evaluation-determinant}(ii), with the same
determinant identity and lower bound \eqref{l:eq:D.8}--\eqref{l:eq:D.9}.
No conjugate pairing of polynomial values is needed.

We can now use the two conditional integrations from
Lemma~\ref{l:lem:border-negative-moment}. Integrate
\(\mathbf x_*=(\mathbf x_{\mathrm b})_{[m]}\) first, fixing the
other border coordinates. The evaluation vector has covariance
\((\kappa/n)HH^*\). Then integrate
\(\mathbf u_*=(\mathbf u_{\mathrm b})_{[m+1,2m]}\), fixing its
complement. In
\(H=\operatorname{diag}(E_{\mathrm{suf}}\mathbf u_*)E_{\mathrm{pre}}+H_0\),
the Gaussian diagonal has covariance
\((\kappa/n)E_{\mathrm{suf}}E_{\mathrm{suf}}^*\).
Lemma~\ref{l:thm:CDN} gives
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E_{\mathbf x_*}\prod_{h=1}^m|P_T(z_h)|^{-1/2}
 &\le C^m(n/\kappa)^{m/4}|\det H|^{-1/2},\\
 \mathbb E_{\mathbf u_*}|\det H|^{-1/2}
 &\le C^m(n/\kappa)^{m/4}
          |\det E_{\mathrm{pre}}\det E_{\mathrm{suf}}|^{-1/2}.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{l:eq:Z.15}
\end{equation}
Both bounds are uniform in the fixed means. Finiteness of the second
expectation gives the almost-sure invertibility needed in the first.
Tonelli combines the two integrals. The calculation in
\eqref{l:eq:D.13}--\eqref{l:eq:D.14} yields
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb P_\kappa\{\rho(T)\le r\}
 &\le C^{2m}\left[
   \frac{(n/\kappa)^m(w+r^m)^n}
        {m^mw^{m-1}W_{2m}|Q_B(w)|}\right]^{1/2}\\
 &\le e^{C_\kappa n+(m^2/2)\log(R/a)}.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{l:eq:Z.16}
\end{equation}
Returning to the original border halves the logarithmic term and adds
only a linear cost, proving \eqref{l:eq:Z.14}.
<!-- end proof-47 -->
<!-- proof-48: body_md -->
Fix \(r<1\), set \(\varepsilon=1-r\),
\(a=1-\varepsilon/4\), and \(R=(r+a)/2\). Proposition \ref{l:thm:CML} provides a
deterministic \(m\ge\beta_{\mu,\varepsilon}d/2\) and a bad-core
probability at most \(e^{-c_{\mathrm{link}}d^2}\). For large \(d\),
\((r/R)^m\le1/2\). On the complementary event, Proposition \ref{l:thm:CBORDER}
bounds the conditional border probability by \[
 \exp\{C_{\mathrm{border}}(\mu)n-\delta_rm^2\},
 \qquad\delta_r=\tfrac14\log(a/R)>0.
\] The independent starter is absent from the spectral event. Averaging
over the core and starter and adding the bad-core probability proves
\eqref{l:eq:M.2}, since \(d=n-1\) and \(m\) is proportional to \(d\).
<!-- end proof-48 -->
